## Note to Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

● Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.  
● Accredited Entities should ensure that annexes provided are consistent with the details provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected in all relevant documents.  
● The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 60. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service standard time.  
● The recommended font is Arial, size 11.  

## Under the GCF Information Disclosure Policy

Project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Accredited Entities are asked to fill out information on disclosure in section G.4.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 6

## Provide an executive summary of the project/programme including:
1. Climate change problem  
2. Proposed interventions  
3. Climate results/benefits  

Acumen is establishing a $250m inclusive, innovative and multi-country investment initiative to deploy impact-focused capital and targeted technical assistance to off-grid-solar companies. The goal of ‘Hardest-to-Reach’ is to develop the off-grid solar (OGS) sector in countries where it is currently nascent or far below the capacity required to address the energy access challenge. The initiative will focus on least developed countries (LDCs) in sub-Saharan Africa, particularly with electrification rates at-or-below the sub-Saharan African average and high rates of poverty (45% or above), where investors have not been willing to enter at sufficient scale to meet the energy access and climate resilience needs of underserved segments.

## Climate change problem

Across the 16 eligible Hardest-to-Reach (H2R) geographies, there are around 54 million African households that do not have access to modern electricity services. Even compared to their sub-Saharan African peers, these countries are being ‘left behind’ on energy access and are not on track to achieve Sustainable Development Goal 7 (SDG 7). On average, 78% of the population in these countries lives below the lower-middle-income poverty line, and these communities are both fast-growing sources of new energy demand and are residing in acutely climate vulnerable contexts – in contexts of worsening heat stress, more frequent droughts, more intense floods, and extreme weather events including tropical cyclones. Nearly half of the bottom-twenty countries listed in the ND-Gain Climate Vulnerability Index are Hardest-to-Reach countries, with H2R countries (for which there is available data) obtaining an average index score of 36.6, compared to a global average of 59.8.

![Maplecroft Climate Vulnerability Index (2018)](Fig 1)

4 World Bank PovCal (n.d)  
5 The ND-Gain Country Index summarizes a country's vulnerability to climate change and other global challenges in combination with its readiness to improve resilience. Higher index scores are given to less climate vulnerable countries.  
6 The Maplecroft Climate Change Vulnerability Index provides a single figure rating for climate change exposure, sensitivity and adaptive capacity, helping companies calculate risks from future climate change. It combines exposure to climate extremes and change with the current human sensitivity to those climate stressors and the capacity of the country to adapt to the impacts of climate change. Higher index scores are given to less climate vulnerable countries.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 7

In the face of escalating climate variability and change, the delivery of climate-resilient energy access infrastructure and services is now more critical and urgent. The most recent data from the IEA Africa Energy Outlook projects that, for every person in sub-Saharan Africa to have access to electricity by 2030, thus achieving SDG 7, 6% of the current total population or 90 million people, including a significant proportion from rural areas, would need to gain power access each year from 2022.

Without energy that is both clean and climate resilient, households in Africa’s LDCs will face the climate future with increasing uncertainty and risk to their livelihoods, health and wellbeing.

The interplay of several factors points to the need for an energy access solution that puts climate adaptation and resilience at the forefront.

## Inadequacy of existing infrastructure

The past decade of climate trends and events has displayed the fragility of existing network infrastructure against extreme weather shocks, with the significant damage and long periods without repair being frequent, leading to severe and underappreciated consequences. Along with power outages come disruptions to household routines, diminished public safety, and halted economic activity, while the regularity of these incidents mean that households and small businesses are forced to invest in inefficient coping measures, ranging from the purchase of water tanks and diesel generators, to keeping expensive back-up inventories.

## Vulnerability of people without access

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 8

## Households without any electricity access, meanwhile, are trapped in climate vulnerability, as they lack the social, technological, and financial resources that are enabled and multiplied by energy access, such as early warning and post-disaster public notifications. For these households, energy access will likely be the springboard for climate resilient sustainable development. For example, addressing the energy access challenge through OGS products can have a transformational impact on economic activity, which can boost economic resilience to climate shocks. Around a third of the solar home systems (SHS) in East Africa were purchased with the intent to extend the working day or boost enterprise. Usage of SHS in East Africa boosted monthly earnings by US$ 46 a month, around 14% increase on average income.

## Dependency on nature
Research from the Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) has pointed to the disproportionate dependence of the poorest and most marginalized on environmental resources to meet their basic needs, such as forests (for energy, materials and food), land (for farming) and water (for drinking, household use and farming). This is a reminder that the pathways of electrification that investors and developers deploy in low-income communities, should be the least nature-intensive, the most climate resilient, and the most supportive of community norms, even as the benefits of economic development unfold.

## Inequitable distribution of climate burden
On these considerations alone, entry-level distributed residential solar compares favorably to other alternatives, separate from its mitigation and energy security benefits. And though the continent has contributed to less than 4% of global emissions, the burden of climate change on least developed countries and livelihoods across the continent is disproportionately high. Such climate injustice already threatens to undermine development gains and inflict additional economic costs and social disruption. Adaptation alone is projected to cost the continent at least $500 billion annually by 2050.

## The African Development Bank’s African Economic Outlook summarizes several dynamics at play in the continent’s potential low-carbon transition out of energy poverty:
- Low-carbon transitions in Africa will vary by country, but they could be transformational. These variations reflect differing ecological zones, climates, settlement patterns, economic structures, resource bases, and governance systems;
- Africa is generously endowed with renewable energy resources, such solar irradiation, which present significant opportunities for the green transition;
- With Africa’s limited lock-in to fossil-based energy technologies, renewable energy technology opportunities could help the continent build a climate-resilient and integrated sustainable energy sector;

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 9

## Interventions for Climate Resilience and Just Energy Transition

● Interventions to achieve climate resilience and a just energy transition in the continent should be inclusive, "leaving no one behind."

Africa needs a decade of highly adaptive and resilience-promoting infrastructure investment to deliver on the promise of sustainable development by 2030 and in this century. Yet, across the African continent, only a small number of countries have seen strong growth in electrification in the past decade, in particular Kenya, Senegal, and Ghana, as well as Ethiopia and Rwanda where there is still significant scope for development. A substantial scale-up of investment is needed throughout sub-Saharan Africa, as it totals two-thirds of the required additional global investment in electricity access.

In terms of the emissions mitigation pathway, the lesson from across the globe, historically, is that in a business-as-usual environment, electricity and heat generation have been the greatest contributor to anthropogenic drivers of greenhouse gas emissions, sometimes twice as large as the second most-contributing economic sector. Still, today, power generation remains the largest GHG-emitting sector in developed economies across Europe14 and the second-largest in the US,15 suggesting that the carbon-intensity of power generation and economic development trajectories is highly material for mitigation outcomes, and showing that the established pathways of electrification are inconsistent with net zero objectives and climate targets of the United Nations SDGs and the Paris Accord.

A similar fossil-fuelled trajectory in sub-Saharan Africa would be catastrophic for our climate; if African emissions per person were to rise to only the current global average, the continent would emit 12.5 billion tonnes of greenhouse gasses per year by 2050 — roughly equivalent to all emissions from China and India today.1617

## Proposed Intervention

Acumen's Hardest-to-Reach energy access initiative seeks to mobilize a blend of institutional and donor finance to deliver much-needed investment into the region, to contribute to closing the energy access gap in sub-Saharan Africa towards achieving SDG 7 by providing more than 60 million people with clean, climate-resilient, off-grid solar energy products.

Hardest-to-Reach (H2R) will establish two separate financing facilities with different investment risk profiles, and invest with different forms of capital to match the needs of off-grid solar companies and markets. This is designed to help off-grid solar companies to expand in H2R countries and extend their reach to underserved segments, thus achieving sustainable and inclusive scale with cross-cutting climate impacts.

The first facility, the $50M grant-funded Market Support Facility (MSF), intends to deploy debt, equity, and grants using a patient capital approach that is consistent with Acumen's core investment experience. The principal work of the MSF is to prove an approach in a new market with a range of capital investment options and technical assistance accompaniment.

A second vehicle, the $200M blended finance Market Expansion Facility (MEF), intends to extend impact-linked debt to OGS players, with the objective of responsibly expanding the reach and impact of OGS technologies, while achieving high levels of inclusion and sustainability.

To this end, H2R seeks to support companies with technical assistance and invest in ecosystem development to ensure the sustainability of the intervention and its impacts beyond the life of the fund.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 10

The H2R initiative is designed to overcome a number of market barriers that currently hinder more widespread climate-resilient energy access in sub-Saharan Africa. These are outlined in the table below.

| Market barrier | H2R response |
|----------------|--------------|
| Markets in H2R areas are too risky or nascent for many investors. | Perceived risk may differ from the real risk. At the portfolio level, H2R will leverage deeper market research to best assess the risk of each market. At the fund level, the H2R initiative’s blended finance structure mitigates risk by crowding in investors with different risk appetites. The $50M Market Support Facility aims to use philanthropically-backed patient capital to support market entry for OGS companies into H2R markets. The first loss tranche for the Market Expansion Facility can unlock more commercial capital from mainstream investors and build the case for subsequent investment flows, allowing companies to access greater amounts of funding for operations in H2R areas. |
| OGS companies experience capacity barriers to expand into H2R areas. | The targeted use of set-up grants, equity and debt from the Market Support Facility aims to address management, marketing, distribution and risk assessment barriers based on the country's difficulty and company’s needs. The TA will seek to improve capacity and ensure responsible, sustainable growth (including through high standards of consumer protection). |
| There is a lack of flexible funding for OGS companies looking to achieve scale. Those that do receive funding aren’t incentivized to serve the poorest and expand into H2R areas. | Acumen intends to raise a $200M vehicle, specifically designed to invest via impact-linked finance in OGS companies. Acumen seeks to link the interest rate to impact-related KPIs to incentivize companies to reach low-income, first-time energy access customers, thereby avoiding mission drift. The grant capital as part of this vehicle is intended to permit interest rates adjustments to further incentivize first-time energy access. |
| People in H2R areas can’t afford OGS products upfront, are unaware of their benefits, or live in areas with limited to no OGS company coverage. The majority of people do not have access to credit, which would | Acumen incentivizes and facilitates OGS companies to set up, market and sell solar energy products via PAYGo financing. The PAYGo model means that solar energy products are affordable for people in H2R areas, who can spread the cost of the solar product over a few months or years. Acumen, through Hardest-to-Reach, is also forming partnerships with the World Bank Group and donor countries to address the affordability gap through demand side subsidies to expand our reach to the lowest income level customers. H2R aims to accompany OGS companies to implement strong consumer protection practices, aligned with GOGLA Consumer |

## Climate results/benefits

By the end of the program, investments developed, financed, and accompanied by H2R will be able to provide:

- ~72.4M people with year-round access to clean, affordable, reliable and modern off-grid solar energy.
- ~120 MW of newly-installed, decentralized solar energy generating capacity across H2R geographies.
- ~54.3M people with first time energy access and improved climate resilience through decentralized solar power delivery.
- ~5.4M people with improved climate resilience through diversified income sources enabled by first-time access to a solar powered product.
- 5.1M tCO2e in emissions reduced or avoided (excl. black carbon mitigation impact).
- In addition, some 13,922 clean energy jobs, with 4,641 as sources of employment for women.

18 GOGLA Consumer Protection Code consists of a set of principles and an assessment framework to enable companies to measure, demonstrate and improve their practices, and provide investors and other stakeholders with a framework to promote good consumer protection (CP) practice. # B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1. Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### Climate change problem

The climate change problem the proposal is expected to address involves both mitigation needs (GHG emissions profile) and adaptation needs (climate hazards and associated risks based on impacts, exposure, and vulnerabilities). The proposed interventions are expected to address these needs. In the absence of the proposed interventions, the prevailing conditions or other alternative scenarios would continue to remain.

The methodology used to derive such information, including mitigation and adaptation needs, should be included in the feasibility study.

### Context

In describing the mitigation and/or adaptation needs, the target region/area of the proposed interventions should be described, including information on demographics, economy, topography, etc.

### Related projects/interventions

Recent or ongoing projects/interventions related to the proposal from other domestic or international sources of funding (such as the Global Environment Facility, Adaptation Fund, Climate Investment Funds, etc.) should be described. These projects/interventions will be complemented by this project/programme (e.g., scaling up, replication, etc.). Current gaps and barriers regarding recent or ongoing projects should be identified, and how this project/programme complements or addresses these should be elaborated further.

Africa is the most vulnerable continent to climate change impacts under all climate scenarios above 1.5 degrees Celsius. Exponential collateral damage in the face of climate change poses systemic risks to the continent’s economies, infrastructure investments, water and food systems, public health, agriculture, and livelihoods, threatening to undo its modest development gains and slip into higher levels of extreme poverty.

There are substantial disparities across Africa, with many countries in sub-Saharan Africa being the most vulnerable and exposed to the impacts of climate change. Many of these form the group of Hardest-to-Reach target countries. All are highly vulnerable, and least ready, under the NDI-GAIN indexation, with ranks reaching from 1st – 56th most vulnerable to, and 127th to 176th most ready to cope with, adaptation challenges.

### Constraints to supply and demand of electricity

Constraints to the supply and demand of electricity drive the energy access problem. Two drivers - household poverty, which limits a household’s ability to pay for electricity, and the lack of physical access to electricity, due to costly, weak or non-existent coverage of grid electricity outside urban areas - are particularly harmful to climate vulnerable populations.

Accordingly, the baseline context can be presented through two key indicators: poverty rate and electrification rates. At least half of all sub-Saharan Africans have no access to electricity, and two-thirds of the global extreme poor population reside in this region, with more than three quarters living below the $3.65/day poverty line across H2R program countries on average.

Acumen’s Hardest-to-Reach energy access initiative is seeking to contribute towards closing the energy access gap in sub-Saharan Africa’s least developed countries (LDCs).

This vulnerability has recently been confirmed by a large-scale climate survey of thousands of people across six sub-Saharan African countries. The survey found that 88% of people feel that climate change is already having an impact on their everyday lives, that 63% of people consider climate change and environmental degradation to affect their income and source of livelihood, and 57% of people have already incurred losses due to climate change.

These findings demonstrate that climate change is no longer only a future threat, but a reality that many vulnerable people in Africa are living with already.

## Climate Adaptation Context

The IPCC sixth assessment report shows that most regions in Africa have experienced a gradual increase in temperatures in the past 40 years which has led to an increase in the frequency of annual heatwaves.

Rainfall trends in the continent have higher degrees of uncertainty and only a few regions show statistically significant historical rainfall trends. Projections indicate that both mean temperatures and temperature extremes will increase over most of the continent, while mean annual rainfall is likely to increase over the eastern Sahel, and eastern and central Africa.

The frequency and severity of heavy rainfall events are projected to increase throughout Africa, except in the northern and southwestern regions.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 18

## Climate Change Impacts and Projections

### Observed Changes
- The frequency of dry spell and agricultural drought in the region has increased since 1961.
- The frequency of meteorological drought increased by between 2.5-3 events per decade.
- The number and intensity of extreme precipitation events have increased over the last century.

### Mid-century Projections
- Mean annual rainfall in the summer rainfall region is projected to decrease by 10-20%.
- The number of consecutive dry days during the rainy season are projected to increase under RCP8.5.
- The frequency and duration of droughts is projected to increase over large parts of southern Africa at 1.5°C and extreme droughts at 2°C GWL.

## Climate-related Events in Sub-Saharan Africa

The number of observed climate-related hazard events in sub-Saharan Africa has also increased in recent years (Fig 2) and at a faster pace than the rest of the world.

Given the high poverty levels and dependence of most rural households on agriculture, climate events exacerbate the vulnerability of the region.

As seen in Fig 3, climate-related epidemics, floods and storms have been the most common climate-related disasters faced by the continent's fragile states.

An increase in the frequency and intensity of natural disasters has also been observed, which has been accompanied by an increase in the number of people being affected by these events.

- Six times more people were affected by droughts in 2010-2019 relative to 1970-1979.
- Flooding impacted eight times more people over the same period.

Perhaps unsurprisingly, the Hardest-to-Reach countries rank as the top 15% most vulnerable countries in the world on the ND-Gain index.

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 20

Regional vulnerability to the changing climate and increasing climate hazards is exacerbated by non-climate drivers such as high poverty levels, weak infrastructure systems, a lack of emergency response capabilities, and fragile grid electricity and telecommunications networks. In particular, a lack of access to modern energy sources heightens Africa's climate vulnerability. The region's energy systems have been compromised both by extreme and slow-onset climate events, with resulting economic losses, disruptions of services and impacts to wellbeing.

Accordingly, the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change's (IPCC) sixth assessment report (AR6) considers decentralized energy generation to be an important component of the systems transition of energy, where adaptation, mitigation and development solutions are pursued together to exploit synergies and reduce trade-offs within the sector.

Within energy system transitions, the most feasible adaptation options support infrastructure resilience, reliable power systems and efficient water use: "Energy generation diversification, including with renewable energy resources and generation that can be decentralized depending on context (e.g., wind, solar, small scale hydroelectric)...can reduce vulnerabilities to climate change, especially in rural populations (high confidence)."

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 21 

It is now well understood that extreme weather events induced by climate change are a major source of vulnerability. While these impacts are well tracked in developing countries, extreme weather events and their impacts tend to be underreported when they happen in Africa, meaning that we have a much lower level of understanding of their effects on peoples' lives. This is closely linked to a lack of effective hydrometeorological systems in particular in the North and Central African regions. Furthermore, even where events are registered internationally, they often are those affecting the more population-dense, and better connected urban locations, while similar events in rural regions simply go unnoticed.

With this being the case, it is likely that the vulnerability profile of African countries understates the levels of exposure of the rural poor, who are least able to implement measures to protect themselves from this. EM-DAT provides a detailed look at extreme weather events impacting Africa in 2022 (see Figure 4 below).

collateral damage and physical risks to infrastructure investments, water and food systems, public health and agriculture.

The economies and livelihoods of most sub-Saharan African nations are driven by rain-fed agriculture and pastoralism. FAO reported that agriculture alone provided jobs for almost 50% of the total labor force of these countries.

At the same time, rural poverty is widespread in the target countries of H2R, and considerably exceeds the rate of urban poverty (see Figure 5 below). As mentioned, all H2R countries are LDCs with a high incidence of both poverty and climate vulnerability among the millions of smallholder farmers and the communities that dwell in rural, often informal, settlements with low access to the basic services sometimes available in cities.

In addition, as the Global Center for Adaptation (GCA) 'State and Trends in Adaptation Report 2021' notes, large swathes of Africa, and especially the drylands areas that cover 60 percent of the continent, are warming at a rate twice the global average, putting half a billion people at risk.

Rural poverty is also exacerbated by the lack of access to basic services provided by electricity. This explains why spatial and temporal variations in rainfall and recurrent drought episodes have severe impacts on the livelihood of people living throughout the continent.

# [Page 1]

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 23

Up until now the continent has seen adaptation interventions that are primarily reactive rather than proactive, such as efforts focused on disaster relief or reconstruction and recovery following severe floods or a pandemic. The Africa-Led Consultative Dialogue Report on adaptation highlights the complementary need for a hazard and risk-oriented approach to adaptation, which considers it holistically across factors such as vulnerability, exposure, and adaptive capacity. The risk approach to adaptation can potentially "enable preparedness to not just address the climate risks but also the various shocks such as natural disasters…that are either exacerbated by climate change or exacerbate climatic impacts."⁴⁴

The GAC 2021 report points to new research which considers interlinkages between climate, conflict and energy.⁴⁵ In the face of increasing climate stressors, there is the risk that certain energy systems are potentially maladaptive and could play a contributory role in increasing the risk of conflict. The disruption of energy flows at the local, national, regional and global scale can increase the risk of conflict, and energy sources can also become the target of sabotage or attacks, particularly by non-state armed groups. Additionally, there is a close link between energy and food security. Restricted energy access and increased energy prices can have significant impacts on overall food security, contributing to drivers of conflict. Though the research in this area is nascent, it points to the need to develop energy systems that are the most resilient and least vulnerable to the physical and human dimensions of climate risk. By many of these adaptation-based criteria, the case for decentralized solar power, particularly residential solar, as a prominent part of the electricity mix, has become a compelling one.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 24

The ultimate measure of the effects of energy access on climate resilience will be the extent to which off-grid residential solar electrification can be associated with reducing sensitivity and exposure, and increasing adaptive capacity during adverse effects of shocks and stresses related to climate (see Figure 2). Some examples of this impact pathway can be seen in Table 3 below.

Energy access which, for example, supports early warning systems can prevent human and economic losses in case of extreme events, while extending rural electrification can enable adaptation and mitigate climate vulnerability. If based on a diversified and decentralized network of energy sources, it can reduce the vulnerability of energy supply.

As a review of NAPs and NDCs highlights, low levels of energy access are considered to reduce resilience, while measures to increase resilience and adaptability are often leading to higher energy needs, risking increased emissions. It is therefore important for energy access measures to capitalize on the opportunities of energy transition.

For example, modern energy access in drylands reduces reliance on biomass fuels, leading to less wood extraction and charcoal use - which currently exceeds tree production, and increases resilience against the climactic trend of soil depletion and tree loss (roughly three million hectares of forest are lost annually).

At the same time, lack of reliable access affects the ability of people to access important health, or early warning information in the context of disaster preparedness - both of these are areas highlighted for improvements in NDCs and NAPs, but the impact of this improvement will be limited without basic access.

Per capita energy consumption is also strongly correlated to economic output. At higher levels of energy consumption and through productive uses of energy, we observe how clean energy can power adaptation through value-added agro-systems that unlock socioeconomic opportunities.

Adaptation impacts are also unlocked when we understand and capture the potential synergistic links among actions and technologies, for example household entry-level energy access alongside mobile telecommunications, TV or radio to enable early hazard warning.

Socio-economic development is one of the foremost strategies to reduce community vulnerability, and access to clean energy is a cornerstone of sustainable socio-economic development. Energy access as a whole is thus linked to community climate resilience and adaptation, and OGS have an important role to play in providing the stepping stone towards wider access.

Therefore, delivering enhanced, even basic energy access has the potential to unlock a wide range of adaptation-linked socio-economic development outcomes that drive resilience of the population.

At a practical level, lighting, cooking, heating, cooling, communication and information access services are derived from energy. A community’s basic needs, economic activities and institutions (schools, health centers, government buildings) are substantially enhanced through access to these energy services.

For these reasons, long-term access to reliable energy builds self-resilience and adaptive capacity within

## Specific benefits of enhanced access include (see also Table 3 for examples):

- Enhancing energy, food and water security by improving self-reliance in the face of increasing climate-induced risks;
- Providing easier and lower-cost access to information and digital services which may enhance disaster planning and response to climate-driven events such as EWE;

- Diversifying jobs, particularly for women who can expand their opportunities as a result of time-saving achieved through greater household productivity, thus increasing their resilience to slow-onset climate events; and

## Emissions Context

Sub-Saharan Africa currently accounts for less than 4% of cumulative historical global greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. However, the region’s economies and populations are growing rapidly and emissions have been rising steadily. Between 1950 to 2016, Africa’s CO2 emissions increased by a factor of 14.

Today, Africa is home to 1.3 billion people; this number is projected to grow to 3 billion by 2060. More than half of today’s population currently has no access to electricity. For a significant share of peri-urban and rural households, access to electricity services is made possible through local kiosks which provide cell phone charging, refrigeration and other electricity-driven services. In off-grid areas, these kiosks and other local infrastructure such as clinics are primarily supplied by diesel generators or hybrid mini-grids and people’s periodic access to these service points constrains their electricity usage. As a result, off-grid communities are experiencing suppressed energy demand, and current household energy consumption does not reflect the real demand for energy services.

Due to the low base of current energy use on the continent, and particularly in H2R program countries, and the rapidly growing and youthful populations, the continent’s future energy trajectory has real implications for global net zero targets and the safety of our planet. In one scenario, an African transition out of energy poverty that relies on fossil fuels could result in increased CO2 emissions that would offset a 20 percent cut in the emissions of China, the United States, India, Russia, Japan, and Germany combined.

Currently, the majority of rural households rely on emission-intensive kerosene and biomass as their primary energy source for lighting, cooking, heating etc. Across the region, the percentage of total wood harvested used as fuelwood varies from 90% in Africa, 62% in Asia, 50% in South America to less than 20% in Europe, North America and Oceania.

Under current projections, electrification can play a role in alleviating the pressure on native forests, while also reducing the share of agriculture, forestry and land use emissions.

## B.2 (a). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

Present the theory of change (ToC) that contains a goal statement and describes how the proposed project/programme will contribute towards the goal statement by using results chain links from activities, outputs, to outcomes. By referring to the sample ToC diagram template available in the guidance note, present a ToC diagram (approximately 1 page) which visually represents the same logic in the narrative description. The ToC diagram and narrative may include a wide range of co-benefits as applicable in the context of the project/programme.

Note all co-benefits will need to be further elaborated in section D.3 (sustainable development potential) and correspondent co-benefit indicators should be provided under section E.5 (project/programme specific indicators).

The theory of change should also include any relevant barriers (social, gender, fiscal, regulatory, technological, financial, ecological, institutional, etc.) that need to be addressed as well as risks and assumptions. Note that the assumptions can be elaborated further in sections E.3 (GCF outcome level: reduced emissions and increased resilience) and E.5 (project/programme specific indicators) for each relevant indicator, as appropriate.

## Theory of Change Narrative

Currently, sub-Saharan Africa's AFOLU activities account for 60% of CH4 emissions. To mitigate and adapt to an increasingly warming planet, it is critical that all countries transition to renewable energy, even where energy consumption is currently low, embark on clean energy pathways. The energy pathways and resource dependencies developed across the continent today will have profound climate impacts in the medium and long term.

Data shows that developing economies (excluding China) now account for roughly 45% of carbon emissions and will propel almost all the growth going forward. Providing first-time electricity access via a low carbon trajectory avoids approximately 268 million tonnes of CO2eq emissions, with a technology mix that is 45% decentralized renewables.

54 countries in Africa signed the Paris Agreement and submitted ambitious Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs). Most African NDCs incorporated two targets: an unconditional target that was designed to be met by African countries with their own resources (about 15%), and a conditional target, subject to financial support from the international community ( about 85%). Each country's specific NDC adaptation and emission targets as well as climate policies have been assessed and documented in the accompanying country reports.

Acumen's objectives through Hardest-to-Reach are directly aligned to National climate priorities, including National Adaptation Plans, National Climate Change Action Plans, and NDC's (see Section D.5).

## Complementarity & Coherence

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 35

In 2020, 733 million people were still living without access to (affordable, clean) electricity, of which 298 million people were in nascent off-grid solar (OGS) markets where there is little commercial OGS activity.86  
Approximately 70% of this number have the ability to pay but live in areas deemed too remote or too risky to be served by traditional grid extensions and business as usual approaches in these markets. However, if the appropriate investment capital and technical assistance is provided to OGS companies, then they will likely be able to deliver clean energy access and adaptive capacity to low-income, underserved people and build sustainable markets. This will happen because they will be adequately incentivized and capacitated, through an enabling environment, to operate and grow in currently untested, ‘Hardest-to-Reach’ (H2R) areas.

There are however multiple barriers that prevent access to alternative forms of energy access, such as OGS. There is a funding gap, as the OGS market for the most deprived customers is considered highly risky, with the potential customers often the poorest populations in a country, and there are few track records due to market fragmentation. OGS companies themselves struggle to grow, or even survive, due to a lack of internal capacity and inability to access finance. Markets are often either unregulated, or the regulation is not appropriate for the types of products on offer. Taken together, these barriers lead to a lack of access to capital, in turn leading to OGS companies being underfunded, and to markets stagnating, rather than growing rapidly in response to the clear needs of the rural populations where energy access is lowest.

The Hardest-to-Reach energy access initiative aims to deliver energy access to 72.4M low-income customers in least developed and climate-vulnerable countries in sub-Saharan Africa, 54.3M of whom will be accessing energy for the first time. It will do so by engaging with stakeholders, including financing partners, governments and donors, to establish two new funds, H2R MSF and H2R MEF, which will provide early-stage and scaled-up funding to OGS companies. The engagement will also seek to lower barriers where these relate to e.g. regulation or a lack of shared knowledge. H2R MSF/MEF will then provide impact-linked capital and technical assistance to incentivize off-grid solar companies (OGS) to expand into new, under-reached markets, to build sustainable business platforms and to scale up, and for new OGS to be established. Investee OGS companies can then provide more off-grid solar energy products such as solar home systems to low-income customers via Pay-as-you-go mechanisms, providing many people with first-time access to modern energy.

H2R will be focused on learning how our interventions are able to drive impact at the household, enterprise and market level through a range of different measurement techniques, including feedback from customers project beneficiaries through lean data surveys. Furthermore, we will aim to disseminate the learnings to all stakeholders. The sharing of knowledge will be supported through our partnerships with industry, government, civil society and private sector, creating the long-lasting impact of a demonstration effect and the blueprint for other investors to follow.

Hardest-to-Reach focuses on creating impact at the sector, enterprise and household levels in fledgling markets, and thus will seek to deliver a number of outputs by scaling the OGS markets in the participating countries:

## Blended Finance to Crowd in Climate Finance

blended finance to crowd in climate finance is constrained in part because the industry does not yet have a clearly defined narrative on how OGS products and services contribute to adaptation and resilience. In addition, the scale of many proposed initiatives in this space has been too small to attract commercial investors.

## Activities and Outputs

Acumen will establish the H2R blended finance structure to crowd in investors with different risk appetites. Acumen also will develop partnerships that convene different financiers, TA providers, policy advocates, government, and OGS company investees in an effort to jumpstart the OGS market in H2R areas, support the creation of an enabling environment for other investors and businesses and share knowledge within the OGS sector. Specifically:

- Activities 1.1 and 2.1 comprise of fundraising and the establishment of the two funds that facilitates the investments, discussions and interest in the H2R regions.
- Activity 1.2 intends to drive partnership development designed to crowd in new investors, improve the level of knowledge sharing in the sector, coordinate the actions of various stakeholders, and make the initiative's impact sustainable.

Furthermore, the H2R initiative encourages more investment by improving the risk-return profile and track record of these markets. It does so by providing new market entrants with concessional finance and targeted technical assistance, and supporting the development of local networks and capacity. It intends to also increase the capacity of local distributors to become reliable partners to suppliers expanding in the region, and spur inclusive market development with a range of actors.

## Outcome

H2R markets are developed and scaled with greater engagement towards an improved enabling environment and market conditions. Markets are shown to be viable, with the right approach, providing incentives and conditions to sustainably attract OGS companies, local participants and investors (philanthropic, development and private capital). The building blocks for OGS market advancement - such as the creation of market intelligence studies, capacity building for off-grid solar manufacturers and local distributors, consumer awareness efforts, policy engagement, access to finance and risk mitigation, and consumer subsidies, where needed - are developed in H2R countries.

increasing awareness of OGS products. In addition, the Market Expansion Facility provides companies  
with $200 million of impact-indexed loans, providing targeted concessional capital. This impact-linked  
capital intends to also incentivize companies to retain a focus on entry-level solar products for lower-  
income customers and prevent mission-drift.

consumer protection, business development, gender and ESG. Per activity 1.3, 2.3 and 1.5, H2R

Household level: Delivering 13.3M sales of off-grid solar projects and increasing the resilience and adaptive capacity of 54.3M people in Hardest-to- Reach areas and avoidance of 5.1M tCO2.

## Challenge

In H2R areas, many people are climate-vulnerable because they are located in areas that are highly exposed to the impacts of climate change and their sources of income are tied to activities which are weather dependent, such as agriculture, while access to basic energy services is both costly and time-consuming, impacting on household incomes. Their incomes can therefore be intermittent and they typically lack access to credit on reasonable terms.

Furthermore, these people are often living in unelectrified areas with no grid connection. As a result of their low asset bases, inconsistent incomes and lack of supporting infrastructure, people in H2R areas may be unable to adapt to the effects of climate change. The result is that many people living in H2R areas have to rely on fossil fuels to power their day-to-day activities, such as light, charging and cooking. Fossil fuels such as kerosene and diesel are harmful to those using them, harmful to the environment through the carbon emitted, and are notoriously expensive and unreliable. They are also made more vulnerable by not having assured access to communication tools to keep abreast of weather, health and market information.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 41

Finally, women and children are likely to be disproportionately affected as they often spend more time at home with the harmful and emissions-intensive fossil fuel source which can lead to respiratory illnesses, or with candles which can lead to fires and burns.

## Activities and Outputs

All fund activities contribute to seeing impact at the household level. Critically, products and services supplied through H2R investments intend to support household resilience/adaptation. (See B.1). Acumen incentivizes and facilitates OGS companies to set-up, and market and sell solar energy products via PAYGo financing to low-income people in H2R areas. This means that unelectrified people are able to access clean energy sources, many for the first time, and are able to afford them thanks to longer-term payment plans. Furthermore, after a payment period of 12-36 months, households should own their SHS outright for the remaining lifetime of 24-48 months. Companies are expected to monitor impact by collecting gender-disaggregated sales data as well as representative sampling on product accessibility, use and benefits.

## Outcomes

Clean energy pathways are created for 72.4M people in H2R areas. This leads to increased resilience and adaptive capacity of the 54.3M beneficiaries accessing energy for the first time, including through improved energy security, income diversification and access to climate information. Data shows that a portion of customers will be able to diversify their sources of income, making their livelihoods more resilient to climate change. Beneficiaries will also be able to readily access climate-related information through the use of ICTs such as mobile phones and radios, allowing them to prepare for upcoming weather events. Furthermore, some people should benefit from a safer home and better health due to reduced use of lighting sources that can cause fires and burns or are harmful to respiratory health (co-benefit).

OGS use can also lead to reduced vulnerability in the context of climate change via a less climate-vulnerable energy supply. OGS is less vulnerable to weather events than grid-connected energy sources because they are decentralized. As the owners of a high quality OGS product, in the event of displacement due to a climate-related natural disaster, a person and their household are more able to retain access to electricity (with the benefit of it being clean and safe). Furthermore, OGS should take less time to repair or replace, meaning that people with SHS are able to access energy more quickly following a weather event.

Finally, displacement of emissions-intensive lighting sources is intended to lead to 5.1M tCO2 being avoided.

## Introduction to Hardest-to-Reach

Hardest-to-Reach (H2R) is an energy access initiative dedicated to reaching more than 70M of the 286M people who live in H2R countries without electricity. Using benchmarks from the GOGLA Market Trends Report 2022, approximately 190M people in H2R countries could afford an entry level off-grid solar connection, but are at risk of being left behind by business-as-usual market approaches to electrification.

88 GOGLA Market Trends Report: State of the Sector (2022)

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 45

## H2R aims to unlock first-time clean and resilient energy access for these communities.

These are challenging markets with electrification rates at-or-below the sub-Saharan Africa average (48%) where investors have not been willing to enter to serve low-income segments. Our approach is to incentivize and accompany companies to enter into and grow in these challenging geographies to serve underserved segments by mobilizing capital at scale from various investor bases in off-grid solar. This approach is based on Acumen’s deep knowledge of the energy access challenge, relationships with players in the off-grid solar sector with an ability to scale, and the experience of deploying innovative, patient capital to achieve impact.

## H2R will target 16 sub-Saharan African countries with electrification rates of 48% or below and high poverty rates (above 45%).

All eligible countries are considered least developed countries and they hold around 54 million African households that do not have access to modern electricity services. On average, 78% of the population living in these geographies lives below the lower-middle-income poverty line.

## All 16 H2R countries are eligible countries for Acumen’s H2R program.

As part of H2R preparation, in-depth country studies have been conducted across a number of H2R countries. In the first window of the Market Support Facility (MSF), explained in more detail later, country level research has identified barriers and guided the development of investment approaches and ensured policy alignment. Ongoing research and engagement in these countries will help to ensure that H2R achieves complementarity and coherence with other interventions and investments that donors, DFIs and private investors are doing.

## At Acumen our approach is always to use the right kind of capital to tackle the right kind of problem.

Hardest-to-Reach expects to use a diversified approach to inject capital and technical assistance to match the needs of off-grid energy companies at each stage of development and capacity. To build an off-grid solar market in these challenging H2R geographies, “start-up to scale-up” or “whole-of-life cycle” financing is needed to support entrepreneurs to set up in these markets and then scale up their operations, and thereby achieve their impact. This is why Acumen is structuring two independent financing mechanisms, tailored to the different stages of operators’ evolution and the different risk appetites of investors.

## Components, outcomes, and underlying activities within Hardest-to-Reach.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 46

## Component 1: Sustainable Market Creation in H2R countries (Market Support Facility)

A philanthropically-backed Market Support Facility aims to support the development of local players and regional companies’ expansion into these new challenging markets. This $50 million facility is expected to support companies with the flexibility to set up operations through grants, equity and debt, and the technical assistance to enter and navigate unfamiliar territory of high risk countries.

The MSF is specifically designed to provide the right type of financing and accompaniment to enable companies to enter new markets. Set-up grants are expected to be provided to companies to address the high costs associated with expansion activities. Equity/equity-like capital, such as convertible notes, simple agreements for future equity (SAFEs) and recoverable grants, enables companies to innovate and scale up their operations. Concessional debt lowers the cost of operating in H2R markets compared to business-as-usual.

Technical assistance aims to help reduce the real and perceived risks of entering new markets and build the capacity of companies in key areas such as management and strategy, credit risk analysis, consumer protection, gender equity and market studies. The facility aims to also improve the capacity of local distributors to become reliable partners to suppliers expanding in the region to build a local ecosystem for off-grid solar markets.

## Component 2: Sustainable Market Expansion in H2R countries (Market Expansion Facility)

A subsequent blended finance-backed $200 million Market Expansion Facility aims to provide impact-linked concessionary loans to scale up companies’ operations and settle in H2R countries. A blended finance structure helps mobilize different types of investors with different risk appetites for the same purpose.

The primary instrument used in the Market Expansion Facility is expected to be impact-indexed loans, an innovative RBF approach, where we intend to determine interest rates based on the impacts achieved in terms of first-time energy access and poverty level of the customers. The interest rates should range between a market rate and a zero percent, depending on the impact performance.

The impact-linked incentives are designed to motivate the companies to provide first-time energy access to low-income customers and minimize the level of concessionality to the required level for delivering specific impacts. The objective of this approach is to integrate the impact into our financing structure, therefore into companies’ operations, which will likely allow us to adapt, strengthen and maximize impacts along the way.

Certain KPIs will also be used to ensure companies manage their growth sustainably and inclusively, disincentivizing sales growth at the cost of portfolio quality, for example.

MEF also seeks to invest in accounts receivable facilities, which should allow OGS companies to finance themselves rather than rely on third party investors, making them self-sustaining. Fundraising for the MEF is ongoing. We are not aware of any investors proposing exclusions or restrictions on their use of proceeds. Our intention is to deploy loans only to the borrowers for operations in NOL countries. Assuming there are no exclusions, our intention is to have a single set of criteria when selecting borrowers, and that all investors will receive returns on loans pro rata to their contributions to MEF. We also seek to ensure that all MEF investors will participate in each financing transaction entered into by MEF pro rata to their commitments to MEF.

See sections C.1 and C.2 for a summary of MSF/MEF breakdown by financing source and component.

# Component 1 Intended Activities

## Activity 1.1: Acumen engages stakeholders (partners, donors, govts) towards development of H2R markets, by fundraising & establishing H2R MSF ($50M)

Acumen intends to set up the philanthropically-backed Market Support Facility (MSF). We seek to develop and manage the strategy, naming, branding and legal arrangement of the facility, ready for the implementation stage.

Acumen raises $50M for MSF across several donors, including a $10M repayable grant from GCF. Acumen engages relevant stakeholders (partners, donors, governments) throughout. This includes engaging with nationally designated authorities to receive no-objection letters, workshops with government ministries and entities, town halls with project affected communities, and other engagement strategies with relevant public and private sector organizations.

In relation to the overall management of MSF, the MSF will have a sole member, the AE. The MSF will set up a management committee to oversee the activities and progress of the MSF, currently contemplated to be the Chief of Strategic Initiatives, CFO and GC of the AE. The MSF will have an investment committee to make grant and investment decisions, currently contemplated to be the President and CFO of the AE, together with a Board member of the AE.

The MSF team is expected to consist of a director of investment, a director of operations, a manager of grants and impact, and support personnel. The intention is not to limit the term of the LLC or have it necessarily be required to unwind at the end of the program. Depending on the success and evolution of the vision for the H2R project, the entity may have continued utility, which is hard to predict at this time. However, we may choose to liquidate the entity if there is no further need for the entity at such time.

### H2R aims to develop partnerships and engage with stakeholders (government, investors, opcos, capacity building programs) to improve coordination, address market barriers and share knowledge in H2R markets.  

Hardest-to-Reach seeks to work with and invest in established off-grid solar operators – manufacturers, distributors, and local distribution networks – and, through the Market Support Facility, expects to expand its reach to locally-rooted value chains that will serve these populations over the long term. By encouraging partnerships between established off-grid energy players and local distributors, H2R aims to build robust and coordinated off-grid energy markets in Hardest-to-Reach geographies.  

Across the range of H2R partnerships, coordinated knowledge sharing may help address market barriers to investment. We aspire to generate coordinated insights and opportunities to help reduce inefficient use of resources, create opportunities to leverage stakeholder experiences and competencies, and minimize distortive risks. H2R’s frontier investments and coordination efforts serve to de-risk and prove the viability of OGS investments in these underserved markets.  

We aspire to support the REACH partnership with lean but impactful staffing engagement that will be part of our knowledge sharing ambitions. Hardest-to-Reach aims to inspire other activity by sectoral leaders, industry experts, and relevant stakeholders within the REACH partnership and other forums.  

The aspiration is that by sharing insights from our investment activity with stakeholders across the sector, including those in the REACH partnership, we can generate an enabling environment for others to participate in impactful ways in OGS sectors in H2R markets.

The team will seek to identify a pipeline of potential H2R joint ventures and ensure their strategic fit with the project goals. Acumen will seek to partner with the companies to replicate success cases in H2R countries.  

The team will conduct thorough due diligence on the company including a gender assessment and ESG diligence to be mission aligned and support responsible market development.  

The MSF also will make equity-like investments and impact indexed loans to distributors as fit for the stage of the companies and needs. Similar to above, the team expects to identify a pipeline of distributors for OGS products that can or already do operate in H2R markets with a focus on locally owned and managed distributors to build a healthy local ecosystem.

# Outcome 2: Establishment and Growth of OGS companies in sustainable and gender- and poverty-inclusive ways

H2R seeds new ventures and backs existing players in challenging markets, supporting the establishment and growth of these nascent ventures and markets through partnerships and productive engagements with government and non-government stakeholders. New sales of OGS products help provide first-time connections and electrification through clean energy technologies. By supporting stronger and more viable businesses, creating a local investment history, and incentivizing impact, we hope to create sustainable and impactful opportunities for companies operating in or seeking to expand into H2R markets.

We seek to demonstrate that OGS companies operating in Hardest-to-Reach markets can build climate resilient, sustainable, and inclusive businesses with technical assistance. H2R companies will receive technical assistance across consumer protection, gender, ESG, and enterprise development activities.

Consumer protection activities focus on enhancing consumer protection policies, procedures, and sales strategies for companies, and monitoring portfolio quality to manage credit risk and consumer protections.

On gender, gender experts will support companies in developing and executing gender action plans, improving gender policies, and collecting sex disaggregated data. Additionally, technical assistance funds will be assigned to be used to improve gender equity in the OGS market by training women workers and raising female customer awareness of OGS products.

ESG and climate TA focuses on ESG action plans for companies, support companies on enhancing e-waste policies and procedures, and building climate and ESG resources for OGS companies. Enterprise development work focuses on making companies more investable, supporting talent retention, improving operations, building customer and market knowledge, and supporting financially distressed companies.

We expect companies to more strongly protect customers and mitigate credit risk, identify and engage with ESG risks, build more equitable and empowering workplaces and customer experiences, and develop and manage stronger, more talented and investable OGS companies because of targeted technical support.

## MSF Structure

- MSF is intended to raise grant and repayable grant capital to build capacity for entities to establish and bolster a market for clean energy access for individuals in least developed countries. Target size is $50 million, of which a majority has already been raised and for which several pilots are in planning.
- GCF is requested to provide $10 million in the form of a partially repayable grant for MSF. Such repayable grant shall be used for all activities of the MSF, including expenses, grants and investments; of such GCF funding toward grants and investments is hereinafter referred to as "GCF Investment Proceeds".
- MSF is expected to provide technical assistance grants and potentially recoverable grants/convertible notes/equity, engage in joint ventures and provide impact indexed loans to incentivize established solar home systems manufacturers and distributors to enter LDC markets, and to bolster existing distributors in LDCs.
- Any joint ventures are expected to contain mechanisms for ownership to transfer to the counterparty once the ventures are financially self-sustaining. Joint venture agreements may incorporate grants to cover set up and other initial costs.
- The operations of MSF are overseen by a management committee consisting of the Chief of Strategic Initiatives, Chief Financial Officer and General Counsel of the AE. The management committee seeks to ensure that the operations of the MSF are compliant with GCF requirements, applicable laws, regulations and policies. Grants and investments made by MSF are subject to review and approval of the global Investment Committee of the AE.
- Members of the AE that will operationalize the MSF include the General Counsel, the Chief Strategic Officer, the Chief Financial Officer, a Director, an Associate Director, and the Legal Counsel of the AE.
- MSF is expected to build capacity within entities to be a viable pipeline for MEF borrowers. MSF will take the risk during the development stage of LDC operations for these potential borrowers with its high risk grant and repayable grant capital, reducing the risk for MEF.
- MSF expects to have an investment period of 7 years.

## MEF Structure

- MEF is intended to bring together senior, mezzanine and junior equity capital to incentivize investors with different risk profiles to provide clean energy access for individuals living off-grid in the least developed countries.
- MEF also intends to raise grant capital to enable further interest rate adjustments payable by borrowers as a reward for first-time energy access, among other metrics, which grant capital is intended to be swept to the operating account of MEF and made available for fees, expenses and deployment, as an offset for such adjustments.
- MEF is designed to make loans for working capital to borrowers providing clean energy access in H2R countries, with incentives to borrowers to enable first time clean energy access for their customers, with interest rate adjustments to the extent of such access.
- MEF is expected to use performance incentives within the loan structure to incentivize businesses to achieve specific impact targets, in the form of interest rate adjustments to the extent of achieving impact, to essentially provide a partial offset to interest.
- Borrowers may receive impact adjustments based on certain predetermined metrics. Such metrics will be collected and analyzed annually (using 3rd party verification of certain metrics, such as lean data studies), and result in an impact index score that will be aligned with an interest adjustment.
- In addition to impact adjusted loans, the MEF expects to lend to accounts receivable financing facilities, to support cash and inventory management of OGS companies operating in our target geographies. Current facilities under review have 4 year terms.
- Target MEF size is $200 million, with indicative allocations across layers as follows: senior – $60 million; mezzanine – $60 million; junior – $60 million; and grant – $20 million. This proposal requests GCF to provide $50 million for junior equity and $5 million for non-repayable grant capital.
- The above structure is designed to incentivize capital with different risk tolerance, noting that mission oriented and concessionary capital is expected to provide the mezzanine, junior and grant capital, while more commercially oriented capital is expected to provide senior capital.
- MEF is expected to have a 7 year investment period, which may be extended for up to two one-year periods.
- Investment decisions for MEF will be made by an investment committee designated by the GP.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 62

## Track Record

Acumen is an AE that has three funding proposals in implementation. The first program is KawiSafi Ventures, a venture capital fund focused on creating clean energy access in East Africa, and is at the end of its investment period, having successfully deployed approximately $42 million in investments that as of end of Q3 2022, impacted 110 million low income individuals and offset 24 million metric tons of carbon dioxide and kerosene. The second program is Acumen resilient Agriculture Fund, a venture capital fund focused on building climate resilience for smallholder farmers in West and East Africa, and is in the middle of its investment period, having successfully deployed $20.2 million in investments that as of end of Q3 2022, impacted almost 1.6 million low income individuals. The third program is Energy Access Relief Fund, a debt fund focused on providing concessional capital to support businesses in the solar home system sector survive the impacts of COVID, and is in the middle of its investment period, having successfully deployed approximately $49 million in loans.

In addition, for over 20 years, Acumen has been directly investing in small and medium-sized enterprises that serve low-income communities in developing countries across South Asia, Sub-Saharan Africa, and Latin America, with the goal of changing the way the world tackles poverty. As of the end of 2021, Acumen had deployed approximately $146 million into 151 companies, impacting approximately 443 million lives. A significant sector of focus for Acumen has been the off-grid energy sector, and Acumen contributes to many sectoral initiatives, including with GOGLA.

## Why H2R needs GCF

Addressing the unelectrified populations requires affordable solutions, which themselves require affordable, abundant capital. Yet the perceived risk and small size of these markets means that capital there is both scarce and expensive. Blended finance, anchored by the most influential concessional investor focused on climate change adaptation (GCF) is the only way to overcome this impasse and bring energy to people who are vulnerable to climate change.

GCF’s involvement in this project is critical. GCF’s concessional capital will play a catalytic role to mobilize capital at scale and channel it toward serving low income and climate vulnerable customers in LDCs. Public and private capital directed to the energy access sector has been concentrated in a few lower-risk markets in Africa and has avoided the regions with the lowest electrification rates. Between 2012 and 2019, western, central and southern Africa accounted for 71 percent of SSA’s unelectrified population, yet received just 24 percent of cumulative off-grid solar investment.

Research shows that even when investment dollars are directed to low-income regions, they are not always directed where the need is greatest. Most investors only operate in markets that are lower risk or proven to yield financial returns. For this reason, progress has been contained to a few investor-ready countries.

According to IEA research, the affordability of clean energy transitions will depend on reducing the cost and improving the availability of capital. Addressing the remaining unelectrified population demands a

> [91 IEA SDG 7 Database – Access to Electricity (n.d)]
> [92 IEA Financing clean energy transitions in emerging and developing economies (2021)]

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 64

shift towards more challenging market environments and thus keeping financing costs low will be vital to enabling clean energy transitions while keeping them inclusive and affordable. However, capital is substantially more costly in emerging and developing economies than in advanced economies. Nominal financing costs are up to seven times higher than in the United States and Europe, with higher levels in riskier segments.93 This points to a relatively high bar for projects to raise debt finance and offer sufficient returns on equity.

OGS companies have therefore not been able to expand into these riskier markets, because their cost of financing is too high, financing doesn’t exist, or they don’t have the capacity to expand into nascent markets. Sales of off-grid solar products are very low or non-existent in many of these geographies, even though the technology has been proven as an effective means of providing energy access for low-income communities. For example, OGS sales in Burundi over the last 3 years were less than 0.1 percent of total sales in East Africa across the same period. Furthermore, sales growth in Sierra Leone over the last 3 years has been four times lower than the average across West Africa. What's more, sales of OGS products in DRC over the last 3 years were 1.6 percent of the total SSA sales, despite the country making up over 8 percent of the population of sub-Saharan Africa. The business-as-usual approach is not serving low-income communities in Hardest-to-Reach geographies, even though they have a significant need for energy access.

The GCF will enable H2R initiatives to channel much needed financing and technical assistance to OGS companies to support them to enter riskier markets and grow sustainably. The concessional component provided by GCF is vital because it means that Acumen can issue financing with lower interest rates and longer tenors. Companies will therefore be able to access affordable capital and should not suffer from over-indebtedness. Furthermore, they will be able to pass the concessionality onto customers in the form of cheaper products. Without a concessional component, OGS companies would have to charge potentially prohibitively high prices to their customers, many of whom will be earning below $3.20 a day. If this were to happen, the H2R initiative would not be able to have an impact on the people who need energy access most.

H2R is creating a paradigm shift in African energy markets by unlocking finance from investors that would otherwise find these markets too risky. GCF's catalytic capital will be vital in providing companies with concessional financing and mobilizing different types of investors with different risk appetites at scale to tackle the aforementioned problem. Each type of financing will likely provide unique benefits:

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 65

## Financing will allow Acumen to provide a range of financial instruments to meet the needs of companies entering risky markets, including start-up grants and technical assistance.

### Junior equity:
GCF’s $50 million investment of junior equity, along with a target $10 junior equity from other financiers, is needed to create the Market Expansion Facility’s blended finance structure, which is designed to mobilize an additional target $120 million from investors with different risk appetites at scale. GCF’s risk-taking capital in this structure will seek to mobilize financing from private sector investors, thereby crowding mainstream investors into a market that has mostly received financing from foundations, risk-supportive DFIs and impact-focused investors. This will allow the H2R initiative to reach significant scale and maximize its impact.

### Grant:
GCF’s $5m in grant funding will be used to provide performance incentives to investee companies based on the performance of impact targets particularly targeting low-income customers with first-time energy access - sustainably and inclusively.

The 1:1:1 structure is intended to match the risk appetite of the investors with the incentive needed by companies to expand into H2R markets. Concessional financing is needed by companies to go to hardest-to-reach markets and thus the cost of capital has to be low enough to incentivize the companies to do so. At the same time Acumen would like to attract private investors into the sector. This tiered structure provides a de-facto guarantee for senior investors, despite high risk, low return investments.

Without GCF’s $10 million repayable grant to MSF, this intervention would not be able to innovate and bolster the LDC markets in which H2R seeks to operate. Without GCF’s $55 million anchor investment in the Market Expansion Facility, Hardest-to-Reach would not be able to crowd in other investors and would not be able to reach sufficient scale to have a significant impact in the region. With GCF’s investment, Hardest-to-Reach aims to shift the paradigm and develop a new frontier for energy investment by creating new markets that have the potential to thrive beyond the lifetime of the initiative.

## What GCF brings to H2R

The GCF is the world’s largest dedicated climate fund, and as such brings significant credibility to the Hardest-to-Reach initiative. In addition to the direct investment into the initiative, GCF’s involvement will have the following benefits:

- Provide a framework for mobilizing support from relevant governments, with nationally designated authorities as touch points for continued engagement and alignment
- Mobilize investors by enhancing both the risk profile and reputability of the H2R initiative
- Enhance stakeholder engagement with the initiative through its dedication to country ownership
- Strengthen the climate rationale of the initiative through its robust investment criteria and integrated results management framework
- Have strong signaling effects to the market of the importance of energy access in increasing the adaptive capacity of beneficiaries

The proposed approach has replicability in-built. Two separate financing facilities are proposed with different investment risk profiles, and will invest with different capital blends to match the needs of the company and market, as scale is achieved. The Market Support Facility (MSF) expects to extend debt, equity/equity-like capital, and grants using a patient capital approach that is consistent with Acumen’s core investment experience. While the MSF seeks to prove an approach in a new market with a range of capital, the Market Expansion Facility (MEF) deploys impact-linked debt that aim to help responsibly expand the reach of investees in the long-term.

By sharing insights and results from the initiative, valuable lessons can be passed on to other investors and OGS enterprises. The H2R intervention seeks to provide capacity building activities in the form of technical assistance to investee companies and ecosystem development to strengthen the off-grid solar markets in Host Nations. A Technical Assistance Facility (TAF) is expected to be established as part of the Market Support Facility. By integrating capacity building into the initiative through the TAF, H2R aims to develop more resilient and impactful business models that can scale their operations sustainably in H2R markets. As a result, these markets should become stronger and more competitive, paving the way for other OGS businesses to thrive even beyond the life of the initiative.

TA will be provided for four main areas: Consumer Protection; Enterprise Development; ESG; and Gender. In particular, Hardest-to-Reach aims to support investees to navigate the business challenges, risks and barriers of delivering energy solutions to underserved segments; to grow sustainably and inclusively, with an eye on protecting consumers; to access capacity building for better financial performance; to enhance commercial and leadership capabilities for greater business sustainability, particularly for locally-owned and managed companies; to identify and mitigate environmental, social and governance risks; and understand and address the inequities women face in energy, as users, entrepreneurs and members of the workforce.

H2R aims to also support the development of off-grid solar markets with a gender equity lens by providing training for female professionals and community members, and by uplifting women in OGS markets, as leaders, entrepreneurs, valued workforce and consumers. Similarly, H2R aims to strengthen ESG
