# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 79

## Sub-Saharan Africa and Carbon Emissions

Sub-Saharan Africa produces, on average, only 0.8 tonnes of CO₂eq per person per year — compared to a global average of 4.8 tonnes.

However, highly developed and coal-dependent South Africa (85 percent electrified) produces nearly ten times that per person — an extreme example and a cause for some concern if large nations such as Mozambique (29% electrified), DRC (19%), and Uganda (29%) were to only partially follow suit.

These are all countries with electrification rates below the regional average of just over 48 percent, which is the primary selection criteria for Hardest-to-Reach target countries.

Predictive modeling illustrates Africa’s low-carbon SDG 7 scenarios and their correlation to SDG 13 as the region electrifies.

## Low-Carbon Energy Access

Providing first-time electricity access via a low carbon trajectory avoids approximately 268 million tonnes of CO₂eq emissions, with a technology mix that is 45 percent decentralized renewables.

If African emissions per person were to rise even to the current global average, the continent would emit 12.5 billion tonnes of greenhouse gasses per year by 2050 — roughly equivalent to all emissions from China and India today.

## H2R Initiative and Sustainable Development

The H2R initiative seeks to contribute to SSA’s low-emissions sustainable development pathway.

By providing people with first-time energy access through low-emissions energy sources, such as solar home systems, customers gain trust and familiarity in the technology and payment models and opt to use similar technologies in the future, even as their energy consumption grows.

This aligns with the idea of low-carbon development pathways, where economic development is decoupled from increasing carbon emissions.

## Impact Potential

- Impact Potential (iii): 5.4M people with improved climate resilience through diversified income sources enabled by first-time access to a solar powered product
- Impact Potential (iv) # of investments, including follow-on investments
- Impact Potential (v): 13,922 jobs (4,641 women)
- Impact Potential (vi): stronger more robust primary energy systems through decentralization of energy supply (see below)

# D 
## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 80 

### Impact Potential (vii): anticipatory, absorptive and adaptive resilience for households via OGS characteristics (see below) 

Energy System Resilience through decentralized solar  
Access to electricity is considered a key part of adaptive capacity; one of the three principal factors which determine climate change vulnerability in a region, along with sensitivity and exposure to hazards and impacts. Adaptive capacity, the characteristic which allows people to cope with these hazards and impacts, includes elements such as social networks, livelihood diversity, asset diversity, innovation and access to financial services. Anticipatory capacity, the characteristic which allows people to plan for and invest and/or change behavior and habits in advance of events, includes disaster preparedness, including access and the ability to respond to weather forecasts and warnings of hazards. Electricity access is the thread linking all of these, as it plays a key role in enabling all these components. As such it plays a significant role in making livelihoods less vulnerable in H2R target countries.  

Limited power access in turn threatens livelihoods which rely on agriculture for a number of reasons. First and foremost, the inability to access power means the inability to reliably access information and to communicate. This is a crucial weakness during natural disasters, which are expected to continue to increase in frequency and intensity as a result of climate change. In terms of enabling anticipatory capacity, energy access can contribute to increasing household income by increasing working hours through access to light and increasing opportunities for home based business, while direct access to power reduces the cost and time investment in keeping a mobile phone charged, or of keeping a kerosene lamp fueled.  

Energy systems have been compromised both by extreme and slow-onset climate events, with resulting economic losses, disruptions of services and impacts to wellbeing. The threat to the reliable provision of energy is exacerbated by the centralization of power generation and where distribution is interconnected, including across regional power pools. Centralized energy systems across sub-Saharan Africa are already facing the strain of climate change. For example, following low rainfall and droughts in 2015-16 in southern Africa, power generation reduced by 75 percent at the Kariba dam in Zambia, and by 66 percent at the Cahora Bassa dam in Mozambique due to insufficient water resources, with widespread impact on electricity supplies across neighboring countries. As a result, Malawi, Zambia and Zimbabwe all experienced widespread and prolonged load shedding. The impact was felt throughout the regional economy and reflected in reduced GDP growth.  

Furthermore, in 2019 Cyclone Idai caused significant damages to Mozambique’s energy sector, and resulted in an estimated loss of $47.9 million. The cyclone damaged or destroyed 1,345 km of transmission lines, 10,216 km of distribution lines, two 90 MW generation plants, 30 sub-stations, 4,000 transformers.  

Similarly, Malawi lost 30% of its electricity to Tropical Storm Ana in February 2022 because of damage to the 130 MW Kapichira hydropower plant.  

The World Bank approved a loan of $45M to restore the power plant, which is set to reopen in May 2023, leaving the country at 2/3 of its energy generation capacity for over a year.

# D GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 81

demonstrate some of the ways in which traditional utility scale energy systems are vulnerable to the effects of climate change.

The IPCC has therefore recognised the role that decentralized renewable energy can play in increasing the resilience of energy systems, as it helps to diversify energy supplies and reduce pressure on the grid.108

Decentralized energy also has a role in reducing the vulnerability of rural populations to climate change. In fact, evidence suggests that solar home system usage is considered as a coping mechanism against natural disaster-induced electricity disruption by households in climate vulnerable areas. The research points in particular to the attributes that make distributed renewable energy easier to be rebuilt (where necessary), reconnected, and fully functioning in a shorter period.109

By diversifying energy sources, increasing the distribution of energy solutions and adopting technical improvements to reduce systems’ climate vulnerability, we are able to see more adapted and resilient energy systems in H2R countries.110

Anticipatory, absorptive and adaptive capacity for households through characteristics of OGS

Energy access impacts the ability to anticipate, avoid, plan for, cope with, recover from and adapt to (climate related) shocks and stresses’.111

Three dimensions or capacities of climate resilience can be improved through off-grid solar: adaptation (adaptive capacity), anticipation (anticipatory capacity), and absorption (absorptive capacity). By enabling the sale of climate resilient, affordable and reliable OGS products to underserved communities, Hardest-to-Reach contributes to all three.

Firstly, OGS energy products are durable and portable, meaning that they are able to withstand climate-change related phenomena, and can be moved and safely stored in the event of extreme weather events.112

They are also repairable, enabling a resumption of energy provision following product damage.

Thirdly, they lead to a more diversified energy system and take pressure off the grid, which can reduce systemic climate vulnerability.

As described, distributed renewable energy (DRE) reduces vulnerability to climate impacts by increasing the resilience of energy provision in the face of natural disasters and climate shocks, which are increasingly frequent and intense in these geographies.113

Each solar home system is an important independent energy source that can continue to provide a household with energy even if the grid has been compromised because of increased demand or damage caused by an extreme weather event.

Recent data show that an estimated 775 million people worldwide are connected to weak grids, undermining the potential social and economic development of access to electricity. Off-grid solar can play an important role in improving the quality of electricity access here as well as deliver significant climate mitigation and adaptation benefits; Diesel generators used to provide grid back-up emit more than 100 megatons of CO2

## ARA 2: Health, wellbeing, food and water security  
- Total project direct and indirect beneficiaries: 54.3M  
- Impact Potential (i): 54.3M people with first time energy access, displacing harmful and/or emissions intensive lighting sources  
- Impact Potential (ii): 70% of customers (~50.6M) indicating quality of life improvement  
- Impact Potential (iii): Improved health, wellbeing and quality of life through energy access (see below)  

### Improved health, wellbeing and quality of life through energy access  
In least developed country contexts, in particular, energy access is an enabler for poverty reduction that is crucial for enhancing people’s livelihoods and wellbeing. According to the IPCC and research by the UK’s Foreign, Commonwealth & Development Office (formerly DFID), there is growing evidence of the role of DRE in reducing people’s overall vulnerability, especially in underserved populations, through more well lit private land and assets, less hazardous household lighting, such as via open flames, and cleaner air in the absence of black carbon emitted from kerosene lanterns.  

Energy access can also reduce the impact of climate hazards. Without electricity, for example, households cannot receive SMS, radio or TV news to receive early warning messages or public safety announcements before and after a climate event. Thus, across sub-Saharan Africa, where 33 of the world's 46 LDCs are situated, delivering energy access to enable specific applications such as information communication technologies, and by driving socio-economic development (i.e., improving incomes, health outcomes and access to education), is highly impactful for quality of life perceptions, health and overall well being.  

The 60 Decibels Energy Benchmark, a Lean Data survey of off-grid energy customers in developing economies, found that 51% of people felt that their quality of life had improved significantly after buying an off-grid product in general, compared to 68% for solar-powered off-grid products in particular. Acumen’s PEII portfolio companies scored high on this metric, where 60 Decibels surveys of PEII portfolio customers found that 64% of off-grid energy customers felt their quality of life had improved substantially.  

Whilst this measure is subjective and could incorporate a number of different factors, it indicates how significant an impact energy access has on people’s lives. Finally, residential solar PV requires neither water nor additional land to generate electricity, meaning OGS products reduce stress on land use and water systems, with broader positive impacts on food security and biodiversity.  

Even though it can be concluded that the impact of energy access through SHS on livelihood resilience in SSA is conditional and limited, solar lamps and SHS are nevertheless an important milestone on the route to initial last-mile electrification in rural areas of sub-Saharan Africa. # D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Paradigm shift potential is defined as “degree to which the proposed activity can catalyse impact beyond a one-off project or programme investment”. In this section, elaborate on the contribution to paradigm shift and how the proposed project/programme aims to contribute towards it based on the theory of change described in section B2(a). Also describe how and to what extent the project/programme will be able to promote or contribute to paradigm shift through the below.

- Potential for scaling up and replication  
- Potential for knowledge sharing and learning  
- Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment  
- Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies  
- Overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways consistent with relevant national climate change adaptation strategies and plans  

## Paradigm Shift

The H2R initiative will deliver a paradigm shift by:

1. Nurturing and developing companies to serve underserved off-grid solar markets; and  
2. Unlocking the provision of - and investment in - clean, climate-resilient decentralized solar, that will  
3. Power low-carbon economic development for the long-term.  

One of the key aims of Hardest-to-Reach is to transform the African energy sector by building (and eventually scaling up) off-grid markets where there currently are none or they are fledgling. The initiative will focus on LDCs in sub-Saharan Africa, particularly with electrification rates at-or-below the regional average, where investors have not been willing to enter. H2R’s blended finance structure is transformational in its design as it will crowd-in investors that would otherwise find the markets too nascent or risky. The initiative intends to de-risk these markets by providing new market entrants with impact-linked concessional finance and targeted technical assistance, and seeks to support the development of local networks and capacity. By proving the OGS business model in untested markets, companies can also crowd in additional capital to achieve scale in these markets.

In the scale phase, H2R’s impact-linked debt financing aspires to incentivize OGS companies to maintain their focus on low-income, first-time energy access customers.

A blend of capital is needed to achieve this. Data from Shell shows that approximately $11 billion will be required in debt into SHS enterprises towards SDG 7 in non-established markets.118 A further $2.8 billion of

# D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Describe the wider benefits and priorities of the project/programme in relation to the Sustainable Development Goals and provide the potential in terms of:  
● Environmental co-benefits  
● Social co-benefits including health co-benefit  
● Economic co-benefits  
● Gender-sensitive development benefit  

Modern society depends on reliable and affordable energy services to function smoothly and to develop equitably. Carbon intensive development patterns have historically relied on inexpensive and energy-dense fossil fuels, which are also the primary source of greenhouse gas emissions contributing to climate change.  

Hardest-to-Reach is aligned with SDG 7 (affordable, reliable, modern and sustainable energy for all) and through the power of energy access will enable broader sustainable development outcomes; good health and wellbeing (SDG 3), improved educational outcomes (SDG 4), economic savings and income generation (SDG 1 and SDG 8), and climate action (SDG 13). Acumen’s intervention is also partnership-focused, envisioning a more coordinated investment ecosystem to transform the prospects for growth in the off-grid solar sector (consistent with SDG 17).  

The 16 target countries for Acumen’s Hardest-to-Reach initiative signed the Paris Agreement and submitted Nationally Determined Contributions (NDC). All 16 countries have pledged both conditional and unconditional targets to reduce their GHG emissions by 2030 under the Business-as-Usual (BAU) scenario. The unconditional targets are targets expected to be met by countries with their own resources without external support, whilst conditional targets are subject to financial support from the international community. Two of the 16 countries have set their conditional mitigation targets at over 50% emission reduction by 2030, namely: Malawi and Togo at (51% and 51%) respectively.120 The lowest conditional emission reduction targets were from Burundi at 16%.  

The NDCs further incorporate mitigation and adaptation actions. For instance, Uganda’s NDC provides 48 priority adaptation actions and 82 indications with adaptation targets for 2030, one of the targets is to achieve 75% electrification by 2030.121 Acumen’s objectives through Hardest-to-Reach are directly aligned with national climate priorities, including National Adaptation Plans, National Climate Change Action Plans, and NDCs. Further details on country electrification, climate policies and climate adaptation overviews can be found in the country climate reports.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 86

The H2R initiative has a number of broader sustainable development impacts. Firstly, off-grid solar products can improve people’s health, safety and wellbeing. For those trapped in energy poverty, particularly the rural households in H2R target countries, fossil-fuel based energy sources such as kerosene may be the only available energy source. These traditional lanterns provide low-quality light, are harmful to people’s health because of household pollutants, and can cause burns and fires. This disproportionately affects women and children who spend more time at home. Replacing fossil-fuel based energy sources with clean off-grid solar products can therefore have positive impacts on people’s health and safety as customers will have access to better quality lighting, which can prevent falls at night, help with security, and allow for cleaner air at home.

A 2018 study in Uganda cited the fact that single-wick lanterns were once responsible for 70 percent of fire incidents and 80 percent of burn injuries in energy poor households. These data points are likely to be less stark in today’s Uganda, thanks in part to some success of off-grid solar technologies. There is still a task to do to ensure no household has to rely on these fuels for lighting. Meanwhile, where households have replaced kerosene lanterns with single use dry cell battery LEDs, these batteries pose a health (and environmental) risk, as they contain heavy metals. Additionally, fuel combustion releases hundreds of pollutants into the air, including carbon monoxide, formaldehyde and benzene, along with a myriad of other damaging particles.

Secondly, OGS products can enable people to generate income through their use. This can be directly (use in a business) or indirectly (task-shifting activities to the evening because of better quality light). This can help increase people’s economic security.

Thirdly, the PAYGo financing structure should enable many people to access mobile wallets and credit for the first time, leading to the financial inclusion of low-income people and helping to build their credit scores. This is particularly important for women, who are often excluded from accessing credit because of land-based collateral requirements.

Fourthly, the local distribution networks that will be created and after-sales services provided will be a source of livelihood options and income generation opportunities, as they should create employment for people in low-income areas. The increased income they receive is expected to be an important contributor to their increased resilience and adaptive capacity.

Lastly, H2R envisions a co-benefit of job creation that is consistent with SDG 8. As OGS companies scale, they will require additional human capital to support their growth and sustain their level of service. Jobs include customer service personnel, office and administrators, product sales staff, and technicians for installations and repair.

The H2R ESMS is also aligned with the GCF Environmental and Social Policy and the Acumen ESMS for GCF-funded Projects. The ESIA and ESMS are submitted as part of this proposal.

The project addresses the absence of alternative sources of financing by providing support for the development of the OGS industry, which can help fill fiscal or balance of payments gaps that prevent government from addressing the needs of the country.

The project addresses the need for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity through the development of a gender assessment in accordance with industry best practices, guidance and support from gender experts, community input, and following the GCF Gender Policy. The investment deficit is at the root of both the energy access gap and the lack of adaptive capacity faced by countries in sub-Saharan Africa. Despite the tremendous progress made by off-grid solar companies in improving energy access in sub-Saharan Africa, a substantial number of financially viable customers in difficult, hard-to-reach markets will not be catered for by current market-based interventions.

OGS companies were estimated to have cumulatively raised US$1.5 billion as of 2019 from an increasingly diverse investor base as the industry has matured. However, there is a clear geographic bias in the flow of funding. 80 percent of the capital raised in the sector, between 2012-2019, has flowed to larger and more established, first-generation, companies whose primary location and focus is East Africa, with Kenya attracting the lion's share of the capital.

Additionally, as OGS start-ups evolve towards profitability, they have increasingly focused on entrenching their market position and attracting higher margin customers in the most active markets at the expense of expanding into the less-active markets with a higher actual and perceived risk.

As the two Figures show, the regional disparities in the size and capitalisation of the OGS sector are largely incongruent with the distribution of people without access to electricity. Thus, it is imperative to direct more resources, financial and otherwise, to where they are needed the most.

The overall level of investment has been adversely affected by the pandemic, with foreign private investment into Africa falling some 16% in 2020. Significant financial commitments are required to close this gap and governments are unable to carry the fiscal burden alone. One estimate suggests that $35 billion a year is required to reach SDG 7 by 2030.

At the same time, the NDCs of 40 African countries cumulatively show a need for an estimated $331 billion in investment for adaptation through 2030, with only 20% of this figure coming from public budgets. The map shows the dearth of international financing going to developing economies in support of clean energy, particularly given the size of developing-economy populations.

Within sub-Saharan Africa a concentration of investment flows to now-proven markets risks leaving other countries and regions behind. However, barriers exist, including political instability, macroeconomic uncertainty (because of inflation and exchange rates), policy and regulatory issues, institutional weaknesses, and lack of transparency. All these make for a less favorable investment climate.

### Benin Country Context
Benin is a low-income country, with a population of more than 13 million people and a population growth rate of 2.8%.¹³³ The rural electrification rate is 9%. Benin’s current electrification rate stands at 33%, which is below the sub-Saharan African average of 48%, leaving 7.9 million people without access to electricity.¹³⁴ This is disaggregated with 58% and 9% urban and rural electrification respectively.¹³⁵

As the country develops, the energy demand is anticipated to increase, thus there is a need to match the energy supply to the growing energy demand.

### DRC Country Context
DRC is a low-income country, with a population of more than 96 million people and a population growth rate of 3.2% per year.¹³⁶ According to the World Bank Group, poverty in DRC is high and is among the five poorest nations in the world. The 2019 electrification rate for the DRC stood at 9% relative to the sub-Saharan African average of 48%, leaving 79 million people without access to electricity.¹³⁷ 19% of the urban population has access to electricity whereas this figure is less than 1% for the rural population.¹³⁸

In 2021, DRC had an installed electricity capacity of 2.8 GW comprising 99% hydropower and 1% non-renewable sources. The national electricity grid powers 19% of the population living in urban areas but only 1% for the population living in rural areas.¹³⁹

### Burundi Country Context
Burundi is a low-income country, with a population of more than 12.55 million people and a population growth rate of 2.7%.¹⁴⁰ In 2019, Burundi’s national electrification rate stood at 11%, relative to the sub-Saharan African average of 48%, leaving 11 million people without access to electricity.¹⁴¹ There is a 66% connection rate in urban areas, but only 2% in rural areas.

As of 2019, the total energy supply in Burundi was sourced from 85% renewables, 14% oil, and 1% coal and others.¹⁴² Of the renewables, 98% is biomass, 0% solar, and 2% hydro. Hydroelectric power alone provides electricity to 9% of the population, which translates to 75% of those with access to electricity.

## Mozambique Country Context

Mozambique is a low-income country, with a population of more than 32 million people and a population growth rate of 2.9%.¹⁴³ Only 22% of the population in rural areas had access to electricity in 2019.¹⁴⁴ This is comparable to the urban electrification rate of 57%. ## Zambia Country Context

Zambia is a low-income country, with a population of more than 18 million people and a population growth rate of 2.9%.¹⁴⁶ Zambia’s primary sources of energy include wood fuel, hydropower, coal and solar. Zambia has an installed capacity of 3.3 GW, comprising 80% hydropower, 11% coal, 4% diesel, 3% heavy fuel oil (HFO) and 3% solar.¹⁴⁷

## Gender Context

The link between energy access and gender equality is compelling. Energy poverty has a strong gender dimension, as the lack of access to energy inhibits a woman’s freedom from household drudgery, her freedom to allocate time, to access economic opportunities, and enjoy improved household living conditions. Off-grid energy technologies have a direct benefit to women’s health, particularly in terms of reduced risks of pulmonary and respiratory diseases, caused by long-term proximity to fumes from kerosene lanterns.¹⁴⁸

At the enterprise level, women’s participation and leadership in the OGS sector can help accelerate the transition to a low-carbon economy by promoting clean energy use and addressing energy poverty, while also advancing women’s economic empowerment. However, multiple barriers limit women’s ability to access OGS technologies and to fully participate in OGS value chains as business leaders, employees and entrepreneurs. These barriers include, but are not limited to, (i) a historically unequal distribution of economic assets which impacts women’s ability to access OGS products; (ii) the limited collection and use of sex-disaggregated data to help better understand the needs of women as energy users, and more broadly as stakeholders in the sector; (iii) a lack of targeted training and mentorship opportunities for women to enter the workforce and thrive in the clean energy economy.

Overall, evidence shows that women have less access to off-grid electricity than their male counterparts, and they mostly use pico-solar products and lanterns or other lower-tier technologies.¹⁴⁹ SHS customers are predominantly rural men living under the median poverty line of $3.10.¹⁵⁰ In a survey conducted on 35,000 customers of 49 OGS companies in 16 countries, 60 Decibels found that only 32% of customers

are women.151 The Gender Country Assessments in Annex 8 outline these national-level dynamics in detail.  

### Energy Access Companies  

Equity investors push for profit margins, creating pressure on energy companies to shift in focus toward multi-purpose and higher-end SHS and away from entry-level products and first-time access as a result.  

Companies are often reluctant to pursue more first-time access customers in unfamiliar territories and have indicated that entering a market as a second or third mover is far more advantageous. This is due to the significant hurdles that need to be overcome in a completely new market such as finding good local management, establishing logistics lines, and understanding the nuances of the local finance and regulatory system. While the challenges faced by OGS are of a similar nature across Sub-Saharan Africa, they can be prohibitively more pronounced in some countries, substantially diminishing the attractiveness of a market as an expansion destination.  

OGS companies in these countries also face geographical barriers such as vast distances between the location of targeted customers and the capital city or warehousing locations, which is exacerbated by poor road networks.  

These challenges or viability gaps can be segmented into three broad categories—company-specific constraints, market constraints, and funding constraints—as summarized in the table below.  

### Company-specific viability gaps  

Refer to constraints which are an outcome of the OGS company’s strategic and market positioning and includes issues with existing capital structures and lack of management bandwidth.  

### Market constraints  

On the other hand, are specific to each country and are the result of structural and economic deficiencies such as high poverty rates, low mobile penetration rates, different fiscal systems and political risk.  

### Funding constraints  

Refer to lack of bespoke financial products that can be leveraged by OGS to expand into more challenging markets.  

These viability gaps are presented in the table.

In addition, there are those for whom commercial access to energy access will not be a viable option. Payment plan models, such as PAYGo, can enable lower upfront investment and payment over time, making systems affordable for poor customers. PAYGo increases by 40% the number of people who can afford an entry-level (Tier I) solar product. Even in the short term, the PAYGo model enables customers living in off-grid regions to transition away from fossil fuel-based energy sources, such as kerosene or diesel generators, to solar energy, with material GHG emissions avoidance impacts.

In addition, a climate justice component is tied to the needs of communities living in energy poverty. Low-income people in Hardest-to-Reach regions have contributed the least to climate change, yet they stand to suffer the most from it and benefit the least from global capital allocated for renewable energy and climate-friendly investment. Governments and climate investors have bypassed them, particularly in the most remote and fragile regions, prioritizing the countries that have higher greenhouse gas emissions and offer better returns on investment. Through Hardest-to-Reach, Acumen will invest where others won't, to bring remote, low-income people access to clean, renewable solar energy, helping them adapt to a changing climate and build the resilience needed to face climate and economic shocks.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 95

## Climate Resilience in Priority Thematic Sectors

Low-carbon energy development, in particular via solar and other renewable sources, is outlined in the plan.

### Guinea

- The national electrification rate in Guinea is 46%.
- Guinea aims to achieve universal energy access by 2030.
- There is the potential for off-grid solutions in the country, and one of Guinea’s strategic priorities is to promote decentralized energy solutions involving local communities and the private sector at the rural level.
- In its NDC, Guinea outlined an unconditional emissions reduction target of 9.7% by 2030 relative to the business-as-usual scenario, and an emissions target of 17% by 2030 that is conditional on external support.
- Guinea relies on hydropower for 58% of total electricity generation, meaning its energy sector is vulnerable to climate change.
- Reduced rainfall combined with warming is projected to reduce flows in the Konkouré River in western Guinea by 30–50 percent by 2100.
- This is a major concern, given that several dams along the river provide much of the country’s hydropower.
- Water has therefore been highlighted as a key adaptation area in Guinea’s NDC.

### Sierra Leone

- Sierra Leone has a national electrification rate of 26%.
- The government has a target to reach a 92% electrification rate by 2030 and recognized that 42% of the population would be best reached by off-grid stand-alone systems by 2023.
- Sierra Leone contributes to 0.02% of global GHG emissions and the government has committed in its NDC to reduce emissions by 25% by 2050 compared to the projected business-as-usual level.
- There is strong alignment between Sierra Leone’s NAP and H2R.
- It aims at universal access to energy through the promotion of renewables, in particular promoting off-grid stand-alone solar investments through technical advice and knowledge sharing.
- The government has a dedicated Off-grid Solar Energy Strategy (OGSES) with a strong focus on mobilizing financial institutions to create investment packages and counterpart funding.

### Uganda

- Uganda has a national electrification rate of 29%.
- Uganda has made huge progress in closing the energy access gap, as electrification rates grew by 3% per year between 2010 and 2019.
- Uganda has made electrification a national priority and aims to achieve a national electrification rate of above 98% by 2030 under the Sustainability Energy For All (SE4All) Initiative.
- The Government estimates there are potentially 2,906,525 rural households that could be electrified through the provision of solar home systems and up to 130,000 households that could be electrified through mini-grids.
- In its NDC Uganda outlined an emissions reduction target of 22% by 2030 relative to the business-as-usual scenario, conditional on external support of 70% of the required financial resources.
- Energy is one of its key sectors of focus for emissions reduction.
- The country’s energy sector is also highly vulnerable to climate change as it is dominated by hydropower.
- Energy supply is at risk of being disrupted because of extreme weather events, such as reduced river flows resulting from droughts, and damage to power plants and transmission lines from floods and landslides.
- Energy is therefore an adaptation priority under Uganda’s NDC.

### Zambia

- The national electrification rate in Zambia is 37%.
- The country is targeting universal energy access by 2030, which means connecting over 11M people.
- The Government also aims to connect 31.6% of the population to SHS by 2030.
- Zambia’s NDC outlined an ambitious unconditional commitment to reduce GHGs by 25% compared with BAU by 2030, and a commitment to reduce emissions by 47% compared with BAU based on conditional support.
- Energy is one of its key areas of emissions reductions.
- The energy sector is also vulnerable to climate change, as 84% of its electricity generation is from hydropower.
- Energy generation has

Hardest-to-Reach’s Market Expansion Facility is expected to be funded by capital from a range of investors, including private sector financiers, Development Finance Institutions (DFIs), governments, foundations, and corporations. The blended structure with the support of GCF should mobilize different types of investors with different risk appetites at scale for the same purpose, providing first-time energy access for low income customers in Hardest-to-Reach geographies.

Generally, the initiative’s goal is to substantially preserve investor capital, not to produce significant returns. The H2R initiative anticipates providing funding to energy access companies with the intermediate goal of providing sufficient and appropriate liquidity to firms that are in the pioneer gap. Lack of returns of invested capital (including some losses) that enable the sector’s preservation or development would be deemed a successful outcome.

Hardest-to-Reach has set a fundraising target of $200 million for the overall MEF, inclusive of the potential GCF investment. The targeted raise is meant to be sufficient to sustain the operations and impact of a significant number of energy access companies. Government agencies, DFIs, and even commercial investors have indicated an interest in layering their capital with first-loss grant capital. In fact, GCF’s $55 million investment into the facility will likely allow Acumen to crowd-in $145 million of co-financing from other investors, including those from the private sector.

GCF is mobilizing nearly three times as much capital into an LDC-focused initiative that investors would otherwise find too risky.

The Hardest-to-Reach initiative is also very efficient and effective in terms of its impact. The $250m raised for both the Market Support Facility and Market Expansion Facility is targeting the provision of electricity to 72.4 million people, many of whom live below the poverty line. The initiative intends to provide electricity to millions of people at a very low cost, especially when compared with the material impact that electricity access will have on people’s lives.

As a further marker of efficiency, we note that the total investment amount well exceeds the total H2R project size through reinvestments. Without GCF’s participation, Hardest-to-Reach may be able to proceed with an initial raise, but it will likely be unable to meet more than a fraction of the demonstrated demand for liquidity. ## Other Sources for Ongoing Project Monitoring

The Project team intends to also utilize several other sources for ongoing Project monitoring. First, the team expects to work with a consumer impact surveying company to conduct consumer-level surveys to get beneficiary feedback and data on a number of topics. H2R hopes to learn about impact, poverty, consumer protection, gender, ESG, climate resilience and adaptation, and other topics. This data is expected to inform Project implementation and strategy.

Finally, the H2R team expects to monitor the Project through extensive stakeholder engagement. The H2R team wants to share Project results with stakeholders to get their feedback on an ongoing basis to ensure that their voice informs our monitoring practice and understanding of the Project impact.

## Reporting

The Project expects to report financial, impact, and climate results to a number of stakeholders on an annual basis. Investors are expected to receive reporting on a quarterly and annual basis detailing the Project activities, financial statements, climate results, and impact data. GCF will also receive reporting via the APR. Reporting requirements are expected to be established in legal agreements between donors, investors, and the project team.

H2R expects to provide investors with an annual MEF report and an annual impact and ESG report. MEF investors will also receive an annual audited account of the MEF within 120 calendar days of the end of the relevant accounting period. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 149

## Selected Risk Factor 2: Macro

### Category: Probability Impact
- **Other**: High High

### Description
Hardest-to-Reach markets often face significant security risks, political volatility, regulatory inconsistency and legislative unpredictability. These risks do not exist in isolation, some even have the potential to be correlated across a given region. A major crisis or series of interlocking risk events could derail progress on energy access.

Moreover, global trends have been very impactful to volatile markets and unstable political environments. Inflation, a potential global recession, and the ongoing effects of the Covid-19 pandemic have created even more instability in H2R markets.

Macro and global risks have had significant impacts on potential offgrid solar customers. Rising inflation has increased the costs of necessary goods and services making other expenditures less affordable for people in poverty. Covid-19 created lock downs and supply chain issues that contributed to unemployment in some markets and harmful shipping delays for OGS companies.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Targeted technical assistance may support legal evaluation to understand risks. Political risk insurance can be a valuable tool to hedge some of these risks. The team has conducted multiple political risk studies on H2R markets. The team intends to create political risk assessments for each market. Macro risks for each investment will be summarized in investment memos and will be shared with the Investment Committee along with strategies to address the risk aligned to the context of the specific investment. It's also worth mentioning that H2R will have a broad portfolio, diversifying risk across a range of geographies.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 150

## Description
Energy companies that feel under pressure to repay loans and show growth may opt to sell more to higher-income segments, negating some or all of the company’s proposed additional impact.

## Mitigation Measure(s)
The use of impact-linked grants and loans, which will incorporate a measure of the depth of poverty of a customer base, will be used to incentivize companies to target lower-income segments. The impact-indexed loans will have KPIs to ensure customer retention is achieved responsibly and sustainably. Additionally, Acumen will conduct thorough due diligence on each Hardest-to-Reach investment to ensure that companies are capable of properly utilizing H2R funds. The IC will be instructed on ensuring that the mission is a core component of each investment. Term sheets will stipulate the use of funds for each loan or equity investment.

## Selected Risk Factor 4: Distortion

### Category
Probability: Medium  
Impact: Low  

### Description
The act of choosing a subset of companies to fund, some of which may operate in existing sub-Saharan African markets, could distort the future market for off-grid solar, and crowd out less well-connected companies. Relatedly, the volume of concessionary debt going towards energy companies could distort the overall energy access debt market.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
While there are existing companies operating in Hardest-to-Reach markets, there are not many of them, and few if any that have reached a significant scale serving the most challenging reaches of the country. By incentivizing companies to reach first-time customers, H2R is creating and nurturing new markets within weak-grid and off-grid areas, thus the risk of distorting an existing market remains low. As evidence from ODI suggests, the risk of crowding out other possible (private) sources of finance is less relevant in low-income countries where financial markets are insufficiently developed, and investors are unlikely to have access to international capital markets at affordable rates in the absence of the concessional intervention. In addition, the concessionality provided to the companies is linked to the impacts achieved in terms of first time energy access and poverty level of customers, which will likely ensure that the benefits of concessionality pass down to the low income, first time energy access customers (in terms of increased supply of entry-level products) and minimize a potential for market distortion.

## Selected Risk Factor 5: Continuity

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 151

## Category: Probability Impact
- **Operational and Technical**: Medium High

## Description
When the program ends, the end of concessionary financing could mean that firms find Hardest-to-Reach markets unsustainable to operate in or cease serving low-income segments within those markets.

## Mitigation Measure(s)
Hardest-to-Reach is an integrated life-cycle financing to accompany companies to expand into new, challenging markets and to support their sustainable growth over the longer term through supportive patient capital and line of sight working capital financing which is linked to impact outcomes, which will contribute to building robust markets in Hardest-to- Reach geographies. Over time, the success of H2R investees can derisk these previously untested markets while also demonstrating the success pathway - including through the use of blended finance. Combined this should make it easier for these companies to access financing.

In addition, H2R seeks to address the viability gaps faced by companies in terms of capacity, strategy and enabling environment. Over the life of the two vehicles, two pools of technical assistance capital will support ecosystem development to ensure that, through training, local capacity building, and stakeholder coordination efforts - among other things - the sustainability of the intervention is enhanced and impacts beyond the life of the fund continue in a self-sustaining way.

## Selected Risk Factor 6: Due Diligence

### Category: Probability Impact
- **Governance**: Low Medium

### Description
Operating in certain countries and through certain on-lending structures (particularly onto smaller distributors) poses a DD challenge, Where good market information is scarce, this increases the complexity of conducting satisfactory due diligence and thus the risk of financing operationally, financially and/or reputationally unsuitable companies.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
In addition to careful due diligence and investment structuring, in-depth market studies inform the H2R strategy at the country-level, as well as uncover macro risks. In the case of distributor finance via manufacturers, the investment team will work closely with the manufacturer borrowers/on-lenders to screen-out distributors that are not suitable for this facility for financial or ESG reasons.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 152

## Category: Probability Impact
### Forex
- **Medium** Medium

## Description
Currency or Foreign Exchange risk (FX Risk) is a key inhibitor of capital inflows and deal progress in the off-grid energy sector, particularly in least developed countries such as the ones that Hardest-to-Reach aims to impact. Local companies face challenges when receiving loans in 'hard currency' while their operations and sales are managed with local currency. Significant depreciation in local currency value could make it very challenging for distributors to service their H2R debt.

## Mitigation Measure(s)
The due diligence process will incorporate an assessment of country risk, including foreign exchange and hard currency liquidity risk. Acumen is exploring the feasibility of local currency lending alongside hedging partners.

---

## Category: Probability Impact
### Reputational
- **Low** Medium

## Description
Raw materials for solar products and some solar products may come from geographies currently under international scrutiny for forced labor practices. Several solar product manufacturers also operate in areas under international scrutiny for forced labor practices. It is also estimated that there are 3.7 million forced laborers in sub-Saharan Africa, largely in the informal economy (particularly in informal, artisanal cobalt mining industries within several H2R target countries). There is therefore some risk of forced labor in the value chain.

## Mitigation Measure(s)
Acumen will include forced labor in its ESMS and will integrate it into its company due diligence, including requesting disclosure of suppliers from potential portfolio companies, and conducting ongoing monitoring. Acumen will mitigate forced labor through the use of screening tools, in its deal terms and as part of its monitoring and evaluation process.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 153

## Governance Medium Low

### Description
Some companies that receive H2R investment are early-stage startups, and may have inefficient management or governance structure which may result in ineffective decision making and the funds allowed to them may not be utilized properly. This risk could materialize due to separation of key executives or change in group share-holding structures. The materialization of this risk could result in ineffective decision making at company’s level and the company might not achieve desired outcome from the availed funds which will ultimately impact on their repayments. Projected H2R impact, including GHG emissions, households accessing renewable energy, and retained employment, may fail to materialize. This failure could result from a number of factors, including, but not limited to, ongoing effects of the COVID-19 pandemic, customers’ inability to pay, and/or mismanagement of companies receiving H2R investments.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The facility manager has a well-designed credit due diligence process which involves detailed review of ownership, governance, management and board structure/effectiveness along-with interviews of senior management. In case any weakness is identified, at the assessment level the borrowers are advised to resolve these issues appropriately along-with these strong covenants are also incorporated to improve the governance structure. Furthermore, any major change in ownership structure is also restricted in the legal documentation. The screening process described in the ESMS is meant to ensure that H2R investments are directed to companies with a viable, impactful business model. ## Mitigation Measure(s)

The Hardest-to-Reach team has conducted extensive market and political risk research across H2R markets. The team has developed a strong level of knowledge of country-specific risks across H2R markets.

As a result of this research, Acumen has developed a project-specific AML/KYC plan that details the AML/KYC policies and procedure.

Each potential investee that has been IC approved is expected to undergo a thorough background check. The diligence team will also seek to diligence key persons including their board, investors, senior management, founders, and the C-suite. If any company is linked to money laundering or financing of terrorism then they will not receive H2R funds.

In the DRC and Somalia, prior to investment, the H2R team will check UN Sanctions Lists, Terrorist Exclusion Lists, The Office of Foreign Assets Control watchlist, and others to ensure that no key persons are on the +100 watchlists. If people are identified on the lists, H2R will not proceed with the investment.

## Selected Risk Factor 11: Excluded Activities

### Category: Probability Impact

- **Prohibited practices**: Low Medium

### Description

Hardest-to-Reach is reaching new markets and investing in early-stage companies with limited track records who may engage in prohibited practices. If regulatory entities or investors find that a company engages in prohibited practices, the company or individual could face criminal charges, shutting down of operations, or blockage of funds from investors. If H2R is found to invest in such a company, the materialization of this risk can have serious consequences such as blockage of funds from investors, regulatory or criminal proceedings against the funds/investors, sanctions and may also cause monetary losses.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Hardest-to-Reach has developed an Exclusion List to screen out potential Investees engaging in any prohibited practices. Potential Investees will be required to declare that they do not engage in any activity, do not fund any activity, and do not support any activity on the Exclusion List. If a company does engage in activity on the Exclusion List then they cannot receive H2R financing. H2R investees will also agree to a binding legal agreement that prohibits engagement in Exclusion List activities.

- OGS investment

  - Pattern of gender or SEAH incidents
  - Pattern of reversible environmental and social impacts

- OGS investment
- Investment smaller than $5M
- Due diligence finds the following:
  - Funds used for working capital, accounts receivable, operational expenditures
  - Minimal environmental and social impacts
  - May not have sufficient policies and procedures

## Engagement with stakeholders throughout project development and implementation

The H2R project team also utilized extensive desktop research to understand specific risks for the project, investees, the country context, and relevant mitigants.

## Key Project Level Environmental and Social Risks

The project has four primary environmental and social risk concerns:

### E-waste

While there are many strong environmental benefits to Hardest-To-Reach, the project team recognizes both environmental and climate risks for the project. One of Hardest-To-Reach's primary environmental concerns is solar e-waste. Off-grid businesses will be selling millions of products to first time buyers in the H2R markets. These markets have weak and nascent waste management sectors. Moreover, many African countries have weak regulatory requirements for e-waste. Last mile distributors may have weak or immature e-waste policies, procedures, and procurement. Moreover, the e-waste markets in Hardest-to-Reach countries are in a nascent stage with few players and little coverage.

### Consumer Protection

There are two primary social risks for Hardest-To-Reach. First, pay-as-you-go (PAYGo), products sold on credit, create risks for customers who can face issues such as over-indebtedness, poor credit, and defunct products if they fall behind on their payments. PAYGo also allows many customers who could not afford to pay for a solar home system up front the opportunity to access electricity with smaller recurring payments.

Additionally, the team has concerns around consumer privacy and protections for PAYGo and other solar home system customers. While many companies are now GOGLA consumer protection signatories, customers still face the risk of customer data being compromised, improperly stored, stolen, or sold to malicious third parties. Infringing on customer data protections may open off-grid solar customers to stolen identity, fraud, and other issues surrounding identity theft.

### Forced Labor

Second, the H2R management team is concerned about forced labor in the solar supply chain. Human rights investigators have found evidence of forced labor in some regions where there are solar manufacturers. Advocacy groups warn that marginalized people may be facing mass detention in certain regions.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 160

## OGS Company ESG Readiness

The project team expects that Hardest-to-Reach will invest in companies with varying degrees of ESG readiness. Larger and more established off-grid solar companies are expected to demonstrate maturity across a number of ESG issues. Several larger pipeline companies have indicated that they have strong ESMSs, consumer protection policies, and other environmental and social mitigants in place. Acumen’s experience has demonstrated that early-stage SHS companies, especially last mile distributors, may not have robust ESG policies and procedures, nor would they have strong organizational capacity to mitigate ESG risks. These companies have a small footprint so their risks are marginal to the project success and impact on beneficiaries.

The Hardest-to-Reach ESMS includes the ESIA, which has a more extensive report on project and company level environmental and social risks and impacts.

## Environmental and Social Risk Mitigants

To ensure the Hardest-to-Reach project achieves its mission and impact, the team has designed a thorough ESMS aligned with GCF Policy, Acumen Policy, and industry best practices.

The ESMS shares the project development, management, and monitoring and reporting policies and procedures. All investments will be screened against the project Exclusion List before being accepted for due diligence.

The project team will diligence companies with a variety of ESG diligence tools including base-line questionnaires, site visits, company evidence in the form of policies and procedures, desktop research, a climate resilience assessment tool, and supplementary questionnaires.

The ESG findings will be memorialized in an ESG due diligence report and the investment memo. The Investment Committee is expected to consider ESG factors in their deliberations. The ESG due diligence report will also provide the information needed for developing the term sheet binding, time bound, ESG Action Plan.

The project will offer Portfolio Companies technical assistance tailored to supporting company-specific ESG, consumer protection, gender, and business readiness needs. This will include: needs diagnostics, webinars, direct support, and policy and procedure guidance and resources.

# G  
## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 162  

Acumen has conducted a thorough and robust gender assessment for the Hardest-to-Reach project aligned with the objectives of GCF’s Gender Policy, Acumen’s Gender Sensitivity Policy for GCF-funded projects. The gender assessment informed the development of the Hardest-to-Reach Gender Lens Investing Strategy and the Hardest-to-Reach Gender Action Plan. The H2R gender assessment and GAP are detailed in Annex 8.

As part of a H2R pilot project, Acumen hired a lead consumer impact surveying business to survey beneficiaries of the pilot project. The H2R team will need to learn from more beneficiaries, but the initial findings demonstrated similar impacts for men and women. Both groups felt the need and importance of using SHS products to attain electricity.

The team also conducted extensive stakeholder engagement across the Hardest-to-Reach markets including speaking with companies, government entities, industry associations, NGOs, and civil society organizations. The H2R team spoke with local gender experts and civil society organizations from multiple H2R markets on local gender challenges and opportunities that informed our gender lens investing strategy and gender work.

The H2R team worked to do project-level gender assessment that included 14 country assessments. The team generated two country assessments for the remaining Hardest-to-Reach countries. The team relied on desktop research, industry experts, gender experts, OGS companies, and survey data to inform the gender assessments, GLI strategy, and Gender Action Plan.

## Findings  

### Barriers to gender equality in OGS sector  

The link between energy access and gender equality is well recognized. Energy poverty has a strong gender dimension as the lack of access to energy inhibits women’s well-being, and their access to economic opportunities, and it strongly affects women’s living conditions and time-use.

One of the key barriers in the market relates to lack of sex-disaggregated data for the OGS sector both at the consumer and workforce levels.

Gender and social norms limit women’s access to technologies and their abilities to enter the workforce.

Companies have a limited understanding of the business case for gender inclusion in their businesses, and of how to make their practices more inclusive. Several companies we spoke with shared that they are beginning to build institutional capacity on gender and want to build inclusive policies and procedures into their operations.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.0 | PAGE 163

## Serving women with OGS technologies

Overall, evidence shows that women have less access to off-grid electricity than their male counterparts, and they mostly use pico-solar products and lanterns or other lower-tier technologies.

An analysis conducted by Value for Women on 59 companies working in the energy sector found that only 23% of companies collect sex-disaggregated data on customers, and half of them reported that they serve 46% of customers who are women.

In H2R country contexts, prevalent gender norms are likely to affect access to off-grid solar technologies, influencing companies’ abilities to reach women. Discriminatory gender norms inhibit women’s rights to own land and other assets, making it challenging for women to access credit and purchase renewable energy technologies. PAYGo companies may reach a lower percentage of women customers due to additional barriers related to mobile ownership and mobile money usage among women.

## Women's participation in OGS companies' workforce

ESMAP and GOGLA recently surveyed 37 OGS companies from 20 different countries and found that on average women constitute 41.7% of workers across all levels. In contrast, a previous GOGLA study indicated that only 27% of women work in the OGS sector.

An analysis completed by Value for Women found that on average women represent 33% of the workforce of energy companies.

The country assessments indicate that several barriers limit women's participation in the workforce and in the economy more generally, with prevailing gender norms, gaps in education levels and discriminatory policies and practices affecting women's productive activities and economic empowerment in several countries.

## Hardest-to-Reach Gender Work

Hardest-to-Reach seeks to build OGS markets with a gender lens, and strong commitment to gender equity. H2R is also committed to supporting companies to build their gender capacity to support their operations and their customers. Additionally, H2R is committed to mitigating risks of sexual harassment, abuse, and exploitation across the project, investees, and beneficiaries.

All companies will undergo a gender assessment to determine their gender strengths and weaknesses. Opportunities for growth will be captured in a binding Gender Action Plan. All companies in the H2R portfolio will have to create a GAP that will be monitored over the company engagement with H2R. Companies must commit to strong anti-sexual harassment policies, non-discrimination policies, and their best effort to collect sex-disaggregated data.

## 1.2. Recording and Processing

Accounts payable will be reviewed and processed at the end of each week. Each invoice is submitted to the Program’s accounting platform for review and approval and must have an associated vendor record in the system. Before approving, the appropriate finance employee will review all invoices, wires, checks and account coding.

## 1.3. Reconciling

The Program reconciles all bank, petty cash, and investment accounts monthly, as follows:

### a. Once available, the Finance Associate (or designate) reviews all relevant bank statements

### b. The Finance Associate (or designate) will complete the reconciliation within seven days

### c. The following is the general outline to be used to reconcile accounts:

#### i) Comparison of bank statements and cash receipts, verifying dates and totals deposited.

#### ii) Comparison of bank and/or wire transfer dates received against dates sent.

## 1.4. Vendor Selection

Vendor selection is dependent on:

### i) The results of a standardized background check. Each vendor and its related entities and owners are subject to Patriot Act searches. Each search reviews international sanctions lists, enforcement sources (i.e., civil, criminal offenses, indictments, and convictions), and financial history (i.e., foreclosures, bankruptcies, etc.).

### ii) The different investing strategies the the Program will implore (Market Support Facility and Market Expansion Facility), and requisite sectoral expertise and operational infrastructure necessary for the distinct approaches.

## 1.5. Procurement

The Program’s Market Support Facility (MSF) will seek to engage service providers with track records of delivery in alignment with the above policies and the AE’s procurement standards. Service providers must demonstrate capacity and execute around the scope of work developed by the MSF team and shared with the AE for review, where relevant. All procurement decisions will be reviewed by the MSF Director.

The Program’s Market Expansion Facility (MEF) will seek to engage service providers with track records of delivery in alignment with the above policies and the AE’s procurement standards. Service providers must demonstrate capacity and execute around the scope of work developed by the MEF team and shared with the AE for review, where relevant. All procurement decisions will be reviewed by the MEF Director.

### iv) When required by investors, the Program may publish an RFP for a specific project.

## 1.6. Procurement Standards

Service providers must demonstrate capacity and execute around the scope of work developed by the MSF team and shared with the AE for review, where relevant. All procurement decisions will be reviewed by the MSF Director.

The Program’s Market Support Facility (MSF) will focus on early-stage investments, employing equity, equity-like, impact index loans, grants, and joint venture financing options. Launched in January 2023, the MSF will run through to 2033. Although negotiations remain on-going, the Program has preliminarily selected both a local market advisor and a separate small-ticket debt advisor. In each case, the firms distinguished themselves from other providers through their unique value (i.e., their extensive experience in both debt and equity lending to early stage off-grid energy companies across the Program’s target geographies).

Conversely, the Program’s Market Expansion Facility (MEF) has both a separate investment vehicle structure, strategy, and instrument size. The MEF will target later stage, more established companies with larger ticket sizes, utilizing impact index loans (working capital), and receivables financing, and will be structured as a pooled investment vehicle. With an expected launch in January 2024, the MEF will run through to 2034. ## G.4. Disclosure of funding proposal

Note: The Information Disclosure Policy (IDP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all information and documents relating to the GCF and its funding activities. Under the IDP, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website, simultaneous with the submission to the Board, subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the IDP. Information provided in confidence is one of the exceptions, but this exception should not be applied broadly to an entire document if the document contains specific, segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm.

Indicate below whether or not the funding proposal includes confidential information.

- ☐ No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.
- X With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence.

Accordingly, the accredited entity is providing to the Secretariat the following two copies of the funding proposal, including all annexes:

- Full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity’s disclosure policy, and
- Redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, if containing confidential information.

# To: The Green Climate Fund (“GCF”)

## Kinshasa, 18 October 2022

### Re: Funding proposal for the GCF by Acumen regarding Hardest-to-Reach

Dear Mr. Yannick Glemarec,

We refer to the programme titled "Hardest-to-Reach" in Angola, Benin, Burkina Faso, Burundi, Central African Republic, Chad, Democratic Republic of the Congo, Guinea, Guinea-Bissau, Lesotho, Liberia, Madagascar, Malawi, Mozambique, Niger, Sierra Leone, Somalia, South Sudan, Togo, Uganda, United Republic of Tanzania, and Zambia as included in the funding proposal submitted by Acumen to us on 18 October 2022.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the National Designated Authority of the Democratic Republic of the Congo.

Pursuant to GCF decision B.08/10, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no-objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no-objection, it is implied that:

(a) The government of the Democratic Republic of the Congo has no-objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal;  
(b) The programme as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the national priorities, strategies, and plans of the Democratic Republic of the Congo;  
(c) In accordance with the GCF’s environmental and social safeguards, the programme as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no-objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

Page 178

Kind regards,

Dr. Hans André Lohayo Djamba, Ph.D.  
National Designated Authority  
Democratic Republic of the Congo

# [Page 1]

EPA-SL, Protecting our environment for a better future, website: www.epa.gov.sl, email: info@epa.gov.sl  

To: The Green Climate Fund (“GCF”)  

Freetown, 13 March 2023  

Re: Funding proposal for the GCF by Acumen regarding Hardest-to-Reach  

Dear Madam, Sir,  

We refer to the programme titled “Hardest-to-Reach” in key Sub-Saharan African countries including Sierra Leone, as included in the funding proposal submitted by Acumen to us on 13 March 2023.  

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the Environment Protection Agency, the National Designated Authority of Sierra Leone.  

Pursuant to GCF decision B.08/10, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no-objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal.  

By communicating our no-objection, it is implied that:  

(a) The government of Sierra Leone has no-objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal;  
(b) The programme as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the national priorities, strategies and plans of Sierra Leone;  
(c) In accordance with the GCF’s environmental and social safeguards, the programme as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.  

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no-objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.  

We also confirm that our no-objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of the programme  

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.  

Kind regards,  

____________________________  
Dr. Bondi Gevao  
National Designated Authority  
Environment Protection Agency  
Sierra Leone  

GOVERNMENT OF SIERRA LEONE  
Environment Protection Agency  
Ministry of the Environment  
92 Dundas Street, Freetown

**The Green Climate Fund ("GCF")**  
Lomé, 30 January 2023  

**Re: Funding proposal for the GCF by Acumen regarding Hardest-to-Reach**  

Dear Madam, Sir,  

We refer to the programme titled "Hardest-to-Reach" in Angola, Benin, Burkina Faso, Burundi, Central African Republic, Chad, Democratic Republic of the Congo, Guinea, Guinea-Bissau, Lesotho, Liberia, Madagascar, Malawi, Mozambique, Niger, Sierra Leone, Somalia, South Sudan, Togo, Uganda, United Republic of Tanzania, and Zambia as included in the funding proposal submitted by Acumen to us on 30 January 2023.  

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the Directorate of Environment, Ministry of Environment, the National Designated Authority of Togo.  

Pursuant to GCF decision 8.08/10, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no-objection to the programme as included in the funding proposal.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.06  
## Page 197  

### Independent Technical Advisory Panel’s assessment of  
#### FP211  
##### Proposal name: Hardest-to-Reach  
###### Accredited entity: Acumen Fund  
####### Project/programme size: Medium  

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel  

### 1.1 Overview  

1. The Hardest-to-Reach (H2R) programme is designed to supply off-grid solar (OGS) energy to low-income populations in 16 least developed countries (LDCs) in sub-Saharan Africa. It aims to develop an OGS ecosystem in countries with low electrification and high poverty rates. The initiative supplies capital to OGS companies entering and expanding in these nascent markets. Although the OGS market is thriving in many parts of Africa, there is reluctance among companies to enter LDC markets and to engage with the poorest. These markets are considered too risky by OGS companies, which prefer to focus their limited capacities on more profitable and low-risk markets. The programme seeks to deliver clean first-time electricity access to over 54 million beneficiaries with high rates of poverty, where investors have not been willing to enter at sufficient scale to meet the energy access needs of underserved segments.  

2. The USD 250 million programme plans to establish two separate financing facilities with different investment risk profiles. This is designed to help OGS companies expand in H2R countries and extend their reach. The USD 50 million Market Support Facility (MSF) shall be grant funded by philanthropies and donors and includes a USD 10 million reimbursable grant from GCF. The MSF aims to support and improve the capacity of local players and regional companies for market entry through grants, equity, debt, and technical assistance.  

For the USD 200 million Market Expansion Facility (MEF), Acumen intends to raise USD 145 million across commercial investors and development finance institutions (DFIs), anchored by a USD 50 million junior equity tranche and a USD 5 million non-repayable grant capital from GCF. The MEF aims to provide impact-linked concessionary loans to scale up companies’ operations and settle in H2R countries. Interest rates shall be adjusted based on results achieved in terms of first-time energy access and poverty level of the customers.  

The USD 65 million concessional financing from GCF in the form of grant and junior equity is expected to mobilize private finance to support OGS companies and build a sustainable OGS ecosystem in the challenging markets for just energy systems transitions.  

### 1.2 Impact potential              Scale: High  

3. The programme has both mitigation and adaptation impacts. The mitigation impact is estimated at 5.1 megatonne of carbon dioxide equivalent (Mt CO2 eq) reduced over the lifespan of the programme and, as adaptation outcome, the project expects to reach 54 million beneficiaries of which 9 million are direct beneficiaries.  

4. The mitigation outcome is achieved by providing OGS products to people to achieve clean energy access (tier 1+). 120 MW of decentralized OGS energy generation capacity shall be newly installed. Currently, the majority of rural households rely on kerosene and biomass as  

---  
**Note:** This text appears to continue from page 197 but ends abruptly without completing the sentence about rural households relying on kerosene and biomass. The full content may be available in subsequent pages or sections.
