# GCF/B.36/02/Add.06  
## Page 198  

Their primary energy sources for lighting, cooking, heating, etc. In the baseline scenario, a large portion of households do not have access to electricity due to lack of grids and low-income levels, which limit a household’s ability to pay for electricity. The programme reduces the need to rely on fossil fuels and biomass by replacing them with solar energy.

5.  The greenhouse gas (GHG) mitigation impact was calculated using the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change registered clean development mechanism methodologies AMS-III.AR. (“Substituting fossil fuel-based lighting with LED/CFL lighting systems”) and AMS-I.L. (“Electrification of rural communities using renewable energy”). Assumptions for distribution of funds between solar home systems and lanterns, the number of units to be financed per area, as well as the expected lifetime of products are presented by the programme in a transparent manner and are considered to be reasonable. Black carbon emissions have not been included by the programme in the calculations, which is conservative especially as kerosene lamps have high potential black carbon emissions. The achievement of the expected mitigation outcome is reliant on reaching the expected fund size and product placement. Overall, the projected GHG emission reductions are deemed to have been calculated in a conservative manner.

6.  The expected adaptation outcome is 54 million direct and indirect beneficiaries. Indirect beneficiaries are calculated by multiplying OGS sales by factor 5, which corresponds to the average number of people per household in H2R countries. The 54 million beneficiaries are people with first-time access to electricity. Rural electricity access in programme countries is 11 per cent on average.

7.  The increase in resilience to climate change through the programme is based on the following expected outcomes:  
(i) Most vulnerable people and communities would still have access to energy in the aftermath of displacement-inducing natural disasters caused by climate change due to owning OGS products. It was calculated that 1.6 million people could benefit from enhanced energy security through the programme based on the expected number of people to be displaced due to climate hazards within the total projected number of H2R beneficiaries;  
(ii) Health, well-being, food and water security could be enhanced as OGS customers would be able to more securely access information including extreme weather event and health warnings, health information, and market data, such as for agricultural produce. OGS products linked with additional devices, such as radios or mobile phones, allow access to climate-related information. Seventy-five per cent of the total 72 million beneficiaries (of which 54 million are beneficiaries with first-time electricity access and therefore counted as beneficiaries in the adaptation outcome) are expected to access electricity power for the first time with an OGS product. This results in a total of 54 million beneficiaries with improved access to information; and  
(iii) Most vulnerable people and communities benefit from increased and/or diversified income sources. Energy access can contribute to increasing household income by increasing working hours through access to light and increasing opportunities for home-based business. A 60 Decibels energy benchmark study revealed that 10 per cent of OGS clients could diversify and increase their income base. Based on the 54 million first-time customers of OGS, this results in 5.4 million direct beneficiaries profiting from increased and more diversified income sources. The total number of 54 million beneficiaries is based on the maximum number of beneficiaries of the three aforementioned impact areas. The programme thus has a potential impact on increasing the climate resilience of around 15 per cent of the total population in the participating countries.

8.  The three core adaptation outcomes of the programme, while expected to be significant, may be challenging to measure or report to GCF. While the programme’s logical framework includes “User surveys to determine beneficiaries with improved and/or new climate-resilient livelihood options”, no mention is made of tracking enhanced access to climate-relevant information.  

1 60 Decibels. (2020). Why Off-Grid Energy Matters.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.06  
## Page 199  

### Information through OGS-powered communication media  
In terms of the assumptions made that the OGS products installed will provide resilient energy access, through durability in the face of extreme events and portability in the case of displacement, no mechanism has been built in to monitor performance of the OGS products.  

### Overall, the independent Technical Advisory Panel (iTAP) considers that the programme has a high adaptation and medium to high mitigation impact. Cumulatively, the impact potential is considered to be high.  

#### 1.3 Paradigm shift potential     Scale: Medium  

### The programme promotes the entrance of companies in the African energy sector into off-grid markets, where investors have not been willing to enter up to the present.  
The blended finance structure has the ability to crowd in investors that would otherwise find the markets too nascent or risky through initiatives to de-risk these markets by providing new market entrants with impact-linked concessional finance and targeted technical assistance.  

### Last-mile distributors have a crucial role to play in delivering OGS products, but capital is currently not flowing into the space.  
The programme can thus achieve a transformational impact in reaching clients and markets currently only marginally covered by private sector approaches.  

### Companies that thrive in these risky and nascent markets would be willing to increase their business in these markets, thus resulting in positive multiplication effects.  
The programme supports companies to build solid operational platforms in challenging markets, and to grow in such markets, thereby scaling up their ventures and providing clean energy to a larger population of unelectrified people.  

### Acumen intends to share the knowledge and evidence-based insights gained through the programme.  
This can result in increased capital inflows and additional investments on the ground. The programme’s demonstration of linking energy access and adaptation, as well as showcasing that companies can also thrive in such difficult surroundings, should also unlock funding for OGS ecosystems in more difficult markets.  

### Demonstrating the viability of challenging markets can unlock other investments from broader sources of capital, thereby sustaining the growth of these markets in the long term and adding to a replication effect.  

### Scaling up the programme would depend to a large extent on the sustainability of market efforts.  
Experiences with COVID-19 have shown that OGS companies struggled with high levels of default among their customers.  

### The Acumen project Participation in Energy Access Relief Facility (FP148) outlined that energy access companies struggled with dramatically reduced sales and collections, coupled with disrupted supply chains, limited cash reserves, and tightening funding sources, which are crippling companies’ ability to fund operations.  
This shows the fragile base of most companies in this market. While COVID-19 in conjunction with other external factors can be considered extraordinary, the exposure of OGS companies to external shocks remains high and the risk of defaults from their customers permanent.  

### The targeted poorer segments of society are especially exposed to numerous external shocks, including climate-related ones, which can negatively affect their payment ability.  
For people who are close to or below the poverty threshold, the payments for OGS equipment might be one of the first to discontinue in case of existential needs.  

### The experience of many development activities is that successful interventions do not replicate automatically.  
On-ground demonstration of successful intervention is a positive first step but not sufficient for a high level of replication and transformational change.  

### The programme does require activities to include all relevant stakeholders and to actively manage and spread the knowledge generated.  
This might not coincide with the goal of involved private enterprises to avoid inflow of competition.  

### Overall, the planned programme activities in the area of knowledge management and outreach are not considered to be comprehensive enough.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.06  
Page 200  

## 15. The programme’s focus is on working with private enterprises to make commercial ventures in this market successful. The programme recognizes the importance of creating an ecospace that is positive towards OGS, reducing barriers and creating incentives. This is largely related to government policies and activities. While the programme does relate with the involved governments, there is no clear and comprehensive engagement plan with the national governments to improve the framework conditions.

## 16. Overall, iTAP considers that the programme has the potential to increase the appetite of private companies and capital to also invest in more challenging markets and thus create a paradigm shift. To have an even larger transformational impact, additional measures in the realm of outreach, capacity-building, knowledge management, and stronger engagement with governments will be required. However, this is not necessarily the core expertise of the parties involved in the programme and it is highly probable that positive programme experiences would be taken up by the international development community to “spread the word”. The paradigm shift potential is considered to be medium.

### 1.4 Sustainable development potential            Scale: Medium to High

## 17. The programme is aligned with Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 7 (affordable, reliable, modern and sustainable energy for all), SDG 3 (good health and well-being), SDG 4 (improved educational outcomes), SDGs 1 and 8 (economic savings and income generation), and SDG 13 (climate action).

## 18. One factor that could limit sustainable development impacts, or even cause negative impacts, is the local currency rate risk from MEF on-lending to OGS companies. The iTAP is concerned about the financial sustainability of an MEF USD loan both from an OGS company and an end-customer perspective. In the funding proposal, it is written that “Acumen is exploring the feasibility of local currency lending alongside hedging partners” but, in discussions with iTAP, Acumen wrote that “In the meantime, the H2R MEF would make loans in USD. The target borrowers for H2R MEF would be expected to be sophisticated OGS companies with multinational operations, aware of the impact of currency fluctuations on their borrowings.” This essentially means that the OGS companies will pass those currency risks to the ultimate hardest-to-reach consumers. Given current volatility in currency markets, such a pass-on can potentially have devastating impact on the ability of those consumers to pay. Alternatively, if the OGS companies are “forced” to keep that risk on their own balance sheet, it may jeopardize the companies' financial viability. For the very sophisticated OGS companies who truly understand the local currency risks in some of these markets, the MEF funding may not be even worth it.

## 19. In terms of benefits, OGS products can have positive impacts on people’s health and safety. Traditional kerosene lanterns are harmful to people’s respiratory health because of household pollutants, and can cause burns and fires. Indoor air pollution from kerosene lanterns is related to particulate matter and carbon monoxide and disproportionately affects women and children, who spend more time at home. According to a 60 Decibels study conducted in 2020, 48 per cent of OGS customers reported health improvements after acquiring OGS products.

## 20. OGS systems can potentially help to preserve ecosystems and reduce deforestation by substituting unsustainable biomass harvesting for energy usage with OGS systems.

> 60 Decibels. (2020). *Why Off-Grid Energy Matters.*

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.06  
## Page 201  

### 21. OGS products can enable people to generate income through their use, either directly (e.g. expanding business hours or offering new services such as cell phone charging) or indirectly (e.g. shifting tasks or activities to the evening because of better quality light). In the case of biomass harvesting used in absence of OGS systems, it can also reduce workloads, primarily of women and children, allowing more time to be dedicated to other activities. The programme can also contribute towards increased financial inclusion and access to the formal banking system through usage of the PAYGo financing structure to provide OGS products, allowing people to build their credit histories.

### 22. The programme targets the creation of around 14,000 additional green jobs as a co-benefit. An estimated 4,600 of these jobs could be a source of employment for women. However, at least some of these jobs might not be additional ones but substitutes for jobs held before (e.g. vendors of kerosene lanterns).

### 23. The programme will target the industry’s benchmark of having 32 per cent of its customers as women and will aim to support women-led businesses. The programme also intends to engage companies and the OGS ecosystem in developing more opportunities for women to work. The programme will track gender-disaggregated data for multiple indicators, allowing for the generation of knowledge on the engagement of women with energy products and services useful throughout the OGS industry.

### 24. Overall, the sustainable development potential of the programme is considered medium to high. It can make critical steps to improve the livelihoods of the poorest in a sustainable manner. Tackling the currency risk could strongly improve the sustainability potential of the programme.

### 1.5 Needs of the recipient      Scale: High  

### 25. Across the 16 African countries included, there are around 54 million beneficiaries that do not have access to modern electricity services – representing roughly just under 10 per cent of the total unelectrified population of sub-Saharan Africa. On average, 78 per cent of the population in these countries lives below the lower-middle-income poverty line. They are residing in acutely climate vulnerable contexts in terms of worsening heat stress, more frequent droughts, more intense floods, and extreme weather events. Nearly half of the bottom twenty countries listed in the Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative (ND-GAIN) climate vulnerability index3 are programme countries. Regional vulnerability to the increasing climate hazards is exacerbated by non-climate drivers such as high poverty levels, weak infrastructure systems, lack of emergency response capabilities, and fragile grid electricity and telecommunications networks.

### 26. At country and company level, limited investment can currently be attracted to the market targeted by the programme. Eighty per cent of the capital raised by OGS companies between 2012–2019 has flowed to larger and more established, first-generation companies whose primary location and focus is East Africa, with Kenya attracting the lion’s share of the capital. OGS start-ups also focused on entrenching their market position and attracting higher margin customers, leaving out the rural, poorer markets of LDCs with a higher actual and perceived risk.

### 35.  The USD 250 million programme (for the MSF and the MEF) targets the provision of entry level electricity access to 72 million people (of which 54 million access for the first-time electricity) at a cost of around USD 3.5 per person, which is considered to be low-cost.  

### 36.  The estimated GCF cost per t CO2 eq avoided, considering the 50 per cent assigned GCF financing to mitigation, is around USD 6, which is considered low. The GCF funding generates significant adaptation benefits by providing first-time energy access and strengthening the climate resilience of 54 million people.  

### 37.  Acumen has successful and broad experience with similar initiatives, also with the GCF (i.e. KawiSafi Ventures Fund FP005 and Participation in Energy Access Relief Facility FP148). The capacity of Acumen to set up, oversee and manage such funds is deemed as high. The MSF funds shall be transferred to a newly formed limited liability company, which will be a pass-through entity wholly owned by the accredited entity (AE). The MEF is expected to be formed as a general partnership with the AE which is expected to indirectly wholly own the general partner.   

### 38.  The overall iTAP assessment concerning efficiency and effectiveness is high as the programme can achieve significant mitigation and adaptation impact at a low cost while crowding in potentially some, albeit limited, additional private finance.  

## II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel  

### 39.  Based on the assessment above, iTAP endorses this funding proposal.  

### 40.  The iTAP notes that the programme is entering a potentially high-risk market and that, in the learning stage, various ventures will probably fail. However, the approach proposed, and the measures taken, as well as the experience of the AE in this type of venture, ensure that risks are managed well while still striving to reach people underserved by traditional private sector approaches. The role of GCF is to provide risk finance to push the borders beyond business as usual and to reach people not covered by traditional approaches through an effective approach with a high impact and replication potential. Therefore, the programme ticks all the boxes of the investment criteria set forward by GCF.   

### 41.  The iTAP recommends that the AE strengthen the logical framework to be able to track progress towards adaptation outcomes 3 and 4. The user surveys planned to track the number of beneficiaries with enhanced climate-resilient livelihood options (outcome 5) can also include questions to track enhanced access to climate-relevant information, or to communication media as a proxy for that (outcome 4), as well as the durability/portability of the OGS systems in the face of any extreme events experienced (outcome 3).  

### 42.  The iTAP recommends that the GCF concessionary capital be utilized to take local currency basket options or other hedging products at the MEF fund level rather than push such a risk onto OGS companies, or worse, onto end customers. The hedging products may be expensive for the MEF, but they will certainly be expensive for the OGS companies and their end customers who will bear that volatility (which must be borne by someone). Such a hedge may ultimately also address concerns from potential limited partners on the financial sustainability of MEF on-lending. Either way, the main goal of blended finance is to crowd in commercial capital, but it is equally important to utilize concessional capital to pay for “insurance” products, which minimize risk for the companies and customers operating in hardest-to-reach markets.

# GCF/B.36/02/Add.06  
## Page 204  

### Reply to the Independent Technical Advisory Panel assessment findings  

#### Proposal name: Hardest-to-Reach  
#### Accredited entity: Acumen Fund  

### Impact potential  

Acumen is pleased to note that the impact potential is considered ‘high’. The commentary captures the cross-cutting scope and we note the emphasis on the adaptation and resilience impact s (#6 and #7), for which there is a pressing need in low income communities where this project will reach. As referenced, the 54 million adaptation beneficiaries are people achieving first-time access to electricity (around 15 per cent of the total population in the participating countries ), and many will receive entry level solar home systems, via a pay as you go plan, to match their ability to pay.  

Monitoring the adaptation outcomes of the program is a core consideration of the program design. Acumen’s experience of incorporating user surveys has shown their effectiveness in capturing a range of impact dimensions, including climate resilience and adaptation metrics, and gender disaggregated impact. Survey questions can reveal patterns of household access to climate-relevant information through OGS-powered communication media , at baseline and endline, in terms of both objective and/or quantitative [e.g., point(s) of access, frequency of access , information subject matter ] and subjective and/or qualitative [e.g., perceived ease of access , perceived usefulness of access ] metrics. Tracking the frequency of access to climate information , for example, is a specific and measurable indicator to inform outcome 4, and we are considering additional monitoring methodologies to add depth to this picture, as well as verification capacity.  

In terms of Outcome 3, resilient energy access, through durability in the face of extreme events and portability in the case of displacement , in addition to event-responsive customer surveys, in the proximate aftermath of extreme climate events, our discussions are ongoing with providers of technologies able to capture and track location an d usage data of off grid solar products that will enable accurate monitoring of energy consumption patterns during and after a climate event.  

Acumen recognizes the opportunity to apply innovative methods to monitor and verify climate resilience impact, with a possible demonstration effect for the other energy programs, with broad positive impact across the sector.  

### Paradigm shift potential  

Acumen notes the iTAP’s balanced and helpful assessment of the program’s paradigm shift potential . As highlighted in the commentary, H2R has the potential to play a pivotal role in attracting investment into hard-to-reach markets where capital has not traditionally flowed, with the help of a blended finance structure which effectively mitigates the real and perceived risks associated with investing in these nascent markets . Overall , we are also positive about the multiplication effects that can be achieved through this approach, including through the knowledge sharing component.  

Acumen is paying close attention to the various risks which could undermine the impact of the program. The sector’s lessons from the shock of the COVID pandemic should be seen as a source of resilience for the sector in the medium and long-term. Off-grid solar companies faced challenges such as higher costs of raw materials and decreased incomes among their predominantly rural and economically disadvantaged customer base. As noted, there is still a need to address the fragility of OGS companies and the risks they face from external shocks , particularly to protect customers, as well as H2R investments, and our ability to achieve the desired volume of reinvestment .  

As mentioned, beyond H2R’s investments, a dedicated technical assistance facility will support companies to build up their capacity and long-term viability. TA areas include consumer protection, gender, enterprise development and ESG.  

Acumen is aligned with the view that broad stakeholder engagement is needed to enhance the potential for transformational impact . Acumen is a member of the REACH partnership, a newly formed group of leading energy access practitioners and a knowledge sharing platform, with expertise in areas including capacity building – including technical assistance – of all stakeholders (companies, financiers, and governments) and policy and regulatory support to create enabling environment.

# Hardest-to-Reach Gender Assessment  

## Introduction  

The Hardest-to-Reach Gender Assessment is a tool designed to evaluate the effectiveness of gender-specific interventions in reaching marginalized populations. It focuses on identifying barriers and opportunities for women and girls who are most difficult to reach, particularly those living in poverty or facing multiple forms of discrimination.  

## Key Components  

### 1. **Gender Analysis**  
This section evaluates how gender norms and roles influence access to resources and services. It includes assessing the impact of social, economic, and cultural factors on women’s ability to participate in decision-making processes.  

### 2. **Intersectionality**  
The assessment considers how different identities (such as race, class, age, disability) intersect with gender to create unique experiences of marginalization.  

### 3. **Data Collection Methods**  
Methods used include surveys, focus groups, and interviews to gather qualitative and quantitative data from hard-to-reach populations.  

## Implementation  

### Steps to Conduct the Assessment  
1. **Identify Target Populations**: Determine which groups are hardest to reach based on criteria such as geographic location, economic status, or social marginalization.  
2. **Design Data Collection Tools**: Develop tools that are sensitive to cultural and linguistic differences.  
3. **Engage Community Members**: Involve local leaders and community members in the process to ensure relevance and accuracy.  
4. **Analyze Results**: Use findings to inform policy and program development.  

## Challenges  

### Common Barriers  
- Lack of access to information  
- Limited mobility  
- Cultural norms that restrict participation  
- Economic constraints  

### Solutions  
- Collaborate with local organizations  
- Use culturally appropriate communication methods  
- Provide flexible scheduling for interviews or meetings  

## Conclusion  

The Hardest-to-Reach Gender Assessment plays a crucial role in ensuring that gender-sensitive programs are effective and inclusive. By focusing on the most vulnerable populations, it helps to bridge gaps in service delivery and promotes equity in access to resources.

# Introduction

This document provides an assessment of the barriers and opportunities for advancing gender inclusion in Hardest to Reach ("H2R") initiative countries. The assessment includes an overview of the evidence on women's access to clean energy technologies, women's participation in the off-grid solar ("OGS") sector workforce, and the key barriers to advancing equality in the sector. This document outlines key entry points for the H2R initiative, followed by detailed in-country assessments for the sixteen target H2R countries.

## Barriers to gender equality in the OGS sector

The link between energy access and gender equality is well recognized. Energy poverty has a strong gender dimension as the lack of access to energy inhibits women's well-being, and their access to economic opportunities, and it strongly affects women's living conditions and time-use. Off-grid energy technologies have a direct benefit to women's health, particularly in terms of reduced risks of pulmonary and respiratory diseases. Women's participation and leadership in the OGS sector can help accelerate the transition to a low-carbon economy by promoting clean energy use and addressing energy poverty, while also advancing women's economic empowerment. However, multiple barriers limit women's ability to access OGS technologies and to fully participate in OGS value chains as business leaders, employees and entrepreneurs.

One of the key barriers in the market relates to lack of sex-disaggregated data for the OGS sector both at the consumer and workforce levels. The limited sex-disaggregated data available about customer segments and demand for products leads to missed opportunities to reach women customers and meet their needs. In addition, the lack of data on women-owned enterprises and the potential for productive uses of energy prevents increasing the number of women customers and the chance to design business strategies and projects that could transform their economic opportunities.

Historical inequities of distribution and control of assets impact women's ability to access OGS products, and affordability is a well-recognized barrier to accessing OGS technologies (for both women and men). In many countries, women face discriminatory property and tenure laws, resulting in women's lower access to financing, to mobile phones and mobile money.

Gender and social norms limit women's access to technologies and their abilities to enter the workforce. Intrahousehold decision-making and mobility constraints inhibit women's ability to be aware of energy products and to purchase them. Women may be discouraged from seeking employment opportunities as sales agents and technicians because in some contexts these may not be considered suitable opportunities for them, or because they are discouraged from interacting with men due to social and gender norms.

Companies have a limited understanding of the business case for gender inclusion in their businesses, and of how to make their practices more inclusive. OGS companies may not strive to reach women customers or to hire women because they are unaware of the positive

Serving women with OGS technologies 

Overall, evidence shows that women have less access to off -grid electricity than their male 
counterparts, and they mostly use pico -solar products and lanterns or other lower -tier 
technologies.2 SHS customers are predominantly rural men living under the median poverty line 
of $3.10.3 In a survey conducted on 35,000 customers of 49 OGS companies in 17 countries, 60 
Decibels found that only 32% of customers are women.4  

An analysis conducted by Value for Women on 59 companies working in the energy sector found 
that only 23% of companies collect sex -disaggregated data on customers, and half of them 
reported that they serve 46% of customers who are women. A small group of companies (6) also 
collect data on end -users, and they reported serving 60%  women among their end -users.5 In 
another study, GOGLA (the global association for the off -grid solar energy industry) found that 
women buy solar lanterns and smaller products mostly on a cash basis. In a survey conducted

disaggregated data on customers, and those who do report that 45% of their customers are

### Table 1: Available data on women served by clean energy and off-grid solar companies

These studies indicate that the sector overall lacks sex-disaggregated data, resulting in a limited understanding of gender-related barriers to access and limited knowledge on the impact that these technologies have on women’s lives. The limited knowledge about customer segments and women’s demand for OGS products leads to missed opportunities to reach women's customers.

In addition, men are the main purchasers of energy products, even when women are the primary end-users; this results in skewed consumer data and products that do not always reflect the needs or preferences of women.

In H2R country contexts, prevalent gender norms are likely to affect access to off-grid solar technologies, influencing companies’ abilities to reach women. Discriminatory gender norms inhibit women’s rights to own land and other assets in most countries, making it challenging for women to access credit and purchase renewable energy technologies. PAYGo companies may reach a lower percentage of women customers due to additional barriers related to mobile ownership and mobile money usage among women (see Table 2).

Overall, in Sub-Saharan Africa, the gender gap in mobile ownership is 30%. This gap has grown from 22% in 2017 since ownership of smartphones among men in the region has steadily outpaced that of women.

African Indigenous People, which can be defined as “nomadic and semi-nomadic pastoralists and hunter/gatherers who live in situations of marginalization and discrimination,” 28 are faced with additional challenges in accessing resources and opportunities. In H2R countries, indigenous communities are hunter-gatherers, pastoralists and nomadic populations often without state recognition. They heavily rely on nature and land for their livelihood and are often located in forests and conservation areas, facing eviction from their land. Further, African indigenous people are generally poorer and lack access to education and health. Indigenous women face additional challenges such as sexual violence (e.g. Pygmies in DRC). In West Africa, political instability and conflicts disproportionately impact women and female-headed households. Women and girls make up a large proportion of internally displaced populations, they are victims of systemic attacks and kidnappings, and are forced into slavery.29   

In the context of a changing climate, indigenous communities and minority groups whose lives depend on the health of their natural environments are increasingly more vulnerable to shocks, while their specific energy needs are often overlooked. Without addressing their needs, the transition to clean energy is hindered in many SSA countries. Empowering Indigenous populations, women, youth, and other traditionally marginalized people is an essential part of protecting rights and creating jobs. In the context of H2R, OGS technologies can provide first access to clean and reliable energy for communities that are usually not included in traditional business models and energy programs. Their additional vulnerabilities (i.e. geographic remoteness, lower access to information and resources, higher poverty rates, discrimination) should be considered when assessing strategies for OGS product dissemination and when they consider hiring in the communities they serve.   

In conclusion, it is safe to assume that product reach to women and other vulnerable communities in H2R markets will be slightly lower than average, or compared to countries where the OGS sector is well established (e.g. Kenya). The H2R initiative provides an important opportunity to fill some of the knowledge and data gaps on reaching women as OGS customers by requesting investee companies to collect and track sex-disaggregated data on sales.

are not suited for technical roles. Companies that intentionally recruit women for these positions 
face pipeline-related challenges and need to address such gender and social norms.  

Women entrepreneurship is also hindered by gender norms and discriminatory policy frameworks.

## The H2R Initiative

The H2R initiative brings opportunities to mandate that companies collect and annually report a sex-disaggregated staff footprint, with data on female versus male employment at all levels. Such data can be also used to showcase the business case for the inclusion of women across all levels of the OGS workforce.

## Stakeholder Engagement

As part of the process of understanding the needs and perspectives of women in Hardest-to-Reach geographies, we have engaged with over 25 individuals, notably via a Stakeholder Consultation Forum with GCF Gender Monitors. The session had representation from over 11 sub-Saharan African countries, as well as from specialist gender lens off-grid solar organisations.

An example of the latter is Smiling Through Light (STL), a Sierra Leone-based social enterprise which focuses on clean energy access by working with a network of local women to distribute and sell solar products. STL focuses on the last mile which creates employment and income opportunities in underserved areas.

# [Page 1]

## We also launched a customer survey for a pilot investment in Benin where we listened to and learned from 85 women about their experience with OGS products. These women shared their opinions on the value of the products, barriers to purchase, and challenges with the products. Their challenges and hopes mirrored what we heard from gender experts, NGO leaders, and other important stakeholders.

The following insights were gained from our consultation and studies:

## The sector needs more support of women leaders and entrepreneurs: The view was expressed that, in order to address the challenges and inequities women face in energy access, it would be impactful to have more women in leadership within the sector. This could be achieved by investors backing more women-led businesses and by the provision of training to uplift women in the workforce into positions of influence and leadership. Our gender lens strategy is aligned with the 2X challenge to increase women participation in the OGS sector. Additionally, we expect to provide training for women to be sales agents and technicians in OGS as a part of our technical assistance.

## Awareness of off grid solar among women in some areas is still low: This is particularly the case in very remote areas, where there are no distributors serving the locale. According to our survey, women were slightly more likely to gain first time access to electricity with OGS and have fewer OGS alternatives than men. As a result of our consultations, conversations with gender experts and surveying of female OGS customers, we are supporting several companies with technical assistance generating gender-inclusive marketing strategies.

## Off grid solar products are very important as entry level electricity in rural areas: Hardest-to-Reach initiative is delivering a product which women in rural areas will benefit from, and will value if they are made aware of the various products and uses. 80% of women in our survey shared their lives improved with access to electricity. 50% said that their lives very much improved with their OGS product.

## There are grassroots women-led initiatives which are seeking NGO and donor support: The consultation shone a light on some worthy initiatives which women are engaged in at the grassroots level. While direct support of these is outside the scope of the H2R initiative, we are hopeful the H2R's uplift of the OGS sector can have a positive knock-on effect, in particular by raising awareness of OGS.

# Key entry points for the H2R initiative

1) Elevate women in the OGS sector as customers, employees and entrepreneurs.  
The country assessments indicate gender gaps in access to energy and employment opportunities, and a comprehensive programmatic approach is necessary to create opportunities for women at all levels.

2) Do no harm from a gender perspective.  
This entails evaluating potential investee companies’ risk identification and mitigation with respect to negative impacts on women as part of its Environmental, Social and Governance standards.  
H2R’s Environmental and Social Risk Management Plan will establish gender-specific screening criteria.

3) Provide clean and affordable energy to women customers and women-headed households.  
The country assessments and the global benchmarks indicate that women are predominantly affected by energy poverty and that poor and vulnerable households are mostly women-headed.  
The H2R initiative can impact women’s lives by requiring investees to collect and track sex-disaggregated data on customers reached and by supporting companies to implement strategies to better serve women, ultimately reducing gender gaps in access to clean energy technologies.

4) Track investments in women-led businesses and in businesses that have gender-forward business practices.  
Investors have a clear role to play in applying a gender lens to the investment process and to inform investment decisions by intentionally examining the gender balance of companies, their commitment to equality, and by measuring the progress of investees in reaching women customers.  
The H2R initiative can encourage companies to recruit, retain and advance women at all levels of the OGS value chain by integrating a gender lens in due diligence, deal structuring and in post-investment support.  
Companies that are intentional in addressing gender gaps in access to OGS technologies and opportunities can apply business practices that build customer trust and loyalty, strengthen women’s capacity to enter and advance in the sector, and at the same time reach women customers.

5) Positively impact organisational culture by supporting portfolio companies to adopt and/or expand gender-inclusive policies and business practices.  
To increase the attraction, retention, and advancement of women in the OGS workforce, companies need to have in place inclusive workplace policies and practices.  
At a minimum, companies should be expected to have anti-harassment, anti-discrimination, and wage parity policies, which are considered necessary requirements to operate in H2R contexts where wage disparities persist and where gender norms and gender-based violence (“GBV”) are inhibiting women’s economic empowerment.  
Other policies and practices such as flexible working arrangements, parental leave policies, flexible return to work after parental leave, etc. are necessary to create women-friendly workplaces and to attract female talent.  
Further, companies can apply a gender lens to other areas of their business, particularly in product design and market research and customer engagement areas (sales, distribution, after-sales), as well as their supply chains.  
Such practices will enable

# 13

Companies to understand women’s needs and to better serve women customers, ultimately meeting sales targets and contributing to H2R’s program impact.

The H2R initiative will mandate companies to assess the level of gender inclusion in their business, and support them in developing actions and strategies to become more inclusive. The gender inclusion assessment carried out during due diligence and the GAP developed within 90 days of investment (see Gender Action Plan for details) will provide detailed information about which policies companies are expected to develop or update, as well as the steps to increase women’s participation in the workforce and strengthen the pipeline of women candidates.

Evidence shows that companies that undertake actions to close gender gaps and that integrate intentionality in their strategies and business processes have been successful in attracting further financing. Such an approach will contribute to H2R’s gender equality objectives and it will help mobilize finance to enter these challenging contexts and to reach underserved markets.

## 6) Provide dedicated training and employment opportunities

At the ecosystem level, the H2R initiative will address the lack of training and mentorship opportunities by supporting women-dedicated training programs that aim at closing skills gaps. The H2R initiative should also focus on establishing partnerships between training institutions, the private sector and recruitment actors to increase awareness of job opportunities and to foster the creation of internships and mentorship programs. The initiative can also enhance collaboration by convening stakeholders such as gender lens investors, women’s associations, women entrepreneurs, and other key local actors in engagement workshops to work together to improve the operating environment for women in OGS markets.

## 7) Facilitate OGS sector transformation

Acumen has a track record of building ecosystems with a gender lens. In H2R, Acumen will use insights to build the case for the benefits of investing in hardest-to-reach markets and taking a gender lens to OGS sector facilitation. There is a tremendous opportunity to gather insights on the strategies companies will adopt to close gender gaps in the OGS sector, from reaching customers to hiring women in their workforce, and disseminating lessons learned to the wider sector.

## Country-level assessments

The assessment of the sixteen H2R countries identified context-specific challenges for advancing women’s participation in the OGS sector. These countries present some of the lowest electricity access rates and some of the highest poverty rates. Disparities between men and women persist in the areas of economic opportunities, health, education, political participation, and empowerment. Gender norms and customary laws shape women’s lives and their ability to access assets and resources and to participate in economic activities.

● The Lumière program, implemented by local NGO FVS -Amade in partnership with

preferences and affordability constraints will be mapped at the household and  
enterprise level, followed by an assessment of the positive impact on women  
as a result of adopting off -grid products (in terms of time savings, drudgery  
reduction, and livelihoods).

# Togo Entrepreneurs du Monde (EdM)

Togo Entrepreneurs du Monde (EdM) is an initiative supported by the Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS) and the government of France, created in 1998, that works with people living in fuel poverty by facilitating access to essential goods and services for their families. Based in Togo since 2012, EdM runs the Mivo Energie project that aims to combat global warming and deforestation in Togo by facilitating access to clean, modern, and affordable energy products for vulnerable populations, especially women.

# Malawi FINCOOP

Malawi FINCOOP is a financial cooperative that provides affordable financial services, serving micro, small and medium entrepreneurs, farmers, and salaried employees. The company’s Green Loan Programme targets low-income households across Malawi using multiple channels such as Village Savings and Loan Associations (VSLAs), civil services, and technology partners. FINCOOP has tailored its payment terms to fit women’s requirements through the deployment of payment methods such as Pay As You Go models, cash, and group loans, and by offering longer repayment terms. From 2018 to 2020, more than 1212 female-headed households were able to access solar products through this program.

# Lesotho

Lesotho has launched a Women In Sustainable Energy Lesotho (WISE-L) initiative that aims to promote gender equality and equity in the energy sector and create opportunities for women.

# Burkina Faso

● The GEF-funded Africa Minigrids Program implemented by UNDP seeks to demonstrate productive uses of electricity to reduce costs, increase the viability and enable mini-grid development at scale. The primary targets and actors of the pilot projects will be small-scale farmers (women and youth, mostly) who will benefit directly from the electricity supplied by the solar mini-grids. The project started in 2022 and it is planned to end in 2026.

● The GCF-funded Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN I): Greening Agricultural Banks & the Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Great Green Wall (GGW) countries program, is implemented by the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) and local organizations from the participant countries, including Burkina Faso. The program seeks to provide concessional loans to foster the best adaptation and mitigation practices for green businesses within the selected agricultural value chains in each target country. Operated by local, national agricultural banks (LNABs) – the Agricultural Bank of Burkina Faso – the facility will offer special lines of financing for green business projects prepared by farmer’s organizations (FOs), women and youth organizations, cooperatives, and MSMEs (including agribusiness dealers and solar operators). Besides providing loans, the program includes a Technical Assistance Facility (TAF) to address capacity, knowledge, and policy.

# Democratic Republic of Congo  
## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country  

The Democratic Republic of Congo is one of the biggest countries in Africa, with a population of around 92 million people – 50.1% of which are women – an annual population growth of 3.1%, with 33% of the total population under the age of 15.  

The Gender Development Index for the DRC is 0.885, placing women’s human development at about 83% of that of men. According to the GDI scoring, the DRC belongs to the group of countries with the lowest human development (countries with low equality in HDI achievements between women and men and absolute deviation from gender parity of more than 10 percent). On the Gender Inequality Index (GII), which measures disparities between men and women in the areas of economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment, the country is ranked among the highest in the world (151).  

The DRC also scores low on human development, with women being in worse conditions compared to men (0.449 HDI for women versus 0.507 for men), and DRC ranks 179th out of 191 countries on the Human Development Index (2019).

## Legal status of women  

The DRC has ratified many UN Conventions relating to equity and inclusion, including the 1969 Convention on Eradication of Racial Discrimination, the 1966 Convention on Civil and Political Rights, and the 1966 Convention on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights (ICESCR). Further, DRC has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW).  

The 2006 Constitution prohibits discrimination against women in its many forms and emphasizes gender parity in public institutions (Article 14). It also provides through Articles 5, 14, and 15 the foundations of legitimization of any policy of equality and equity in the country. Under the Constitution, women and men have equal rights to work, education, vote, justice, and political participation.

# 20

The DRC developed a National Gender Policy (2017 -2021), which aims to achieve gender equality and social empowerment through five strategic axes: (i) the consolidation of gender equality and the empowerment of women; (ii) strengthening the role and place of women and girls in the economy and employment; (iii) increased access of women and girls to decision-making spheres; (iv) fight against forms of sexual violence; and (v) strengthening the institutional mechanism for implementation of the National Gender Policy. Despite an overall favorable policy framework for gender equality, implementation of policies remains a challenge, with a lack of appropriate budget, limited awareness, poor coordination, a limited capacity of implementing agencies, as well as constraining societal gender norms.

## Political participation

The Constitution forbids discrimination against women (Article 14), but there is no law or policy that ensures women’s just representation. The DRC demonstrates only 13% female representation in the National Assembly, 10% of women elected at the provincial level, no female governors in the provinces, and just under 14% women representatives as decision-making authorities for public administration.

Gender norms impact women’s political participation, and there is a strong notion that politics are for men and that women often need to follow their husband’s opinions.

## Social and cultural context

Men dominate both the political and social spheres, and they are the culturally and legally appointed heads of the household, presenting them with decision-making power over most aspects of life. Women are defined by their household or reproductive responsibilities, which include maintaining the physical and moral well-being of their families. Men are perceived to be responsible for the financial health of their families. According to the law, women are required to obey their husbands, and they cannot be head of the household in the same way as men. Further, they do not have the same rights to remarry.

Traditional gender roles and social norms exist in both urban and rural areas; however, they appear to be more rigid in rural settings.

## Sexual and gender-based violence (SGBV)

Sexual and gender-based violence (SGBV) has been a defining feature of the ongoing conflict in the country, and the DRC has one of the highest incidences of rape in the world, and there is no legislation addressing domestic violence. Domestic violence is widespread and is generally accepted as a response to women’s alleged failings in the household.

In fact, sexual violence against women and girls has been prevalent, especially by a partner or household member. UN Women reports that 51% of Congolese women experience lifetime physical and/or sexual intimate partner violence, with 37% reporting violence in the last 12 months.

## Household decisions

Household decisions are dominated by men, and the lack of agency within the home and within communities has a direct influence on women’s productive activities and their ability to access and control resources. A survey conducted by the World Bank found that men take decisions over a woman’s income, health, major household investments, and social visits.

Gender norms mean that there is an expectation for men to be the primary household breadwinner, and consequently they are expected to pay for major

## Household expenses

## Indigenous women face additional challenges in DRC

Indigenous Pygmies are traditional hunter gatherers, and thus remaining poor and marginalised with limited access to land rights. Women from these tribes are often subjected to sexual violence by Bantu people, due to being considered 'subhuman'.

With DRC's forest cover being a vast 62% of the country's land, conservation efforts have been made to protect this "largest carbon sink of the world", which include repossession of lands from them under the garb of protecting these forests, which threatens the traditional way of life for these Pygmies and makes indigenous women even more vulnerable due to displacement.

Recently laws have been passed toward Promotion and Protection of the Rights of the Indigenous Pygmy Peoples, granting them the right and access to free, prior and informed consent before their land is exploited by government or industries.

## Health

A high maternal mortality rate is a major health issue in the DRC (740 per 100,000 live births); it is caused by several factors such as lack of access to medical treatment, early marriage, and high birth rates. Throughout the country, the average age at first birth is 19.9 years, and 37% of women aged 20 -24 were married before their eighteenth birthday, as compared to 6% of men in the same age group. Congolese women suffer from poorer health compared to men, with women's health being undermined by men's control over women's bodies, low access to resources, and women's lack of freedom to move.

## Education

One of the key drivers of inequality in the country is linked to gender disparities in education, directly impacting women's participation in the economy and wage earnings. Articles 43 and 44 of the Constitution stipulate free obligatory education and the eradication of illiteracy. However, girls often drop out of school due to early marriage and pregnancy, which leads to a gender gap in education.

The average Congolese woman will benefit from 5.3 years of formal schooling compared to 8.4 years for men; as such, only 40% of women have attended secondary school compared to 69 % of men. Nationally, an estimated 66% of Congolese women are literate compared to 89% of men. Among young women, literacy is increasing; an estimated 80% of young women are literate compared to 91% of young men. Traditionally, education for women and girls is less valued and households often prioritize education for boys compared to girls.

# Gender and Economic Empowerment in the Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC)

## Overview of Gender Disparities

Women are mostly engaged in agriculture, predominantly subsistence farming, and small businesses in the informal sector. The labour force participation rate for women is 61.2% compared to 69.1% of men, and only 6.4% of women work in wage employment, compared to 23.9% of men.

Gender gaps persist across different economic sectors, with large discrepancies in agricultural production (17.8% gap between men and women), in entrepreneurship profits (66.5% gap) and in wage earnings (77.3% gap). Less than 10% of women are wage or salaried workers compared to 25% of men.

In the private sector, the wage gap is nearly 80%. The underlying causes of such gaps include women’s higher burden of care work, which is the primary factor behind women’s lower labour force participation. Lower educational attainment, skills, and concentration in lower-paying sectors are instead the primary drivers of women’s lower-wage earnings.

The entrepreneurship gender gap indicates that businesswomen have lower levels of capital and lower numbers of workers. The female share of employment in senior and middle management is 6%, while only 11% of firms have a female top manager (2013).

Further, across all sectors, lack of control over land, lack of agency in the household, prevalent gender-based violence, and marriage have been found to be consistent drivers of gaps in economic empowerment.

## Family Code Reforms

The first Family Code (1987) included legal restrictions to women’s mobility, workplace, marriage, and assets. However, the 2016 reform of the Code included the elimination of marital authorization for women’s access to employment, bank accounts, and loans, directly impacting women’s ability to explore new economic opportunities.

While family laws have been updated, many have yet to be ratified. Specifically, Article 444 of the DRC Family Code (1987) provides that a husband is head of the household, and the revised Family Code (2016) still maintains this. However, of note in the revised version is the elimination of the need for a married woman to obtain her husband’s permission to open an individual bank account, or access financial services such as credits or loans.

As such, most married women’s decisions remain under the discretion of their husbands, who retain control of all household, financial, and medical decisions in both statutory and cultural law. Widowed, divorced, or single women face ostracization and most often must submit to the will and authority of male family members.

## Women Business and the Law Index Score

The Women Business and the Law Index Score for the DRC is 78.8 (2021). The overall score for the Democratic Republic of the Congo is higher than the regional average observed across Sub-Saharan Africa (71.5).

## Gender and Energy in DRC

Only 19% of the population have access to electricity. Gender norms restrict women’s access to resources and assets, which directly impacts energy poverty. Because women often do not make decisions alone on household spending, they are likely to have less access to energy products and technologies.

Sex-disaggregated data on access to energy as well as women’s participation in the sector is absent.

# Women's Role in Off-grid Energy Value Chains

Women can play a key role in off-grid energy value chains, but challenges remain particularly related to access to finance, intra-household decision-making, and education levels. Social and gender norms and pervasive GBV affect women's ability to engage in entrepreneurial activities and inactively participate in energy value chains as consumers, entrepreneurs, and agents of change.

## Overview of Gender Equality and Gender Gaps in Uganda

Uganda has a total population of 34.6 million, of which 51.21% are women. The country has one of the world's youngest and fastest-growing populations with 55% of people under 18 years of age and a very high fertility rate of 5.4.

The Gender Development Index in Uganda is 0.927 and is ranked 166 out of 191 in Human Development Index, placing the country among other 'Group 3' countries with a high absolute deviation from gender parity, of 5-7.5%. This is largely driven by a gender gap in mean years of schooling and gross national income per capita.

Uganda is ranked 131 in the Gender Inequality Index which measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.

1. Gender and livelihoods ; with the aim to redress three areas of inequality including gender

government to include gender in macroeconomic policies.

## About 46% of total elected representatives in LGs

However, women are underrepresented in executive committees where decision-making at the LG level takes place, and elected women representatives are unable to effectively fulfill their roles. This is found to be due to an overall patriarchal society in Uganda, wherein there exists misperception associated with women commanding leadership positions, and where women's caregiving responsibilities leave them time-poor to effectively engage with the demands of public life. Further, limited access to income and low education attainment levels among women limit their ability to navigate the commercialized electoral process and technical processes associated with political decision-making.

The high costs of political campaigning have also been highlighted by the UN Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women.

## Social and cultural context

In Uganda, Women's rights on land have been recognized in the Land Act (Cap 227) and Land Acquisition Act (Cap 226) and the country aims to promote tenure security in women's access to land under their IIIrd National Development Plan. These have led to a sharp increase in both land and home ownership by women and in joint ownership between the period 2013 to 2017, compared to male dominance in ownership prior to 2012-13.

However, the country continues to be a patriarchal and patrilinear society with women's domestic and household responsibilities impacting their effective participation in productive work. Further, research has found that in Uganda, as women attain increased access to social networks and empowerment, especially at the age group 30 to 39, they are often subjected to violence by their current or former partners.

Almost 50% of women report experiencing some form of physical and/or sexual intimate partner violence in their lifetime and about 30% reported experiencing it in the last 12 months.

Uganda has developed a male involvement strategy, finalized in 2017 with the aim of engaging men and boys in prevention and response to GBV.

## Health

Uganda has a maternal mortality ratio (MMR) of 375 per 100,000 live births (as of 2017), which is extremely high compared to the Sustainable Development Goal target of achieving no more than an MMR of 70 by 2030. Even with the introduction of 14 different policy instruments over 15 years by the government, to reduce MMR, the failures in referral policies, inadequacies in healthcare facilities and trained personnel and transportation issues remained.

# 26

## Life of the Mother

In Uganda, men are expected to complete 6.7 mean years of schooling compared to 4.9 years completed by women. As of 2018, 83% of adult males are literate and 71% of adult females are literate. Interestingly, however, among the young population, there is gender parity in literacy levels with 90% of women in the age group of 15-24 being literate and 89% of men attaining literacy which shows an improving trend for the next generation of the Ugandan population. However, the lower secondary completion rate is low among both men (27.7%) and women (25.1%) in Uganda.

Under its National Strategy for Girls Education (2015-2019) the country has created a policy implementation Framework to promote girls' education by aiming to harmonize education programs on Girls' Education for effective coordination, monitoring and evaluation, ensure adequate commitment to requisite human and financial resources to promote girls' education, institutionalize research on Girls' Education to inform policy development and build the capacity of education actors to address gender inequaliti es in education.

Uganda has also been commended internationally by the Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women for the strides made in the education sector, including a particular focus on building infrastructure for the education of girls with special needs. Notably, Uganda has a Science Education Policy (2004) under which physics, chemistry, and biology have been made compulsory in lower secondary schools, and 75% of government sponsorships in tertiary institutions are for science-based courses. However, only 25% of students opting for science-based courses at the university level are women, hence they are unable to enjoy state-funded science education at this level.

Although the country has introduced key legislation to ensure improved education access for women, the country is still falling behind in effectively transitioning women into the world of work. There has been a steep drop in labour force participation of women in the ages of 15-24, from 74.6% in 2013 to 54.2% in 2017. While some of this decline can be explained by improved access to schooling, the share of inactivity is also increasing among women with them being more likely than men to be both out of school and out of a job due to familial responsibilities and lack of job opportunities.

# Gender and Energy in Uganda

It is widely recognized that consistent and reliable access to energy is critical for both economic growth and social transformation. Further, access to clean energy can improve health & well-being as well as the status of time poverty among women by reducing the burden of collecting firewood.

Over the past decade, Uganda has increased its electricity generation capacity from 601MW in 2010 to 1839MW in 2020. However, in Uganda, biomass continues to be the predominant source of cooking fuel used by 94% of the population with 72.8% using firewood and 21.4% using charcoal. Further, firewood use is 3% higher among female heads of households than men.

Overall, 56% population has access to electricity, with the majority of the households (27%) using solar kits for lighting, 19% using grid electricity, and 11% using solar home systems. While 51.6% of women-headed households have electricity access in the country, 59% men headed households have electricity access.

There is high potential for women to be employed in the clean energy sector, as last mile agents generating demand for electricity consumption, which continues to be a challenge in Uganda. At 100 kWh per capita, Uganda currently has one of the lowest rates of electricity consumption per capita in the world. However, currently, the participation rate of women in Distributed Renewable Energy is 28% of the total workforce in

# Benin

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Benin has a population of 12.8 million people, with a young population of 42% below the age of 14. The fertility rate in the country stands at 4.6, which is significantly high compared to other countries in the region.

The Gender Development Index in Benin is 0.880 and the country is placed with 'Group 5' countries with a high absolute deviation from gender parity, of more than 10%. This is largely driven by a gender gap in expected and mean years of schooling.

Benin has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.602 and is ranked 152, which measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.

### Legal status of women

Benin has ratified various UN conventions which aim towards ensuring equality and inclusion, including the Elimination of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) in 1982, and the Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC) in 1990.

In the African region, Benin has also ratified the Solemn Declaration of the Heads of State on Gender Equality in Africa and committed to action under the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa.

The Constitution of Benin accords equal rights to all without distinction of origin, race, gender, religion, political opinion, or social position under Article 26. Under its Code of Persons and the Family in the Republic of Benin, the country also sets the legal age of marriage at 18 for both men and women.

promulgates that men and women are guaranteed the same inheritance rights under the law. Further, Benin has also established a policy framework to translate the legal commitment to action through its National Gender Promotion Policy, National Policy for the Promotion of Women in Agriculture, and the National Action Plan to Combat Gender-Based Violence. Benin, under its National Gender Promotion Policy (consolidated in 2009), aims to achieve gender equality by 2025; however, the action plan to implement strategies laid down under the policy was only articulated in 2015 and is yet to be reviewed and assessed which raises questions on how successful the country’s progress is towards the envisioned goal of the policy.

Other gender promotion systems in Benin include the establishment of Social Promotion Centers, a National Commission for the Promotion of Women, a National Institute for the Promotion of Women, and the Ministry of Social Affairs and Microfinance.

Although the legal and policy institutionalization in Benin is robust, policy implementation is replete with enforcement issues in the backdrop of heavily patriarchal social structures still prevalent in the country.

## Political participation

Women in Benin remain underrepresented in political positions. In the 2019 legislative elections, only 6% of National Assembly members were women. Similar to this trend, in the 2020 municipal elections, 4.3% of councilors were women and there were 4 women mayors out of 77 elected representatives. Benin has now deployed a new electoral code was deployed in 2021 which has reserved a minimum quota of 24 out of 109 seats for women, at the rate of one woman per electoral district.

At the communal counselor level, women had only 3.86% representation in 2020.

## Social and cultural context

There is a continued problem of Gender Based violence experienced by women in Benin with 23.8% of women experiencing it at least once in their lifetime.

There is a wide regional disparity in women’s access to land across Benin. While women in central and southern Benin have more control over land, for example; 20.7% in Couffo, and 17.8% in Plateau, women in other regions have significantly limited access like in Alibori (10.9%) & Atacora (12.2%).

As per the Benin Household Demographic and Health Survey analysis showed that while 89% of women reported going for at least one antenatal care (ANC) visit, only half of these women initiated ANC in the first trimester and completed 4 or more visits. ## Education

The literacy rate among women in Benin is 31% compared to 54% for men120 and 10% of females in primary

Literacy and Adult Education which was adopted in 2001 and aimed at reducing the rate of illiteracy among 
men and women by half between 2001 and 2010123 however World Bank data suggests that between 2002 
and 2012, the literacy rate actually dropped by 1%. 124 While enrollment of girls is high at the primary level, 
even surpassing that of boys, there is an evident gender gap in secondary education enrollment which is 
40.8% for men compared to 34.1% for women. 125 Benin has committed to a post -2015 Education Sector 
Plan (2018 -2030) to promote access and retention in education for girls by waiving school fees at the 
secondary level for girls. The country has budgeted USD 6.4 million for the year 2019 for this fee waiver for 
girls. Further, Benin h as budgeted USD 306,023 between 2018 and 2021 to provide scholarships for 
promoting girls’ enrollment in agricultural and industrial education. 126  

However, to improve the enrollment and retention rate of women at the secondary level, Benin needs to 
address structural gaps in its education system. The World Bank -supported Global Partnership for 
Education Project identified challenges that cripple Benin’s education system currently including; high 
administrative burden among staff, slow -moving and highly po litical decision -making processes that 
interfere with managerial decisions, improper division of roles and poor coordination across Ministries, 
rapidly changing leadership at the institutional level and the problem of high -absenteeism of teaching 
staff.127

Germany.128

# Women’s economic participation and entrepreneurship

Benin has seen a growing Labour Force Participation among women in the past 2 decades. While the disparity between men's and women’s labor force participation (LFPR) in not high overall with about 69.3% LFPR for women and 73.3% for men, the type of work conducted by women is largely informal. As per the Enquête Harmonisée Sur le Conditions de Vie des Ménages survey in Benin, 95.5% of women workers concentrated in the informal sector compared to 86% of men.

129 Women and young people constitute the majority of unemployed people, with 37,5% of 15 -24 old being without a job, and 24,6% of them being women.

While the high share of female graduates in Engineering and ICT fields should create a thriving entrepreneurship environment in the country for women, as per data only 25.9% had female directors and 36.7% of businesses have women among their shareholders.

130 However, the country has an above-average estimated gross income per capita among women of 2,998$, compared to other Sub-Saharan African nations.

131 Women-led businesses are concentrated in service industries such as accommodation and food as well as retail and wholesale, which unfortunately have seen been most impacted due to COVID.

Female-headed businesses in Benin remain unregistered with only 13% having certification compared to 21% of men-headed businesses having requisite registration.

132  

## Gender and energy in Benin

According to ERI-ESI 2018, 63.4% of households use charcoal compared to 22% for gas and 12.2% for wood, and energy use for cooking is predominantly biomass.

133 About 41% of households have access to electricity in Benin.

134 Only 35% of women have access to electricity, compared to 69% of men.

135  

The Energy Ministry in Benin has prepared a gender profile for the energy sector in 2020 and a 2021-2025 National Action Plan for the Policy for Gender Mainstreaming and Energy Access in Benin and operationalized a Gender and Development Unit to ensure gender mainstreaming across the Ministry’s projects.

ECREEE is implementing a project entitled: Promoting Agricultural Competitiveness through Solar Energy (PAC-SOLAR) in Benin with the aim to enhance women’s access to renewable energy. As part of this project, women have also women have been trained and charged with the job of cleaning the solar panels regularly, and maintain solar PV installations.

136  

However, overall, women in Benin are unable to access training and finance to learn high-skill trades in the energy sector and there continues to be a higher preference for employing and financing men in the energy sector.

# Zambia

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Zambia has a population of over 19 million, 50.5% of which are women, and where over 55% of the population lives in rural areas – the majority of them being women.

Zambia has a comparatively equal development index when compared with other Sub-Saharan African countries, with a Gender Development Index of 0.965. The country is placed with 'Group 2' countries with an absolute deviation from gender parity between 2.5 percent and 5 percent. This is a result of the country achieving equality in SDG 4.4 of mean years of schooling with both girls and boys receiving 7.2 years of schooling.

Zambia has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.540 and is ranked 138, which measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.

### Indicator Result Source and date

- **Life expectancy at birth**: 63.9  
  UNDP (2022)

- **Maternal mortality ratio**: 213  
  UNDP (2022)

  World Bank, 2018

- **Women’s Labour Force Participation Rate (age 15 to 64)**: 71%  
  World Bank, 2019

- **Mean years of schooling**: 7.2  
  UNDP (2022)  

- **Estimated gross income per capita**: 2,615  
  UNDP (2022)  

### Legal status of women

Zambia has a favorable policy framework to advance gender equality in the country. The Constitution (Amendment) Act No. 2 of 2016 provides equal rights to women and men to freely participate in, determine and build a sustainable political, legal, economic and social order. The Constitution also underlines national values and principles which include equity, equality, and non-discrimination.

Further, the country has also enacted the Gender Equity and Equality Act, 2015, to strengthen the legal framework for the elimination of all forms of discrimination against women and girls as committed under the Maputo Protocol; and put in place

place affirmative action to eliminate all barriers which prevent women from participating meaningfully in all

employer shall not discriminate against a woman by dismissing or demoting 17 the woman on the basis of 
sex, marriage, disability, pregnancy or maternity leave or subject the woman to any other disadvantage in 
employment” further stipulating that women must be awarded maternity leave with pay without loss of 
employment, seniority or social allowances. 143 Progress has also been made with regard to women’s 
human rights with the revision of their penal code in 2005, 2010, and 2012 as per which punishm ents for 
sexual violence have been made more severe.144  

As a result of Zambia’s policy environment, the country has made strides across the board with respect to 
women’s health status, labor force participation, and literacy rate as is highlighted in the following sections.  

## Political participation

Women’s participation in political and economic spheres in Zambia is low, however, it has continuously 
risen. The proportion of parliamentary seats held by women increased from 14% in 2011 to 18.9% in 
2018.145 However, recently women’s political representation has seen a decline in the most recent August 
2021 election, with women now holding 13% of parliamentary seats and 21% of cabinet positions compared 
to 17% and 40% of seats in the executive cabinet in 2016. This decline is a result of the recent limited skills 
and resources available to women candidates required for political mobilization and the inability of leading 
parties to fulfill mandatory quotas for the representation of women within their cadre. Further , the election 
apparatus in the country has also been unable to curtail violence during polls, which dissuades women 
candidates and also impedes voter turnout among women voters. 146  

## Social and cultural context

There are 73 ethnic groups in Zambia, catego rized into three social systems which include matrilineal 
patrilineal, and bilateral social structures. However, overall, Zambian society is based on patriarchal values 
and is highly conservative. Zambia has a dual legal system consisting of civil law and customary law, with 
customary law favoring men over women with respect to land access and marriage. 147 However, data 
suggest that Zambia has a very low gender disparity in ownership of a house with 35% of women owning 
a house either alone or jointly and 38% of men having home ownership rights alone or jointly. 148  

The country presents high rates of GBV, wit h more than one -third (36%) of women age 15 -49 having 
experienced physical violence at least once since age 15, and 14% have experienced sexual violence. 149
