# 36

The MMR in the country is much lesser than in other Sub-Saharan African countries. Maternal mortality rates have been decreasing over the years, from 398 deaths per 100,000 live births in 2013–14 to 252 in 2018 and 84% of deliveries by women took place at health institutions. Further, 97% of women who delivered children in the 5 years preceding the survey received antenatal care (ANC) from a skilled provider. The Zambian government under the Gender Equity and Equality Act also protects and promotes women’s sexual and reproductive health for girls, and reinforces women's right over family planning. The country has also added Comprehensive Sexuality Education to the school curriculum and there has been an increased focus on youth in sexual education and service provision through training programs for healthcare providers and peer educators in adolescent-friendly sexual reproductive health services across districts.

However, there continues to be an unmet need for family planning among 20% of married women in the country.

## Education

As per World Bank estimates, about 83% of adult women in the country are literate and this estimate has been steadily rising over the past 2 decades. This is a result of the country’s holistic Girls Education and Women’s Empowerment and Livelihoods (GEWEL) project to support girls from vulnerable households with school requisites and fees to enable them to complete secondary education. The project aims to increase access to secondary education for 25,000 adolescent girls coming from extremely poor households.

However, higher education is very low in the country, with the DHS estimating that only about 6% of women and 8% of men attain higher education. However, in an effort to increase women’s access to tertiary education, the country has now reserved 30% of state-offered bursaries to support women. Further, to encourage women’s admission in STEM fields at the tertiary level, the country has reduced the cut-off score for girls for entry into these courses.

## Women’s economic participation and entrepreneurship

As per ILO estimates, the Labour force participation rate for women is at 71%, which is rather high compared to other SAA countries. The 2020 Labour Force survey from the country points out a lower LFPR for women; however, the survey indicates that at the national level, females accounted for a higher percentage of the working-age population (15 years old or older) at 52.8% compared to males at 47.2.

However, men constitute 62% of formal employment, while women only 38%, and 73% of women contribute to unpaid family labor. The largest proportion of women works in agriculture, followed by sales professions. Within the agricultural sector, women represent only 19% of formal employees while men make up 81% of formal employees in the Agri sector.

However, the government has extended social security

For instance, the Agricultural Development and Value Chain Enhancement (ADVAN CE) Project targets the women and youth in 288 Chiefdoms of the country by providing tractors and tillers. Furthermore, the Government has launched the Climate Change Gender Action Plan (ccGAP) to mainstream gender in the country’s climate change policy, including in funding and programming.

## Gender and energy in Zambia

Zambia have experienced a high rate of deforestation, posing an increasing challenge to women and girls who are primarily responsible for fuelwood collection and need to travel long distances to collect firewood for household energy. Estimates suggest that women in Zambia spend on average 800 hours a year in fuelwood collection.

Overall, only 32.8% population of Zambia has access to electricity. However, compared to other Southern African countries, Zambia has one of the highest shares of modern renewables in energy consumption at 27.6 in 2017. However, this share has seen a slight decline from 29.4.

The Ministry of Energy conducted a Gender Assessment of the Energy Sector in Zambia in 2018 and is in the process of developing a Gender Mainstreaming Strategy and Action Plan. Zambia’s Energy Regulatory Board (ERB) has also committed to participate in the Women in Energy Regulation Internship Program, organized by the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners (NARUC) with funding support from the U.S. Agency for International Development (USAID) and Power Africa to provide education and training opportunities and foster a gender-equitable workforce in the energy sector.

# Mozambique

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Mozambique is an East African nation that has a population of 33.1 million people, with a young and adolescent population of 34% between the age group of 10 to the age of 24 years. The fertility rate in the country stands at 4.6.

The Gender Development Index in Mozambique is 0.922 and the country is placed with 'Group 4' countries with a medium to low equality in HDI achievements between women and men (absolute deviation of 7.5–10 percent). This is primarily driven by a gender gap in expected and mean years of schooling of 2.4 years among females and 4.1 years among males.

Mozambique has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.537 and is ranked 136, which measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.

### Indicator Result Source and date

- **Life expectancy at birth**: 62.4  
  UNDP (2022)

### Legal status of women

Mozambique has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1997 and signed on the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action in 1995. Further, the country signed The Declaration of Gender in Africa in 2005 and the SADC protocol about Gender and Development in 2008, and the Maputo Protocol.

The country also has a conducive framework for women to have access to land and productive assets through the 1997 Land Law which enables women to register themselves for their land use and the 2005 Amended Commercial Code under which women can engage in business without permission by their husbands. Further, under its constitution, the country also formulated a Law on Domestic Violence in 2009 to protect women from abuse and violence.

# [Page 1]

## Contraception and Family Planning

The prevalence of contraception has increased from 16.5% to 26.0% between 2010 and 2020. Still, 22% of married women report an unmet need for Family Planning.

Further, the presence of skilled birth attendants has also increased from 48% in 2010 to 73% in 2020 (UNDP 2010 and 2020 as cited in UN Women report). However, the prevalence of HIV and AIDS is much higher among women in Mozambique compared to men. Overall, 15.4% of women compared to 10.1% of men are HIV positive in the country.

A UN Women report suggests that this is a consequence of the limited decision-making power women in Mozambique have as compared to the African and global averages and traditional practices whereby women are coerced to have unprotected sexual contact with partners.

## Education

The country's literacy rate for females aged 15 and above is 50%.

As per the Ministry of Education and human development’s 2018 survey, girls represent around 48% of the pupils across levels of education. However, there are vast regional differences in educational attainment with gender parity decreasing as one looks at higher grades, across the central and northern provinces of the country (Mined 2018 as cited in UN Women report).

For girls, school attendance continues to be an issue often leading to high dropout rates for girls. The issue of adolescent pregnancy is also prevalent in the country, causing low attendance rates among girls, with about 38% of adolescent girls giving birth in Mozambique, which is the highest among the Southern African Development Community.

Due to factors such as unintended pregnancy, high burden of domestic work, distance to school, safety concerns, and poor quality of education, there is a limited progression of women to secondary and university levels of education.

As a result, only 31% of girls transition to secondary school, and only 2% complete university.

## Women’s Economic Participation and Entrepreneurship

As per the Gender Inequality Index, there is a negligible disparity in labour force participation (LFPR) with women's LFPR at 77.7% and men's LFPR at 79%.

However, the gender gap is evident when one looks at the quality and intensity of employment, with women working predominantly in the informal and subsistence sectors as self-employed or unpaid workers and working lesser hours in productive employment compared to men.

While 31% of men are unpaid workers, this figure is much higher, at 44% for women.

Agriculture remains the main sector of employment for both men and women followed by sales

# Gender and Energy in Mozambique

Currently 30.6% of the population in Mozambique has access to electricity. About 80% of the energy use is from biomass which places a disproportionate burden on women to collect firewood. To expand the country's electrification, Mozambique launched Mozambique's National Energy for All Programme in 2018 which targets full access to electricity by 2030.

The share of modern renewables in Mozambique's final energy consumption has significantly increased between 2014 and 2017 from 18.7% to 30%. Vast gas reserves have been discovered in the Rovuma Basin of Mozambique, which will enable the country to become one of the top 10 suppliers of liquefied natural gas. However, the power, gas, and mining sectors combined have not been able to generate much employment in the extractive industry, creating no more than 1.1% of jobs in 2018.

In the country, there are existing initiatives and organizations that focus on expanding women's representation and building the capacity of women who are engaged in the energy sector:

1. The Mozambique Women Of Energy is a platform for women in the energy sector who identify areas of action for gender equality in this sector
2. BRILHO programme, an SNV - Netherlands Development Organisation program in partnership with TechnoServe aims to increase the number of women with access to modern energy solutions in off-grid communities and enhance the inclusion and empowerment of women in the sector as collaborators and leading businesses. They do this through technical training of companies on gender-forward company policies and practices

# Sierra Leone  
## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country  

Sierra Leone is a country in West Africa, on the Atlantic Ocean. The country has a population of 8.3 million people, with a young and adolescent population of 33% between the age group of 10 to the age of 24 years. The fertility rate in the country stands at 3.9, which is somewhat lower compared to some of the other West African nations.

The Gender Development Index in Sierra Leone is 0.893 and the country is placed among ‘Group 5’ countries with a high absolute deviation from gender parity, of more than 10%. This is primarily driven by a gender gap in mean years of schooling; with 3.5 years among females and 5.8 years among males.

Sierra Leone has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.633 and is ranked 162, which measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.

## Legal status of women  

Sierra Leone has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1988 and the Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC) in 1990. The country has also ratified the Beijing Platform for Action (1995); UN General Assembly Resolution 52/86 on Crime Prevention and Criminal Justice Measures to Eliminate Violence against Women (1998), The Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (Maputo Protocol) (2003). Sierra Leone has also committed to the Solemn Declaration on Gender Equality in Africa adopted by African Union Heads of State and Government in July 2004; UNSC Resolution 1820 (2008) which calls on states to end impunity for sexual violence and ensure that all victims have equal protection under the law.

Under Article 15 of Sierra Leone’s constitution, the foundations for gender equality are laid; with the commitment that:

# 43

“..every person in Sierra Leone is entitled to the fundamental human rights and freedoms of the individual, that is to say, has the right, whatever his race, tribe, place of origin, political opinion, color, creed or sex..”205  
The country has laid a solid legal foundation for gender equality with the recent Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment (GEWE) policy released in 2021, which is a consolidation of the country’s existing National Policy on the Advancement of Women and the National Policy on Gender Mainstreaming adopted in 2000. Apart from this, the country has three other Gender Justice Acts; including the Child Rights Act; the Anti-Human Traffic Act; the Sexual Offences Act 2012 (amended in 2019). The GEWE has the following broad objectives; which it aims to meet through a multisectoral approach206  

1. Achieve gender equity and equality in all sectors and enable women to take their rightful position in national development, political leadership, and decision-making;  
2. Ensure satisfactory quality of life for women and girls and eliminate the feminization of poverty;  
3. Eliminate all forms of GBV and discrimination  

Taking cognizance of the need to educate and sensitize men, the country also released a Male Involvement Strategy for the Prevention of SGBV in Sierra Leone; aiming to encourage the participation of men and boys as change agents and allies in changing power relations to champion women’s and girls’ rights.207 However, a study has indicated that the donor-funded (UNFPA) objective and intended approach to male engagement in the country have not necessarily translated to project design and implementation by local organizations. And while the involvement of men towards maternal care is reaping dividends, the training aimed at empowering men to engage in women’s empowerment, may in fact run a risk of reinforcing patriarchal structures that dictate that men are responsible for ‘protecting women’, taking the agency away from women.208  

## Political participation

In Sierra Leone, the position of women's political representation remains dismal with only 12.3% of women MPs being women.209 Under the Gender Equality and Women’s Empowerment Policy, Sierra Leone’s cabinet committed to ensuring women's active participation in political leadership and decision-making at all levels by setting aside 30% of elective and public offices for women.210 To enforce compliance with this quota system; the country has also amended the Political Parties Act, to replace the Political Parties Registration Commission with the Political Parties Regulation Commission, enhancing their statutory powers to penalize parties that fail to comply with quota provisions.211 However, despite these measures, women candidates face multiple barriers that impede their participation in public political life including violent hostility, intimidation, limited fund availability, low literacy, and confidence.212 Further, customary

# [Page 1]

## Laws are also in contrast to statutory laws especially at the local levels, with women not being recognized as Chieftains in the Northern Regions and in Kailahun and Kono Districts in the Eastern Region.

### Social and cultural context

The presence of customary laws in Sierra Leone undermines women’s position as owners of land tenure. Since the majority of ethnicities are patrilinear, women are not entitled to land. And while it is a criminal offense to evict widowed women from their land, there continues to be ignorance about the legal rights of women to land tenure and reported dispossession of land among widows or divorced women.

In Sierra Leone, 53% of women have experienced Physical and/or Sexual Intimate Partner Violence in their lifetime.

### Gender-based violence remains a critical area of concern in Sierra Leone, with the President declaring a State of Emergency on rape and sexual violence in February 2019 which led to the amendment of the Sexual Offences Act of 2012 by Parliament in 2019.

As per the amended Sexual Offences Act, the maximum penalty for rape and sexual penetration of a child has been increased from fifteen years to life imprisonment; and provisions have been made for the introduction of prosecution for aggravated sexual assault under this Act.

### Health

Sierra Leone has one of the world’s highest maternal mortality ratios of 1,120 per 100,000 live births as per the UN database, just behind Chad and South Sudan.

The country’s infrastructure withstood stresses under the country’s civil war and Ebola epidemic and the public health infrastructure continues to face challenges of inadequate health practitioner staff, low skills among health workers, limited availability of medical equipment and supplies, as well as delayed referrals from lower level facilities. Further, while almost all pregnant women make 1 ANC visit, only half of these women underdo HIV testing during ANC visits.

Women’s agency to decide their age of marriage and use contraception for family planning is also limited in Sierra Leone. 29.6% of girls are married before the age of 18, 21% of women aged 15 -19 had a birth or were pregnant (in 2019) and 25% of married women report an unmet need of family planning, which has not changed between 2013 and 2019. Women also have poor nutritional status with 47% of women aged 15-49 being anemic.

### Education

Literacy rate among women aged 15 and above is only 40% in Sierra Leone. Regionally this is lower than other African countries and also much lower compared to the literacy rate among adult boys which stands at 55%, as per World Bank data.

The gap is even wider as per the country’s Demographic and Health survey, with 42% literacy among women compared with 62% among men. Data from 2015 indicates that

# 45

For Grade 2 in lower secondary, about 13.6% of girls dropped out of school compared to 10.6% of boys, attributed to harassment and abuse from teachers and male peers, forced marriages, lack of sexual health education, and inadequate support mechanisms. Further, the government had also imposed restrictions on pregnant girls from attending school curtailing the scope to complete their education which was only recently overturned in 2020.

A UNESCO study cites that in 2017 almost a third of girls who dropped out of school did so because of pregnancy. The study however, also highlights an encouraging trend based on analysis of the country’s tertiary institution census; the share of women enrolled in tertiary education has increased from 43% to 48 between 2017 to 2019, and the gender gap in access rate (number of students per 100,000 inhabitants) has halved from 350 to 159. Further, the study points out that a majority of students enrolled in Technical, Vocation Education and Training (TVET) are women (54%), and 39% of Science, Technology, Engineering, Agriculture, and Mathematics (STEAM) students are women. However, only one in five women take up STEAM studies in contrast with nearly one in three men, and enrollment to a variety of TVET courses is highly gendered with women concentrated in service sector training courses (like tailoring, catering, and hairdressing) compared to men in manufacturing and construction related training. To encourage STEM education, the Government has established a grant-in-aid policy for providing scholarships to all students studying these subjects.

## Women’s economic participation and entrepreneurship

While World Bank estimates point to similar LFPR among men (58%) and women (59%) aged 15 to 64, the country’s DHS points to a disparity with men more likely to be employed than women (72% versus 69). Although the trends around women’s tertiary and technical education are encouraging, these don’t necessarily translate to women’s transition to gainful employment opportunities, where existing disparities and discrimination undermine young women’s competitive position compared to men. In 2018 for instance, the Employment-to-Population Ratio for young women with tertiary education is 10 percentage points lower than young men’s, and the gap widens to 12 percentage points in higher education. Tertiary educated women who are NEET (not in employment, education, or training) is 24% compared to 17% men. However, any form of vocational education significantly increases women’s ability to start looking for work, much more effectively than men; with young female NEETs who received any formal vocational training relatively much less subject to idleness (defined by a state where they are not even looking for work) (58%) than their male counterparts (74).

Sectorally, women are majorly concentrated in agriculture (54%) and sales (37%) jobs, and are less likely, compared to men, to be in skilled manual work (4% versus 9%) or in managerial/technical/professional roles (3% versus 14%). Women’s position as workers remains dismal, as an extremely high percentage of women in agricultural work (69%) are unpaid and 31% of women in non-agricultural work remain unpaid.

# Gender and energy in Sierra Leone

Electrification rates in Sierra Leone are low compared to other countries in the region. Only a small portion of the population in Sierra Leone has access to electricity, estimated to be between 26.2% (World Bank, 2020) and 23% (DHS, 2020). About 64.4% of cooking fuel comes from wood, and 32.6% is charcoal, putting women at a high risk of smoke inhalation and respiratory diseases.

Sierra Leone is a part of ECOWAS and is implementing their policies for gender mainstreaming in Energy Access. Under ECOWAS, The Ministry of Energy is responsible for supervising the Barefoot Solar Engineering Training Centre, established to undertake rural electrification projects and to provide solar training to illiterate women.

The country also has a Barefoot Women Solar Engineers Association of Sierra Leone, started by women trained at Barefoot college India on the installation and maintenance of solar PV systems, and installation of solar pumps. While these women are not formally educated, they have been able to play a critical role in last-mile access to energy for households in 32 towns and villages.

The Barefoot women have also provided solar connections to 60 community clinics in northern Sierra Leone, which earlier lacked continuous electricity, putting lives at risk, especially making childbirth risky at these clinics. With solar panels, Barefoot women have been able to ensure cheap, clean, and stable connections thus making institutional deliveries at these facilities much safer for women.

Between 2014 to 2017, 50 solar women technicians were trained under this program and solar PV systems were installed in 3,500 Households.

Among off-grid energy providers operating in Sierra Leone, the women-led company Smiling Through Light founded by Mariamma Kamara is leading the way in deploying women to provide clean energy access through the distribution and sale of solar products, focusing on the last mile. The company has a 100% female sales agent force and 80% of the entire organization including leadership is made up of women.

Smiling Through Light has trained 1000 individuals in Sierra Leone on clean energy access using a storytelling approach and provided solar lamps to 400 school children in off-grid communities under their 'One Child, One Lamp' campaign.

# Guinea

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Guinea is a country in West Africa with vast nature reserves. The country has a population of 13.9 million people, with a young and adolescent population of 34% between the age group of 10 to the age of 24 years. The fertility rate in the country, at 4.4, is very high compared to some other countries in the West African region.

The Gender Development Index in Guinea is 0.850 and the government is placed with ‘Group 5’ countries with a high absolute deviation from gender parity, of more than 10%. While the country does not have a very high difference between men’s and women’s income, the high deviation is potentially a result of high gender gap in mean years of schooling with 1.3 years among females and 3.2 years among males.

Guinea has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.621 and is ranked 157, which measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.

### Legal status of women

Guinea, similar to many other Sub-Saharan and West African countries recognizes customary law along with statutory laws. This, in some areas of social and cultural life, impedes women's ability to exercise their agency as equal citizens, especially in the inheritance of wealth and land, where while statutory law grants women 1/8th of wealth and equal rights to land, customary law prevents women from claiming these.

In the international arena, Guinea has committed to gender equality through ratification of various UN and regional conventions, such as; the Convention on the Elimination of all forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) in 1982, the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women to

"ensure the elimination of all discrimination against women and ensure the protection of women's rights as stated in international declarations and conventions", the Draft Supplementary Act on Equality of Rights between Women and Men for Sustainable Development within the ECOWAS Region, the Draft ECOWAS Plan of Action on Gender and Trade, the Draft ECOWAS Gender and Migration Framework and Plan of Action; and the Common Gender Policy of the West African Economic and Monetary Union (WAEMU) in 2016.

The Constitution of Guinea was amended in 2001 to put in place a regulatory environment that is non-discriminatory and supports gender equality. Further, in 2020, the constitution was amended to specify the age of marriage for girls at 18, and include "physical integrity" as part of their rights. However, various Civil Codes reinforce men’s familial authority by recognizing them as the sole head of the household, with sole guardianship of children and Guinean women cannot transfer citizenship to children through marriage to men outside the country.

However, certain limiting legal restrictions curtail women’s full participation in the Guinean economy, such as women having to seek their husband's approval to pursue a trade or profession of their choice.

## Political participation

As per the Global Gender Gap report, there are 29.6% women in parliament in Guinea compared to 70.4% men. A recent report from 2021 by the country’s first woman Economy and Finance Minister suggests that Guinea has close to 31% of ministerial portfolios being held by women as of 2021, which is about 9% higher than the average from the Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS) countries and there has been a 57% increase in the number of women in politics between 2010 and 2021. The report however notes that while more women have joined public life, their representation in decision-making positions is still very low, with only 25 women hold holding directorial positions, compared to 67% having intermediary positions such as division and section heads.

While Guinea has Gender Focal Points designated in all Ministries to undertake gender-responsive planning and budgeting, research suggests that GFPs (often women in low-level positions) do not have clear mandates and find it difficult to navigate and assert their authority in male-dominated ministerial networks.

Further, many studies point to the ineffective civil registration system in Guinea, due to which women are unable to register for identification proofs, curtailing their ability to participate in public and political life. Further, mobility restrictions and safety concerns during political demonstrations, spousal permissions required, time poverty, and low education rates all limit women’s ability to effectively contribute.

## Social and cultural context

Customary and religious practices in Guinea severely limit women’s ability to realize their rights as citizens 
of Guinea. Within marriage and families, women’s position is undermined under traditional and customary 
law. Child marriage is also rampant in the country with 46.5% of girls married off before the age of 18.²⁵⁵ 
Even though polygamy is prohibited under statutory law,²⁵⁶ among married women in age 15 to 49, 42% of 
women are in polygamous unions.²⁷ Guinea also has one of the world’s highest rates of female genital 
mutilation (FGM) with 94.5% of women in age 15 -49 reporting that they have experienced FGM,²⁸ 
traditionally regarded as an important socio-cultural rite of passage for initiation of girls into adulthood and 
marriage which is ceremoniously celebrated. On the other hand, patriarchal norms dictate that women must 
be chaste before marriage, and forced marriage (especially for widows) is common. At the community level, 
there is judicial inaction on FGM, leading to the continued practice of FGM. And while there have been 
increased awareness drives to educate people on legal sanctions imposed under the law on FGM, this has 
only led to people forgoing celebratory rituals to conduct FGM more privately.²⁵⁹,²⁶⁰  

## Health

The state of reproductive agency among women is poor in Guinea. The high rate of FGM highlighted in the 
previous section is also coupled with inadequate poor maternal care women receive. While at 576, Guinea 
has a lower MMR than some other countries in the region, Guinea has a long way to go to reach the SDG 
target of less than 70 by 2030. Close to 28% of all female deaths in the country are maternal  deaths.²⁶¹ 
This could be due to a variety of factors, such as high rates of anemia among women, 46% of women in 
the 15 -49 age group are anemic, limited usage of and access to modern contraception, with 36% of 
unmarried sexually active women stating that th eir contraception needs are unmet and 22% of all women 
reporting unmet family planning needs.²⁶²

The country has rural second-chance education schools known as NAFA centers that cater to school dropouts or those that have never attended schools, supporting them to gain literacy and numeracy skills and as of 2019 there were 13,000 students enrolled in these centers.²⁶⁷

## Women’s economic participation and entrepreneurship

As per the country’s DHS, the Labour force participation rate among women is 66% while among men it is 81%. Agriculture, animal husbandry, and fishing or hunting are the main sectors of employment for both men and women. Interestingly however, unlike a lot of other Sub-Saharan African nations, 64% of women in Guinea are self-employed in agriculture instead of working as family members on farms. However, only 29% of women working in agriculture were remunerated in cash.²⁶⁸ Women’s agricultural work is 8 percentage points less productive than men's in Guinea due to disparity in land ownership and men being much more likely to use farm inputs such as fertilizer, irrigation, and high-quality seeds.²⁶⁹ Among women, there has been a steep decline in the proportion of those employed in agriculture, from 52% to 37%. There has been a transition of women from agriculture to sales professions, with 32% of women working in sales and services.²⁷⁰

Culturally, entrepreneurship is not seen as a respectable work avenue for women in Guinea.²⁷¹ Regionally, Guinea is much behind average in terms of female entrepreneurship with only 9% of firms having female founders, compared to 29% in the Sub-Saharan African region, as per the 2016 World Bank Enterprise Survey for Guinea (conducted for formal enterprises, and excluding microenterprises, where women are largely concentrated). Further, about 25% of enterprises in the country had no female employees.²⁷²

As per Global Findex women are behind men by 13% points in bank account ownership, with 24% of women having accounts, compared to 37% of men. Similarly, in terms of mobile money account holding, women are behind men with 17% of female having such accounts compared to 26% of men.²⁷³ While there has been significant growth of MFIs and fintech in the country, which could be a way to tackle gendered norms and empower women financially, tedious KYC processes, power connections in the hinterland, and weak

# Gender and energy in Guinea

Currently, only 44% of households in Guinea have access to electricity, and only 5% of the population has access to clean cooking technologies. In Guinea, cooking is predominantly done outside the home, and 60% of households use wood for cooking.

Among notable initiatives in the country, the Women's Fund for Gender Equality (FGE) developed a training program on renewable energy technologies to promote sustainable livelihoods for women and in partnership with a local grantee organization Partenariat - Recherches - Environnement - Médias (PREM), trained 300 women in Guinea's Tristao Islands how to plant, grow, harvest, and sell products made from moringa plants using solar polytunnel dryers. This resulted in 25,000 moringa trees being planted and greenhouse gas emissions being reduced by 40 tonnes as a result of solar drying technology, between 2013 to 2016.

Currently, there is an ongoing Research Project on Energy Transition for Women's Economic Empowerment through the Horticultural Value Chain in Guinea, to understand and address mechanisms for use of solar irrigation systems in the horticultural value chain, for irrigation, processing, and preservation. The project which aims to improve the profitability and working time of rural women by 2025, is also building the capacity of women to use solar technologies and access finance. The program is being implemented by the Initiative prospective agricole et rurale (IPAR) in collaboration with the Centre d'étude et de coopération internationale (CECI) and is funded by the International Development Research Centre (IDRC).

# Burundi

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Burundi is a country in East Africa, south of the Equator. The country has a population of 12.6 million people and one of Africa's highest population densities. Burundi has a young and adolescent population of 32% between the age group of 10 to 24. The fertility rate in the country stands at 5.1.

The Gender Development Index in Burundi is 0.935, and the country is placed with 'Group 3' countries with medium equality in HDI achievements between women and men, with an absolute deviation between 5% and 7.5%. Although there is a gender gap in mean years of schooling, with 2.5 years among females and 3.9 years among males, there is a relatively low difference between women and men in the estimated gross national income per capita, compared to other East African countries ($668 for women and $797 for men).

Burundi has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.505 and is ranked 127. The index measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.

## Legal status of women

Several national and international policy instruments are in place in Burundi to advance gender equality.

Burundi ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1992 and the Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC) in 1990.

As a member of the African Union (AU), the AU's Gender Equality and Women's Empowerment 2018-2028 (GEWE) strategy is part of Burundi's regional regulatory framework. The GEWE strategy focuses on four key pillars, namely:

- Maximizing opportunities, outcomes and E-TECH dividends
- Dignity, security and resilience
- Effective laws, policies, and institutions

# 53

## Leadership, voice, and visibility

The Burundian government also adopted the Optional Protocol to the Convention on the Rights of the Child on the Sale of Children, Child Prostitution, and Child Pornography in November 2007, reinforcing the legal protections from sexual violence and exploitation for women and girls.

Within the sub-region, the Pact on Peace and Security in the Great Lakes region was adopted during the International Conference on the Great Lakes region in 2006, which established measures to address violence against women and children through its Protocol on the Prevention and Suppression of Sexual Violence Against Women and Children.

Regarding the national legislation, the Constitution of the Republic of Burundi (2005, Art 13) recognizes gender equality stating that 'All Burundians are born equal in merit and dignity. All citizens enjoy the same rights and are entitled to the same protection by the Law. No Burundian shall be excluded from the social, economic, or political life of the nation by reason of race, language, religion, sex, or ethnic origin'.

The Public Administration Statute (2006) prohibits discrimination in recruitment, pay or dismissals based on sex, in line with the Labour Code. Recently (2020 – 2021), Burundi mandated equal remuneration for work of equal value.

## Political participation

The 2005 Constitution sets a quota of 30% to ensure the participation of women in decision-making bodies such as the National Assembly and government (Art. 129 and 164) to address women's underrepresentation in public life. This milestone was achieved through the leaders of women's associations' advocacy at the time of the Arusha Agreement.

According to the 2022 Global Gender Gap report, women hold 38.2% of the seats in Parliament and 37.5% of the ministerial positions. Burundi had its first women Prime Ministry, Sylvie Kinig in 1994.

## Social and cultural context

Burundi is characterized as a patriarchal society where women have very little decision-making power compared with men. These norms determine power dynamics between men and women from an early age and are reflected in the high rates of physical and economic exploitation of women.

Women's primary responsibility in Burundi is considered to be the care of the household, which results in carrying out a disproportionate amount of the burden of domestic work, leaving them with little time to accomplish anything.

Women’s rights over land are governed by customary law, which discriminates against women. In the context of a patriarchal culture such as the Burundian, “women do not inherit the land, they are only usufructuaries and their right to use it depends on the goodwill of their parents, brothers and sometimes their in-laws.298 This is particularly problematic for women because it leaves them unprotected in times of hardship or when they are abandoned or widowed. 299  

GBV is a pervasive problem in Burundi. Nearly close to half of the women in the country (46.7%) have suffered intimate partner physical and/or sexual violence. 300 Domestic violence “is a mainstream expectation within family life and something which is seen as a necessity and, to a certain extent, as a good thing. It reflects the woman’s status as being inferior”. 301 Economic violence is also common in Burundi. It is exerted by prohibiting victims from accessing or using household assets, the crops harvested by them and, controlling their access to health care, employment, and excluding them from the household expenditure decision-making.302  

## Health  

Women of reproductive age (15-49 years) often face barriers with respect to their sexual and reproductive health and rights: in 2017, 39.6% of women had their need for family planning satisfied with modern methods.303 The adolescent birth rate is 58.2 per 1,000 women aged 15-19 as of 2015, down from 84.5 per 1,000 in 2011. HIV/AIDS is also a problem that disproportionately affects women in Burundi. There are 85,00 people in Burundi living with HIV, from which 45,000 are adult women and 30,000 are adult men.304  

## Education  

Gender norms in Burundi limit women's access to continued education, resulting in a higher rate of illiteracy in women. 305 While 68% of women aged 15 and above are literate, 81% of their male counterparts are literate. 306 Families educate girls emphasizing the productive and reproductive roles that women must assume. Girls are also expected to look after their younger siblings and help with domestic and agricultural work.307 This unequal division of labour in the household burdens young women, limiting their time to study, while young men have time for studying and gaining vocational skills. Girls are far more likely to be withdrawn from schools and retained at home. Some reasons for girls' premature withdrawal from school

Women’s economic participation and entrepreneurship  
Women in Burundi play an active role in the agricultural sector, which provides 90% of the country’s food 
production.309 They constitute 55% of the agricultural labour and carry out 70% of farm work. Despite 
women producing most of the food crops used for family consumption, they often do not have the decision-
making power over the use of the harvest.310  

Women are highly involved in informal sectors, particularly in rural areas, which generally require low 
qualifications, rather than in the trade and commerce sector, which are better paid. 311 Further, Burundian 
women are expected to carry out a disproportionate amount of the burden of household work, limiting their 
opportunities to find employment or start a business.  

Regarding laws that promote or hinder women's entrepreneurship, there are no differences between the 
opportunities women and men have to start a business since both can sign a contract, register a business 
and open a bank account on unequal terms. However, Burundian law still does not prohibit discrimination 
in access to credit based on gender, hindering women’s ability to pursue entrepreneurial activities. 312  

## Gender and energy in Burundi

Only 11.7% of the population in Burundi has access to electricity313, a rate four times lower than the average 
in Sub-Saharan Africa of 48.2%,314, and access to clean and modern energy in Burundi remains extremely 
low.315 Almost all cooking relies on traditional fuels (99% of the households), and around 90% of rural 
households depend on conventional three-stone stoves.316  

Gender norms and gender dynamics play an important role in purchasing energy - related products and 
appliances within households. Women are often the primary users of energy appliances, and the use of 
fuels and technologies may directly impact their health. However, they may not be able to decide to buy 
cleaner energy technologies since many of them do not have control over their earnings and household 
purchases.317  

Some of the gender -focused programs implemented in the region include the Lumière program, 
implemented by local NGO FVS-Amade in partnership with UNICEF. Active since 2015, FVS-Amade works 
with Village Savings and Loan Associations (VSLAs) to serve the last mile consumers with clean energy

# Togo  
## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country  

Togo is a country in western Africa. The country has a population of 8.7 million people, with a young and adolescent population of 32% between the age group of 10 to the age of 24 years. The fertility rate in the country stands at 4.1.  

The Gender Development Index in Togo is 0.849, and the country is placed in ‘Group 5’, countries with low equality in HDI achievements between women and men, with a high absolute deviation from gender parity of more than 10%. This is primarily driven by a gender gap in mean years of schooling, with 3.4 years among females and 6.8 years among males.  

Togo has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.580 and is ranked 149. The index measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.

### Legal status of women  

In the international arena, Togo ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1983 but has not yet ratified the Optional Protocol on violence against women.  

The country also ratified the Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC) in 1990, the Beijing Platform for Action in 1995, and the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (the Maputo Protocol) in 2005.  

Togo incorporated the principle of gender equality in the Constitution in 1992, which states: “All human beings are equal in dignity and in right. The man and the woman are equal before the law. No one may be favoured or disadvantaged for the reason of their familial,

## Ethnic, or regional origin, of their economic or social situation, of their political, religious, philosophical or other convictions (Art. 2)

Following the recommendations of CEDAW, Togo enacted the reforms in 2012 and 2014 to the Person's and Family Code, making significant progress towards gender equality. Before the reform, the Family Code restricted women's rights by not granting a woman an equal right to choose where to live, to get a job without her husband's permission, and to be named head of household.

However, despite these progresses, the Women, Business and the Law (WBL) 2022 report notes a setback for Togo in terms of gender equality with the enactment of a new Labor Code in 2021, that no longer broadly prohibits the dismissal of pregnant workers leading to a decrease in its WBL score from 84.4 in 2021 to 81.9 in 2022.

## Political participation

Over the years, the participation of women in politics has increased, with women occupying high-level government positions, including the Prime Minister's Office, the National Assembly, and the Ministry of Defense. However, women remain under-represented at the upper levels of power and decision-making: in the Executive, the Legislature, and the Judiciary and the control of information. Women hold only 18.7% of the National Assembly seats and 31% of the ministerial positions. Togo currently has a woman Prime Minister, Victoire Tomegah Dogbé, who has held the position since September 28, 2020, and is the first woman holding the position.

## Social and cultural context

Despite the progress made in recent years in Togo on the legal status of women and their political participation, culture, religion, and strong traditions still allow discriminatory practices against women. While the practice of child marriage has declined in recent years, this practice still occurs in some regions of the country. According to the latest Global Gender Gap report, Togo has an early marriage rate of 13.8% of women. This practice is partly due to cultural factors such as the dowry that can be an economic motivation for low-income families to get girls married off early or for strengthening bonds between families. Both the Children's Code and the Personal and Family Code set the minimum age of marriage at 18 years, and the Law on Reproductive Health 2007 prohibits forced marriage (Art. 11, 2007).

The Civil Law in Togo establishes that marriages can be monogamous or polygamous (Personal and Family Code, Art.42). Women still face discrimination regarding family laws in Togo, for instance, women have to wait 300 days to remarry, unlike men that do not have to wait. Further, there is no legislation specifically addressing domestic violence in Togo. Widows also face discrimination and violence in Togo. Despite existing specific protections under the civil law for widows that prohibit degrading mourning rites, such as tests to make a woman prove she didn’t kill her husband, deny her access to food and any income-

# 59

generating activity, and prohibit widows from any inheritance, unfortunately, these rituals persist in a majority of tribal, rural communities.338  

In terms of inheritance, women are not entitled to inherit from their husbands or fathers under customary law in Togo. However, civil law provides both women and men equal rights to inheritance (Personal and Family Code), although it also stipulates that these rights may be waived if the spouses opt for customary rules in matters of succession (Art. 404).339 Further, civil law grants equal rights and access to land among women and men, but customary law often excludes women or limits their access to land.340 In fact, traditional practices relating to land ownership vary across ethnic groups. In some cases, women can only purchase land through a man, while in other areas, women are excluded from ownership altogether.341  

Gender-Based Violence (GBV) is another problem that hinders gender equality. In Togo, 22.1% of ever-partnered women have suffered intimate partner physical and/or sexual.342 Some of the Togolese government’s efforts to reduce the risk of GBV include the adoption of the Politique Nationale de l’ Equité et de l’Egalité de Genre du Togo in 2011, a policy to establish an institutional, sociocultural, legal, and an economic environment favorable to the achievement of gender equity and equality in Togo; and to address forms of violence against women.343 FGM is also prohibited in the country since 1998 through Act No. 98-016, which is also considered a harmful practice and prohibited in the Law on Reproductive Health 2007 and the Child Code 2007.344  

Despite the existence of a legal framework for FGM, there is still a lot of work to be done in terms of education and sensitization. A government study found that more than 20% of the population believes that it is acceptable and justifiable for a man to be violent against his wife.345  

## Health

Togo’s national health policy, adopted in 1996 and revised in 1997 includes improving women’s and children’s access to primary health care by providing improved geographical coverage as one of its key objectives.346 Togo started taking into account the reproductive health component, and the gender dimension was mainstreamed in it, following the recommendations of the International Conference on Population and Development held in Cairo in 1994.347  

Despite having equal rights to use contraception, women of reproductive age (15-49 years) in Togo face barriers to accessing sexual and reproductive health information and services. In 2017, only 29.6% of women made informed decisions regarding sexual relations and about 39.6% of women had their need for

# Togo: Country Fact Sheet

## Family Planning and Reproductive Health

Women in rural areas have less access to reproductive health services, and men partners can be against their wife’s use of contraception. Abortion is legal in cases of rape or incest if the fetus is impaired or to save the pregnant woman’s life.

## HIV/AIDS

In recent years, Togo has increased its efforts to fight HIV/AIDS due to its rapid spread. In the country, 1.9% of the total population (% of the population ages 15-49) has HIV, of which 65% are women.

As part of these efforts, the National AIDS Programme (PNLS) was reinforced in September 2001 with a permanent secretariat of a National Council to combat AIDS and other sexually transmitted infections. The Joint United Nations Programme on HIV/AIDS (UNAIDS) has supported Togo in its fight against HIV/AIDS.

## Education

One of Togo’s most significant challenges in terms of education is reducing the existing gender gaps. Only 55% of women aged 15 and above are literate, compared to 80% of men.

Many women reportedly drop out of school due to pregnancy, and there is a prioritizing of boys’ education over girls. According to UNESCO, the lower secondary completion gap between boys and girls is 10.7, more significant than the gap in the Sub-Saharan Africa aggregate, which is 4.7.

The Togolese government has made progress in reducing the gender gaps through the implementation of initiatives focused on girls’ education, some of these include:

- Togo joined the Global Partnership for Education in 2010 and received support to implement multilevel interventions tackling school-level and institutional obstacles and harmful social norms affecting girls throughout their education to strengthen their school enrollment.
- Under this initiative, Togo received a 7 million COVID-19 grant from the World Bank, one of its key objectives included supporting girls with targeted back-to-school campaigns through sensitization programs on early marriage, gender-based violence, early pregnancies, and the importance of continuing to learn.
- The country has also developed an education strategy for 2014 to 2025 to “develop a quality basic education to achieve universal primary education by 2022.”

## Women’s Economic Participation and Entrepreneurship

In Togo, women’s participation in the labor force has remained roughly the same since 1990. In 2021, women’s labor force participation rate was 55.5%, compared to 59.4% for men.

# 61

primarily engaged in the informal sector, where 90% of workers are women, which directly impacts their ability to access the benefits of formal employment.

In the past decades, Togo has made significant progress in supporting women's entrepreneurship and economic participation. The country has enacted reforms under all eight indicators in the WBL index (Mobility, Workplace, Pay, Marriage, Parenthood, Entrepreneurship, Assets, and Pension). One of the most significant reforms was the enactment of the 2006 Labor Code that prohibited the dismissal of pregnant workers, mandated equal pay for the same work, and prohibited gender discrimination in employment.

One of the latest setbacks regarding labor laws was enacting a new labor code that no longer broadly prohibits the dismissal of pregnant workers.

Under civil law in Togo, women and men are granted equal access and participation in all spheres of life, including economic life. This right allows women and men to open individual bank accounts and access formal financial services such as a line of credit or financial loan, regardless of marital status. But there are gender gaps in access to financial services and products because of barriers that women typically face, such as lack of collateral. Married under the marriage community of property regime are prohibited from providing such guarantees without their husbands' consent.

Regarding leadership and entrepreneurship, according to the Global Gender Gap 2022 report, only 11.4% of the firms in Togo have female top managers, and 10.7% have a majority of women ownership. The same report highlights that Togo has the highest share of women in senior roles, with 70.1%. Further, the percentage of women business owners has increased in the last two decades, from 22% in 2014 to 32% in 2020.

Women in Togo spend disproportionately more time on unpaid care work than men, which negatively impacts the time they can spend pursuing formal economic activities. As of 2016, women entrepreneurs in Togo spent 19.3 hours per week on care work compared to 8.8 for male entrepreneurs.

## Gender and energy in Togo

Only half (54%) of the Togolese population has access to electricity. The country has a relatively diversified energy mix, and more than 13% of its final energy consumption comes from renewable supplies of energy, mainly hydropower. In Togo, as in other countries of West Africa, women are the most vulnerable and affected by energy poverty and its effects. They are the most exposed to health risks related to the use of polluting fuels they are responsible for cooking. This burden also limits their participation in other activities, especially income-generating activities, causing a vicious circle by not having the means or

# Malawi

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Malawi is a South-East African country bordering Mozambique, Zambia and Tanzania. The country has a total population of 20.2 million people, with a demographic dividend skewed toward the young and adolescent population (between the age group of 10 to the age of 24 years), who make up 34% of the population. The fertility rate in the country stands at 3.9 which is very high compared to regional averages.

The Gender Development Index in Malawi is 0.968 and the country is placed among 'Group 2' countries with an absolute deviation from gender parity between 2.5 percent and 5 percent. The disparity in mean years of schooling for females (4.1 years) and males (4.7 years) is lower compared to other countries in the region.

Malawi has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.554 and is ranked 142, which measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.

  World Bank, 2019

  UNDP (2022)

## Legal status of women

Malawi's constitution of 1994 upholds gender equality, prohibiting gender-based discrimination under Article 20 and accords women the same rights as men to enter contracts, maintain property, retain citizenship and custody of children.

Further, the Constitution principally outlines gender considerations for national policy development and adoption of legislation to obtain gender equality through "(i) full participation of women in all spheres of Malawian society on the basis of equality with men; (ii) the implementation of the principles of non-discrimination and such other measures as may be required; and (iii) the implementation of policies to address social issues such as domestic violence, security of the person, lack of maternity benefits, economic exploitation and rights to property."

The country has also demonstrated its commitment to furthering gender equality by ratifying international treaties such as the

# Convention on the Elimination of all forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) in 1987 and the CRC - Convention on the Rights of the Child in 1991

Malawi is also a signatory to The Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (2005), The Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (BPfA), and the Southern African Development Community (SADC) Protocol on Gender and Development.

## Gender Equality Act

The country has adopted the Gender Equality Act as a Law in 2013 with the aim to “promote gender equality, equal integration, influence, empowerment, dignity, and opportunities, for men and women in all functions of society, to prohibit and provide redress for sex discrimination, harmful practices and sexual harassment, to provide for public awareness on the promotion of gender equality, and to provide for connected matters” with a focus on Human Rights, Employment, Education, Sexual and Reproductive Health and Civic Awareness.

## National Gender Policy

Malawi’s Ministry of Gender, Children, and Social Welfare consolidated a National Gender Policy in 2015, with the goal to reduce gender inequalities in priority areas including; education and training; health; agriculture, food security, and nutrition; natural resources, environment, and climate change management; economic development; governance and human rights.

### Objectives identified by the policy:

1. To advocate for increased access, retention, and completion to quality education for girls and boys.
2. To ensure women, men, boys, and girls' sexual and reproductive health rights, and HIV AIDS status are improved.
3. To strengthen gender mainstreaming in all sectors of the economy.
4. To reduce poverty among women and other vulnerable groups (Orphans, widows, PLHA, persons with disabilities, and the elderly) through economic empowerment.
5. To promote women’s participation in decision-making positions in both politics and public life.
6. To reduce gender-based violence.
7. To strengthen the capacity of the National Gender Machinery.

The policy commitment is complemented by a Monitoring and Evaluation Plan and has been developed in alignment with various other national policies such as the country’s larger Growth and Development Strategy, Reproductive Health Policy, Education Policy, Agricultural and Environmental policies as well as the National Youth Policy.

## Political Participation

Malawi boasts of electing the second African female president, Joyce Banda (from 2012-2014). However, the country still lags behind in women’s political participation.

As per the Global Gender Gap report, 23% of the parliament is composed of women, compared to 71% of men. The gap widens for ministerial leadership, with 17.39% of women and 82.61% of men holding ministerial positions.

# Studies have suggested that Malawian voters view women as less credible leaders than men, expecting 
women to conform to gender norms, however, women are also held to a mu ch higher standard than men 
parliamentarians. There is also a relegation of women to fixed roles within the party ranks, commonly, the 
‘women’s wings’ which do not have a clear mandate within political parties. Further, women candidates 
face discrimination, negative campaigning, and increasing expenses during campaigning, which causes 
high turnover among women candidates. 387 The country is yet to take affirmative action on women’s 
participation in politics, with no legislative quotas for women’s representation.388 

## Social and cultural context 
Malawi’s social life is dictated by customary law which is patriarchal in tradition. While the country has areas 
with matrilinear communities, social norms across these still continue to favor men. Women also have 
limited access to productive resources like agricultural plots, and women -managed agricultural plots are 
also smaller than that of men (by 12%) with 25% low productivity as a result of limited access to farm 
inputs.389 The incidence of violence against wome n is very high in Malawi with 37.5 % of women having 
experienced physical and/or sexual intimate partner violence in their lifetime.390 This figure has continuously 
risen between 2004 to 2016 from 15% to 33%. 391 This continues even as the country’s National Plan of 
Action to combat Gender -Based Violence (GBV) provides an overarching framework to prevent GBV and 
the Domestic Violence Act (2006) underlines the penalization of violent offenses against women. 392 The 
country also has one of the world’s highest rates of child marriage, with 42% of girls married before the age 
of 18, and 9% being married below the age of 15. 393 To tackle the pervasive issue of child marriage, the 
country has developed a National Strategy t o Eliminate Child Marriage, which aims to reduce the 
prevalence of child marriage in Malawi by 20% by 2023. The implementation of the strategy is to be 
coordinated by a multi -sectoral task force and through the establishment of community child protection 
committees, community victim support units (CVSUs), Community-Based Child Care Centres (CBCCs) and 
Children’s Corners (CCs).394

## Maternal Mortality and Healthcare

The maternal mortality rate in Malawi stands at 349 per 100,000 live births. 395 The country has seen great progress in expanding women’s access to institutional care, and as per the country’s last DHS, Malawi has a very high rate of institutional deliveries, increasing significantly from m 55% in 1992 to 91% in 2015 -16. Although only half (51%) of women had at least 4 ANC check ups in their last pregnancy.396 Further, as per the country’s fifth Integrated Household Survey (IHS5), 68% of women were assisted by nurses or midwives while 28% were assisted by doctors or clinical officers. 397 There is also high access to family planning methods among women, with a lower unmet need reported among all women (13%) as compared to other countries in the region.398 A majority of married Malawian women users of family planning methods report making a joint decision regarding family planning methods along with their husbands. However, there has been no increase in the median age at first birth, since 1992, which is currently hovering at 18.9 years. 399  

The incidence of HIV among women in Malawi is high at 27.5%, and along with complications arising during pregnancy, they are the leading cause of death among girls of age 15-19 in Malawi.400 

## Education

Malawi has a universal primary education policy that is free for all. 401 As per the Integrated Household Survey, 62.6% of females are literate. 402 The high incidence of child marriage among Malawians also reflects attitudes toward girls’ education. Among dropouts from secondary school, 32% of females mentioned marriage being the primary reason they had to drop out. 403 There is a gender gap of 6.87 percent in the secondary education enrollment rate in Malawi as per the Global Gender Gap report (2022).404 Access to higher education beyond secondary schooling is limited in Malawi among both men and women (above the age of 15), with only 1.2% of women having tertiary education qualifications, and men having a somewhat higher rate of tertiary education at 2.7%.405  

The country has developed a long-term National Educational sector Investment plan to improve enrollment and quality of education in the country and a particular objective to increase equity in secondary education with a particular focus on girls. To enhance girls’ education attainment, the country has introduced affirmative action by mandating 50-50 selection criteria in secondary schools, construction of girls' hostels, provision of bursaries, and provision of health, nutrition, physical education, and sports. In Technical, Entrepreneurial, and Vocational Education and Training, a code of conduct has been introduced to combat gender-based violence (GBV).406  

The Ministry of Labour, Youth, Sports, and Manpower Development (MoLYSMD) has also released The Malawi TEVET Policy, and the first priority area under this policy is to increase access and equity in Malawi’s TEVET system and increase current levels of enrolment of female students and vulnerable students.

## Women’s economic participation and entrepreneurship

While Malawi has a comparatively high Female Labour Force Participation in the sub-region, their conditions of work are far from ideal. As per the country’s Fifth Integrated Household Survey (2019-20), 91.8% of women in Malawi between the ages 15-64 are engaged in income-generating activities. The highest proportion of women (81.7%) in Malawi have engaged in agriculture or fishing activities and wage work is the least common with only 6.6% of women engaged in it. Wage work is conducted by men more commonly (10.6%), and casual work employs a larger proportion of working women (58%) than men (47%). Unpaid work is also more common among women, with the 2015-16 DHS revealing that 59% of women are not paid for their work compared to 26% of men. There is also a gender pay gap in Malawi, with women earning only 64 cents per dollar paid to men. The Integrated Household Survey also finds that across the board, women wage earners work fewer hours, and spend more time on non-market activities such as household chores.

As per the Global Gender Gap report, only 14.2% of firms in the country have female top managers, and only 11.4% of firms have female majority ownership. While women entrepreneurship is proportionately high in Malawi, they are relegated to non-IT sectors and access to resources and inputs are key barriers limiting their productivity. As per IHS 5, the most common form of non-farm enterprise in Malawi is in the Wholesale and retail trade; Accommodation, and food service sectors. A higher percentage of enterprises in this sector is operated by female-headed households (71%) compared to men (58%). On the other hand, in the Transportation and storage; Information and communication sector, men hold a higher proportion of enterprises (10.8%) compared to women (4.5%).

A word bank analysis also found that women business managers’ sales are about 65% less than those of male managers, however, the difference reduces to 45% when controlled for household and enterprise-level factors which indicated that return on resources is the major reason for the gender gap. Further, the analysis found that while men are more likely to use saved-up agricultural income as start-up capital, women rely on gifts from friends or family.

As per World Bank Data, a very small minority of households in the country have access to electricity (14.9%) as of 2020. As per the country’s IHS, 79% of the population use firewood as the main source of

FINCOOP is a financial cooperative that provides affordable financial services, serving micro, small and medium entrepreneurs, farmers, and salaried employees. FINCOOP has tailored its payment terms to fit women’s requirements through the deployment of payment methods such as Pay As You Go models, cash, and group loans, and by offering longer repayment terms. # Lesotho

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Lesotho is a landlocked country in Southern Africa. The country has a total population of 2.2 million people, with a young and adolescent population (in the age group 10-24) of 29%. The fertility rate in the country stands at 3 per woman, which is lower compared to other countries in the continent.

The Gender Development Index in Lesotho is 0.985 and the country is placed among 'Group 1' countries with high equality in HDI achievements between women and men (an absolute deviation of less than 2.5 percent). Lesotho is the only country among those categorized as 'low Human Development Indexed countries' that falls in Group 1 of GDI and has higher mean years of schooling for females (6.6 years) than males (6.0 years).

Lesotho has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.557 and is ranked 144, which measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.

### Legal status of women

Lesotho achieved democracy in 1993 and the country's constitution's Bill of Rights enshrines non-discrimination and equality with respect to legislation, governance, sustainable development considerations, and democratic institutions of the State.

The country, like many others in the region, has a pluralistic legal system, recognizing customary law, Roman-Dutch Law (Common/Civil Law) along with Statutory Law. While the Statutory law under the Constitution forms the supreme law of the land and provides equal rights to men and women, customary and common laws often relegate women to minority status.

Internationally and regionally, Lesotho is a signatory to key conventions upholding women's rights including The Beijing Declaration and its Platform for Action (in 1995), and the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1995. To empower women, and

# 70

Lesotho has put in place legal frameworks such as The Legal Capacity of Married Persons Act, which entitles both men and women to the same rights and obligations regarding their marital duties, Land Act, and Companies Act which reinforces women’s rights to own property and control productive assets.

The country has a strong gender focus as part of its National Strategy Development Plan, and aims to mainstream gender across the 4 focus areas of the Plan including inclusive and sustainable economic growth and job creation, human capital strengthening, enabling infrastructure building as well as governance and accountability system strengthening.

The country also has an ambitious Gender and Development Policy outlining 12 gender objectives to be implemented through a rights-based, multi-sectoral approach. These objectives range from providing legal protection, political representation, and quality education access for women, to addressing issues related to gender and climate change as well as gender-based violence, providing food security, health, and sanitation access, and aiding women’s involvement in peacebuilding and media & ICT.

Lesotho has also charted out a robust mechanism for the implementation of this policy with cross-cutting strategies including effective research, coordination, capacity building of the governing machinery, and Gender Responsive Budgeting and financing.

The World Bank’s Women, Business, and Law index score Lesotho 78.1 out of 100. The country scores particularly low in the 'Parenthood' indicator since the country is yet to institute legislation mandating maternity leave for women. Further, the country does not have any provisions for criminal prosecution or civil penalties against sexual harassment faced by women in the workplace.

## Political participation

With a strongly articulated policy objective around gender and governance, Lesotho demonstrates a high commitment to ensuring women’s political participation. However, women still remain underrepresented in both national and local level political positions, with only 26.4% of women in the National Assembly and 24.2% in the Senate.

The Global Gender Gap report also highlights a gap between women and men’s representation in ministerial positions with women holding only 22% of positions.

Lesotho has introduced affirmative action with a 30% quota for women at the local council level and a 'zebra-list' model for the 40 proportional representation (PR) seats in the National Assembly where women must be listed alternatively with men, in submitted PR lists, thus granting 40 out of 120 seats in the National Assembly to women. However, regardless of these actions, there has been a steady decline in the political representation of women, falling from 58% to 40% between 2005 and 2017 in local councils, and from 27% in 2012 to 25% in 2015 in the National Assembly.

# Social and cultural context

The progressive nature of Lesotho’s institutional framework to address gender inequalities is evident in key legal amendments made by the country presented in the previous section, however, the country is still patriarchal, and Law of Inheritance Act 26 of 1873 has not reversed the Basotho customary law where the male child becomes the principal heir of his father’s property. Further, Gender Based Violence and Child Marriage are still pervasive in the country. As per the country’s Multiple Indicator Cluster Survey (MICS) from 2018, 19.4% of girls between the ages 20 -49 were married before the age of 18 and 2.1% were married before the age of 15. To reduce child marriages in the country, Lesotho launched a nationwide campaign on Child Marriage in 2016, to raise awareness of the impact of early marriages on girls, reinforcing the need to eradicate it. The Gender and Development Policy (2018-2030) has committed to zero tolerance towards all forms of violence against women and the country’s Sexual Offence Act (2003) also recognizes marital rape as an offense. Further, the country is also in the process of developing Counter Domestic Violence legislation that criminalizes different types of violence, with the support of UNFPA. Although even within this framework, some reports indicate that as many as 86% of women experience GBV in their lifetime.

# Health

Lesotho has faced multiple catastrophic droughts including the El Nino, which have led to crop failures, food shortage, violence, malnutrition, and displacement. These climate shocks often have a disproportionately adverse impact on women’s health as they disrupt care received by women. Data suggests that while the country expanded access to essential healthcare for women, quality provision of care remains a challenge. The maternal mortality rate in Lesotho stands at 544 per 100,000 live births. While the rate of institutional deliveries in the country is high (89.4%) and so is the rate of antenatal care (ANC) received by pregnant women, with 91% of women receiving at least ANC check ups at least once. The country still has a high rate of maternal deaths due to inadequate, inappropriate care received or negligence at the health facility level. Similarly, with respect to reproductive and sexual healthcare, compared to other countries in the region, Lesotho has a low rate of unmet need for family planning, with

# Lesotho

## Health

Lesotho also has one of the world's highest prevalence rates of HIV among women with 30.4% of women infected with HIV compared to 20.8% of men. This is due to unequal power relations as well as GBV, with a significantly higher prevalence in women who had been forced into sexual relationships (39.3%). In 2018, the country also set an ambitious target to end HIV/AIDS by 2030 under their National HIV & AIDS Strategic Plan 2018/19 – 2022/23 which focuses on innovation in interventions and delivery models, multi-sectoral coordination, and adoption of results-based planning language and accountability frameworks. The strategy has a strengthened focus on gender, with objectives around the reduction of Sexual and Gender-Based Violence, along with a reduction in gender inequalities and reducing cultural and normative barriers for women.

## Education

Lesotho has an overall high literacy rate among women compared with other countries in the region, and has a higher literacy rate among women than men. As per the country's 2018 MICS, 92% of women are literate with 56.2% having attended secondary education and 13.2% of women having attended higher education. Comparatively, only 76% of men are literate, with only 43% having attended secondary education and 12.2% having attended higher education.

However, at the tertiary education level, there is a gendered divide in STEM education, with the proportion of female students to male students being 0.4% in the faculty of Agriculture, Science, and Technology at the National University of Lesotho.

The country has a progressive education policy, under the Education Act (2010) which provides for free and compulsory primary education for girls and boys. The policy also underlines that girls who become pregnant while in school must be integrated back into schooling post after the delivery of their child.

The country's Gender and Development policy also recognizes the gendered dimension to choice of disciplines, with girls dominating 'soft disciplines', such as humanities, education, law, social sciences, crafts, and nutrition while the 'hard disciplines' such as sciences have more male students. To correct this imbalance, the policy categorically commits to establishing mechanisms "to encourage women, girls, and other vulnerable groups to study Science, Technology, Engineering, and Mathematics (STEM) subjects".

Technical, Vocational Education and Training courses in Lesotho are government-sponsored to enable girls to complete these trainings.
