# Women’s economic participation and entrepreneurship

As per Lesotho’s 2019 Labour Force Survey, the country has a female labor force participation rate (LFPR) of 45.3%, which is lower than other countries in the region and about 10 percentage points lower than LFPR of men (55%). The survey also highlights that most women are engaged in household-related activities as employers for undifferentiated goods and services -producing activities of the households for their own usage (25%) followed by the manufacturing sector (18%). Comparatively a high percentage of men were engaged in agriculture, forestry, and fishery-related activities (17.4%) and constriction (16). Further, the mean earnings for women are much lower than men, i.e. Maloti 3,377.22 and Malori 4,672.92 respectively.

As per the Global Gender Gap report 2022, there is also a wide gender gap with regards to the representation of women as legislators, senior officials, and managers, with only 33.7% women and 66.2% men as well as professional and technical workers, where women make up 39% workers.

To encourage women’s participation in the labour force, Lesotho underlines very ambitious policy goals under the Gender and Development policy, including (but not limited to) the expansion of women’s control over productive resources, including land, information, technology, safe water, energy and finance, harmonization of legislation and policy to provide better protection and working conditions for women, putting in place public procurement quotas to source directly from women businesses, as well as advocating a 30% quota for women in decision making positions in the private sector.

The country also has a Science and Technology policy from 2008, which aims to achieve high participation of women in science and technology-related work, including teaching, learning research, and development. Further, the policy places special focus on women entrepreneurs by providing them access to training, technical assistance, marketing, and export development support, as well as increased access to technology-based incubation. However, the successes of this policy are yet to be evaluated.

## Gender and energy in Lesotho

In Lesotho, the majority of households do not have access to electricity. As per the MICS, only 43.8% of people have access.

The Department of Energy along with the UNDP, have collaborated on a gender mainstreaming framework under the Sustainable Energy 4 All project with the following objectives:

1. To build an inclusive energy sector with institutions free of discrimination and inequality  
2. To build the capacity of women entrepreneurs to participate in the energy sector supply value chain  
3. To facilitate the integration of gender during the implementation of electrification projects.

The energy policy (2015-2025) recognises gender mainstreaming as one of its guiding principles and states that gender equality is an integral part of the formulation and implementation of energy programmes and activities.

# Burkina Faso

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Burkina Faso is a landlocked country in West Africa. It has a population of 22.1 million people, with a young and adolescent population of 33% between the age group of 10 to the age of 24 years. The fertility rate in the country stands at 4.9.

The Gender Development Index in Burkina Faso is 0.903, and the country is placed with 'Group 4' countries with medium to low equality in HDI achievements between women and men (absolute deviation of 7.5 to 10 percent). This is partly due to the country's significant gender gap in women's and men's estimated gross national income per capita, which is $921.

Burkina Faso has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.621 and is ranked 157, which measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.

### Legal status of women

At the international level, Burkina Faso ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) in 1987, including the Optional Protocol on violence against women, and the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa (the Maputo Protocol) in 2006.

The country also ratified the Convention on the Rights of the Child (CRC) in 1990, and signed the Beijing Platform for Action Conference recommendations and conclusions and committed to implementing them.

# 76

At the national level, the 1991 Constitution (Art. 1) prohibits any discrimination on the basis of sex, stating that all citizens are equal.

In 1997 was created a Minister dedicated to women and gender issues (currently the Ministry of Women, National Solidarity, Family and Humanitaria n Action). Burkina Faso adopted a National Gender Policy in 2009, which specific objectives are:

- Promote equal rights and equal opportunities in terms of access to and control over basic social services.
- Promote participatory economic development, access, and distribution of more equitable resources and incomes.
- Develop equal participation of men and women in decision-making spheres at all levels.
- Promote the institutionalization of gender in all areas.
- Promote a dynamic partnership for gender and development.
- Develop information and awareness-raising mechanisms aimed at all actors for a change in behavior and mentality in favor of equity and equality in relations between men and women.

In terms of laws and regulations that affect women’s economic opportunity in Burkina Faso, the country got a score of 82.5 out of 100 in the Women Business and the Law’s 2022 index. This score is higher than the regional average observed across Sub-Saharan Africa (71.5).

However, when it comes to restrictions on freedom of mobility, men have had the preference to choose where the family will reside (Code des Personnes et de la Famille , Art. 29 4). In addition, the current legislation affects women's employment and income by not having laws that enforce equal pay for work of equal value and paid parental leave. The existence of a law that prohibits women from working in an industrial job in the same way as men (Code des Personnes et de la Famille, Arts. 246 and 393) also limits women's opportunities.

## Political participation

Despite the quotas of 30% of women for legislative and local elections, implemented in 2009 to promote women’s representation in the political sphere, women's representation in the Assemblée Nationale is still low, with women holding only 17% of the seats. The original Law on Quotas from 2009 established financial sanctions (losing 50% of public funding) for non-compliance with the 30% quota, but these were removed in 2020. However, if a party reaches or exceeds the 30 percent quota, it receives additional funding as prescribed by the regulations related to public funding of political parties (Law on Quotas, Article 5 & 6).

Burkina Faso has one of the lowest scores in the Global Gender Gap's Political Empowerment subindex, just before Nigeria, and it ranks 138 out of 146 globally.

## Social and cultural context

Discriminatory practices towards women in Burkina Faso still prevail, due to the variations in social relationships between men and women in the different ethnic groups, as each one has its customs and culture, as well as the social and economic conditions of women in urban and rural areas, and women are generally regarded as having a lower status than men.

# Family and Penal Codes favoring the customary law, which perpetuates practices that discriminate against women

Despite the Burkinabe criminal and civil law establishing that forced marriage is illegal, early marriage is still prevalent in Burkina Faso. Half of the women aged 20–24 (51.6%) were married or in a union before age 18. This is in part because many families force their daughters into marriage due to poverty. Dowry motivates families to arrange marriages for their daughters as soon as they reach puberty.

Polygamy is legal under the Family Code. A large part of the population is married under a polygamous regime (i.e., 38% of unions). Levirate is forbidden under the Criminal Code (Art. 376). However, under customary law, widows are obliged to marry a brother of their deceased husband.

Regarding inheritance, widows and girls have equal legal rights to inherit property under articles 742–744 of the Family Code. However, the customary law grants no right of inheritance to widows or minor children, so they cannot inherit land either. Although civil law does not discriminate against women, women's access to land, particularly concerning rural land, has always been challenging. Only 32% of married women have access to land, and in most cases, they share it with others. Further, only 8.2% of women own land alone.

The poor access of women to land is a recurring problem due to customary laws, in which the land is generally the family's property and only reserves rights of use to the woman, and the fact that inheritance is the primary means of accessing land.

In Burkina Faso, the percentage of women who have experienced intimate partner violence (11.5) is less than the world average of 27%. However, gender-based violence (GBV) continues to manifest in many ways, such as female genital mutilation (FGM), which is still common in Burkina Faso, despite its ban in 1996. 76% of Burkinabe women have had the procedure done.

The country is one of the few in the region to have prosecuted perpetrators under legislation banning FGM (Penal Code Art. 513-7, 513-8, 513-9).

## Health

While legislation is in place that grants the right to women’s reproductive health, more than half of the Burkinabe women (54%) are excluded from the decision-making process relating to their own health.

The national family planning policy, adopted in 2004, guarantees access to contraception to all women. However, women face challenges accessing contraception mainly due to the disapproval of husbands and problems with access, information, or cost.

There is a higher incidence of HIV/AIDS among women than men because they are still highly exposed to infection due to practices such as early or forced marriage.

## Gender and energy in Burkina Faso  

Burkina Faso's energy access rate is 19%, one of the lowest in the West African sub-region.  
The expansion of off-grid electrification faces critical barriers, including inadequate policy and regulatory frameworks, lack of access to finance, lack of local technical capacity, and high cost of new connections, among others.  
These barriers may make it more difficult for women to access off-grid electrification due to gender gaps in education, access to finance, land tenure, and cultural norms that prevail in the country.  

The Africa Minigrids Program supported by UNDP and financed by the GEF, seeks to demonstrate productive uses of electricity to reduce costs, increase the viability and enable mini-grid development at scale.  
The primary targets and actors of the pilot projects are small-scale farmers (women and youth, mostly) who will benefit directly from the electricity supplied by the solar mini-grids.  
The project started in 2022 and it is planned to end in 2026.  

Among other initiatives, the GCF-funded Inclusive Green Financing Initiative (IGREENFIN I): Greening Agricultural Banks & the Financial Sector to Foster Climate Resilient, Low Emission Smallholder Agriculture in the Great Green Wall (GGW) countries program is implemented by the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) and local organizations from the participant countries, including Burkina Faso.  

The program seeks to provide concessional loans to foster the best adaptation and mitigation practices for green businesses within the selected agricultural value chains in each target country.  
Operated by local, national agricultural banks (LNABs) – the Agricultural Bank of Burkina Faso – the facility will offer special lines of financing for green business projects prepared by farmer’s organizations (FOs), women and youth organizations, cooperatives, and MSMEs (including agribusiness dealers and solar operators).  

Besides providing loans, the program includes a Technical Assistance Facility (TAF) aimed at addressing capacity, knowledge, and policy gaps hindering the uptake of green business projects in the selected countries.  
It will target LNABs, central banks, and clients (FOs, MSMEs, and cooperatives), with a special emphasis on women and youth.

# Guinea-Bissau

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Guinea-Bissau is a coastal country in West Africa. It has a population of 2.1 million people, with a young and adolescent population of 32% between the age group of 10 to the age of 24 years. The fertility rate in the country stands at 4.2.

The Gender Development Index in Guinea-Bissau is 0.867, and the country is placed in 'Group 5', countries with low equality in HDI achievements between women and men, with a high absolute deviation from gender parity of more than 10%. The country particularly has a wide gender gap in mean years of schooling, with 2.4 years among girls and 4.9 years among men.

Guinea-Bissau has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.627 and is ranked 159; the index measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.

| Mean years of schooling | 2.4 | UNDP (2022) |
| Estimated gross income per capita | 1561 (PPP $) | UNDP (2022) |

### Legal status of women

Internationally and regionally, Guinea-Bissau has made several commitments to equal rights for women. The country ratified the Optional Protocol to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) in 1981, as well as the Protocol to the Charter of Human Rights and Peoples on the Rights of Women in Africa.

Further, the country's "Social Protection Framework Law", Law 4/2007, defines the conditions for the protection of women workers in the formal market, including maternity leave provisions. However, it does not contain specific protection regulations for the informal labor market.

# Legislation and Social Context

Legislation exists in the country to protect women from violence, however, the implementation of these has been weak with pervasive violence against women, as is examined in the next section. The Law on Domestic Violence (Law Nbr. 6/2014 of 4 February) aims to combat cases of physical, psychological, property, and sexual violence. The country also has a Law to combat Female Genital Mutilation (Law Nbr. 14/2011 of 06 June) which aims to prevent and combat FGM, the practice is still prevalent, as expanded in the next section.

While the country's Land Law guarantees the right to private use of land without discrimination, the Constitution does not have any specific provisions to protect women's right to use land, and their land tenure, as a result, is often insecure. Men continue to control and manage agricultural and household land, while women live under the looming threat of having their land taken away.

As per the Women, Business and Law index, the country has a score of 42.5 out of 100, with the poorest performance in the workplace, pay, and entrepreneurship indicators. Under Lei No. 2 of 1986, Art. 155(4) of the country's legislation, no provisions were found to grant women equal rights to an industrial job, same as men, or equal pay. Under the country's Civil Code, Art. 1680, women do not have the same right to register a business or open a bank account. The country also does not have any legislation, criminal penalties, or civil remedies for sexual harassment in employment, nor does it have maternity or paternity benefits in place for working women.

The country had developed a National Policy for Gender Equality and Equity, aimed at addressing gender inequalities and combating discriminatory practices, however, this policy has not been adopted into implementation yet. With technical support from UNDP, the country's National People's Assembly is currently in the process of revising the constitution with the integration of a gender lens.

## Social and cultural context

Guinea-Bissau is ethnically diverse with numerous sub-groups, with all these having a patriarchal social structure (except the Bijagó group of the Bijagos Archipelago) and customary law aligned with traditional gender roles. Social life among these ethnic groups is characteristically male-dominated, under the culture of matchundaadi. The Matchundaadi culture is based on hierarchical control that men are guaranteed over women, placing them in decision-making roles, and institutionalizing exalted men above the law.

Polygamy is a common form of marriage, with 39.2% of women in the age group 15-49 being in polygamous unions, with men exerting their dominance over multiple women under such unions, where men must prove their masculinity by producing more children. Gender-based violence, including Female Genital Mutilation or "Fanadu di mulher" is a critical aspect of the matchundaadi culture, and is considered

## Political participation

Guinea-Bissau has experienced political instability for decades. The political system is semi -presidential. 
The country took affirmative act ion in 2018, by introducing legislation demonstrating its commitment to 
uplifting women’s position in political decision-making. The country’s Parity Law establishes that there must 
be a minimum of 36% of women in party lists for elective positions in the National Parliament and in local 
municipal elections.528 The law also underlines sanctions in case of non -compliance. In 2019, only 8 of 21

# Guinea-Bissau’s economy

Guinea-Bissau’s economy is predominantly based around agriculture, with cashew farming being the primary source of income and employment for most of the country’s poor.

As per the country’s Integrated Regional Survey on Employment and Informal Sector (2019), 96% of women in Guinea-Bissau work in the informal sector. Women in Guinea-Bissau are relegated to informal work and they compose the vast majority of self-employed people at home; street vendors and domestic workers.

As per the ERI-ESI survey, 51.9% of women are in vulnerable employment as part-time, family workers, or self-employed, compared with 33.4% of men. On the other hand, women are almost nonexistent in high productivity informal non-agrarian sector work such as civil construction work, transport activities, larger commercial establishments, and far distance and regional trade.

‘Idleness’ among disillusioned youth is also higher among women, with 31.8% of women in the age group 15-24 not in the education system or in employment, compared to 18.8% of men. Further, only 9.7% of women have formal remunerated work in the agriculture sector, while 27.5% of men have salaried work in the formal agriculture sector.

## Gender and energy in Guinea-Bissau

Currently, access to electricity among Guinea-Bissau’s population is low. As per the MICS (2020), 18% of households have electricity access through a connection to a public grid and 17.7% of households have electricity through a non-public network connection.

Due to limited access to modern energy sources, women have to spend a lot of time collecting wood and exposing themselves to harmful biomass fumes. Notably, women are engaged in various biomass-intensive processing industries including charcoal production, fish smoking, and street-food vending, and hence are important agents of change in the shift towards cleaner energy use in the country.

The country has released a National Action Plan for Gender Mainstreaming in Access to Energy and The Guinea-Bissau Ministry of Natural Resources and Energy developed a National Action Plan (NAP), with a specific focus on mainstreaming gender into national development plans. The NAP aims to create an institutional framework for gender mainstreaming in access to energy followed by an intervention framework. The plan highlights the need to train women and young people and raise awareness around gender mainstreaming in energy, to enable a diverse and inclusive labor market in the energy sector. The NAP also aims to periodically measure the progress of its implementation and assess effectiveness.

UNDP is currently implementing a project aimed at promoting better access to modern energy services through sustainable mini-grids and low-carbon bioenergy technologies among Guinea-Bissau’s forest-dependent communities. As part of the project learnings, it was found that there is tremendous scope for clean off-grid renewable energy to create entrepreneurship opportunities for women, especially in the food processing and fishing sector (which is the second largest subsistence activity in Guinea-Bissau) and as

## Niger

### Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Niger is a West African country with vast desert terrain. The country has a population of 26.1 million people, with a young and adolescent population of 33% between the age group of 10 to the age of 24 years.

The country has one of the world’s highest fertility rates of 6.6 per woman.

The Gender Development Index in Niger is 0.835, and the country is placed in ‘Group 5’ countries with low equality in HDI achievements between women and men, with a high absolute deviation from gender parity of more than 10%. The country has a high gender gap in mean years of schooling, with 1.7 years among girls and 2.8 years among boys, and in gross national income per capita.

Niger has a Gender Inequality Index of 0.611 and is ranked 153; the index measures disparities between men and women in economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment.

## Gender Development Index

The Constitution of Niger, which was adopted in 2010, upholds equal rights for men and women and guarantees the same rights and duties for women and men in Articles 8 and 10. Further, Article 22 explicitly mandates the State to take measures to combat gender violence and take measures to ensure the full participation of women in public institutions, and in national development, through the country’s policies.

The country’s quota law defines minimum representation percentages for women in public affairs and the commercial and labor codes prohibit gender-based discrimination in hiring, work allocation, wages, and promotion practices.

However, Niger is one of the few countries in the region which has not yet ratified the African Charter on Human and Peoples’ Rights on the Rights of Women. The country ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) in 1999; however, Niger has expressed reservations about its implementation.

Further, the country’s legal and regulatory system accords supremacy to customary law over statutory law. For instance, the Civil Code denotes men as heads of the household with marital authority over wives, the Family Code recognizes customary systems around matrimony and property, and the Rural Code fails to provide promote and protect women’s ownership rights over land.

The country has articulated its gender commitment in some key country policies and strategy documents including the Sustainable Development and Inclusive Growth Strategy (SDDCI, Niger 2035), the National Gender Policy, the National Women’s Economic Empowerment Strategy, National GBV Prevention and Response Strategy, and the Gender and Islam Strategy.

The country’s Gender Policy has adopted 4 key lenses to reach its equality goals including:

1. Improvement of the socio-cultural environment in relation to demography, peace, and security;
2. Strengthening of the institutional and legal framework to fight against gender-based violence and to enable equitable participation of men and women in the management of power;
3. Economic empowerment and inclusive growth in relation to sustainable management of the environment, disaster risk management, migration, and humanitarian emergencies;
4. Strengthening of institutional mechanisms and organizational frameworks for coordination, monitoring-evaluation, and partnership.

The country also has a National Observatory for Gender Promotion under the

## Social and cultural context

The prevalence of Gender Based Violence in Niger is at 28.4%. 563 Women displaced in some regions of the country, due to extremist Islamic groups' armed incursions face a high threat of violence in resettlement or refugee camps. Niger revised the Penal Code in 2003 to make provisions for certain forms of violence against women, particularly sexual harassment, female genital mutilation, slavery, pimping, incitement to debauchery, and rape however the country is yet to take holistic, adequate, and concrete steps to prevent, punish and manage cases of gender-based violence (GBV) and child marriage.564 Niger also has the world's highest incidence of child marriage (76%), with close to 4.8 million child brides of which 2 million were married before the age of 15. To combat this, In 2021, the government issued a decree instituting communal and village committees as formal child protection committees.565 This further impacts girls’ ability to remain in school and complete their education. Polygamy is widely practiced in the country, and high fertility is often a tool for women to command the respect of their husbands.566

At the decentralized level, there are 17 women councilors out of a total of 104 councilors in City Councils however there are no female City Council Chairperson. ## Health

The high fertility rate in Niger has adverse impacts on women’s health and well-being. Niger has a maternal mortality rate of 509 as per the Gender Inequality Index.

The country also has very poor coverage of ANC and PNC checkups, with only 27.8% of women having 4 or more ANC visits in 2016–18 and 39.4% of women receiving post-natal care. In 2017 institutional births were also limited (38.2%) and only 36.8% of births were attended by skilled health professionals.

Anemia among women is also highly prevalent with 46% of women being anemic.

The uptake of contraception in Niger is low due to religious beliefs under Islamic law and women’s fears about seeking reproductive health services from male healthcare workers.

Further, 18% of married women have an unmet need for family planning and only 50% of their demand is met with modern contraceptive methods.

## Education

As per the World Bank data, Niger has a very low female literacy rate of 29%.

Further, girls in rural areas have low enrollment rates in schools. In 2016–17, the Gross Enrollment Rate for girls in primary school was 72% and only 59% of girls completed schooling in rural areas. This further declined at the secondary schooling and higher education level with only 31% and 2% of girls enrolling respectively.

Women face access constraints due to inadequate financial resources available for their education, as well as early marriage.

Within schools, poor infrastructure, gender-based violence, and sanitation further exacerbate these constraints for girls.

To promote equality and gender parity in education, the country has developed a National Strategy for Accelerating Girls’ and Women’s Education and Training (SNAEFFF).

Under this policy, through presidential decrees, the government in Niger has been working towards setting up literacy and ‘second chance’ education programs for girls who have dropped out of schools by providing assistance to parents, food rations, scholarships, and deliberation of a decentralization policy for secondary education so that community schools can be set up and attended by girls and women.

Considering early marriage and early pregnancy often leads to girls dropping out of schools, integrating women back into the schooling system is a key challenge being faced, with these girls often ostracised by their communities for falling pregnant.

In 2019, the government of Niger adopted an order directing schools to allow married and/or pregnant girls to return back to schools, continue their studies, and return after they have delivered/have given birth.

For promoting vocational and technical education, the country has developed a Gender Strategy in Vocational and Technical Education and established 103 community-level training centers and 11

# Women’s economic participation and entrepreneurship

Female labor force participation rate (LFPR) in Niger stands at 61.73%, and the country has a gender disparity of 22.5 percentage points between men and women, with LFPR of 84.27% among men.

The high fertility rate, constricting societal norms, and women’s roles in managing their households are some reasons they are unable to participate effectively in the labor force. As per the country’s Employment survey from 2016, women work an average of 28 hours a week compared to men who work 43 hours.

Agriculture is the dominant sector, and most women work as informal agricultural workers. World Bank estimates that about 91% of women are in vulnerable employment as of 2017.

However, there is a wide disparity in agricultural productivity of plots managed by women compared to men, with 19% less production per hectare, which is due to land ownership issues, limited access to and issues in supervising hired farm labour, and the quantity and quality of fertilizer used.

In formal employment, public officers have a lower representation of women civil servants, especially as senior staff, while at the junior level, there are 73% of women in the Ministry of Public Service.

A high proportion of women youth (77.1%) are also ‘idle’ and not in education, employment, or training.

Within wage employment, there is an estimated 29% gender gap in earnings between men and women in similar roles, due to disparity in educational attainment and concentration of women in low-productivity sectors.

# Entrepreneurship in the informal sector

Entrepreneurship in the informal sector is a crucial phenomenon among women in Niger, and 23% of women aged 16-64 own an enterprise in the country. However, there is a vast gender gap of 61% in profits accrued by women-owned businesses versus men-owned.

This is due to factors such as the limited availability of labor to help run the enterprise, literacy, and numeracy gaps, and informality of enterprise registration.

Textile manufacturing followed by wholesale retail trade and motor vehicle repairs are two main sectors with the high disparity in profits, 82% and 68% respectively.

# Gender and energy in Niger

Currently, there are only 16.6% of households in Niger with access to electricity. The main source of fuel in the country is wood. This causes a lot of time burden on women who are primarily the ones responsible for firewood collection.

Butane gas is another alternative source of energy in the country, however, the use is expensive for poorer Nigeriens.

In urban 38.2% of women-headed households have access to grid electricity.

# Chad

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Chad is a Central African country, with a population of around 17 million people – 49.8% of which are women – an annual population growth of 3.2%, with 48% of the total population under the age of 15.

The Gender Development Index for Chad is 0.77. According to the GDI scoring, Chad belongs to the group of countries with the lowest human development (countries with low equality in HDI achievements between women and men and absolute deviation from gender parity of more than 10 percent). On the Gender Inequality Index (GII) which measures disparities between men and women in the areas of economic opportunities, reproductive health, and empowerment, the country is ranked among the highest in the world (165).

Chad also scores low on human development, with women being in worse conditions compared to men (0.339 HDI for women versus men 0.441), and Chad ranks 190th out of 191 countries on the Human Development Index (2019).

### Legal status of women

The Republic of Chad is a member of the United Nations and the African Union. It has ratified a number of UN Human Rights Conventions and thus has made binding international commitments to adhere to the standards laid down in these universal human rights documents. All inhabitants of Chad may turn to the UN Human Rights Committee through procedure 1503, to the Special Rapporteurs for violations of specific human rights or to ECOSOC for women's rights violations.

590 Women in Chad also have full voting rights. However, despite improvements over the years in the legal framework, there are still important challenges in reflecting women’s legal rights in practice. Chad has a strong constitutional framework for equality which

# 92

includes the State’s obligation to eliminate all forms of discrimination against women, and has made some progress towards achieving women’s right to equality. For example, Chad has taken the necessary legislative measures to ban female genital mutilation and child marriage. Unfortunately, enforcement of these laws is inadequate. Women and girls continue to face the prospect of lives of gender-based violence.

There are signs of positive steps. In 2022, the Ministry of National Education and Civic Promotion (MENPC) of the Republic of Chad, a member of the Gender at the Center Initiative (GCI), sought international support to provide technical capacity-building towards integration of gender equality issues into education policies. In addition, the Government of Chad is preparing its Partnership Pact, a sector-wide document that will articulate a priority gender-sensitive reform that a handful of donors and non-governmental organizations will support over the next five years.

## Political participation

In Chad, women represent more than 50% of the total population and are potentially a decisive electorate. A law on representative parity exists, though there is currently less than a 50% representation of women in seats of national and local governments. As in other countries in the continent, gender norms impact women’s political participation, and women’s groups within the country have called on the government to guarantee parity in nominative and elective functions in the Republic of Chad and at regional level. Despite the challenges, 32.3% of seats in Chadian Parliament are held by women, which is higher than the sub-Saharan Africa average of 25.7%.

## Social and cultural context

The social and cultural context of women is shaped by factors such as religion, traditional beliefs, and societal norms. Despite legal protections, women in Chad often face discrimination in various domains, including education, employment, and inheritance rights. Harmful practices like child marriage and female genital mutilation/cutting (FGM/C) persist, particularly in rural areas, despite being prohibited by law. Religion, predominantly Islam, significantly influences the social and cultural context of women's rights in Chad. Conservative interpretations and cultural traditions may curtail women's autonomy in areas such as marriage, divorce, and inheritance. In addition, Chad is a country where land ownership is often governed by customary laws and practices that may vary among different ethnic groups and regions, and women's access to and ownership of land can be influenced by social, cultural, and legal factors.

Traditional gender roles and norms also play a significant role, with women often expected to conform to traditional roles as wives, mothers, and caregivers, constraining their opportunities for education and employment. Women in Chad have made progress in advocating for their rights, with active women's rights organizations and civil society groups working towards gender equality and women's empowerment.

# Chadian Government and Gender Equality

Chadian government has also taken steps to promote gender equality through policy reforms and legal protections, although challenges persist in implementation and enforcement.

## Health

A high maternal mortality rate is a major health issue in Chad. 1,140 women die per 100,000 live births, which is more than double the sub-Saharan African average, although it has improved from 1,420 in 2000.

The high maternal mortality rate is caused by several factors such as lack of access to medical treatment, early marriage, and high birth rates. Chad also has a high adolescent fertility rate of 138.3 births per 1,000 women aged 15-19. This is higher than the sub-Saharan average of 100.9.

Throughout the country, the average age at first birth is 18.1 years. Furthermore, 24% of women aged 20-24 were married before their fifteenth birthday, and 60% of women aged 20-24 were married before their eighteenth birthday, compared with 8% of men in the same age group.

Chadian women suffer from poorer health compared to men, with only 31% of pregnant women receiving adequate prenatal healthcare, and 34% of women being subjected to female genital mutilation.

Moreover, women of reproductive age (15-49 years) often face barriers with respect to their sexual and reproductive health and rights: despite progress, in 2019, only 17.5% of women had their need for family planning satisfied with modern methods, and 30% of married women had an unmet need for contraception.

Furthermore, severe legal restrictions on abortion lead women to have abortions performed clandestinely, usually in unhealthy conditions.

## Education

The education situation for girls in Chad faces numerous challenges, including limited access to quality education, low enrollment rates, high dropout rates, early marriage and pregnancy, gender-based violence, cultural and social norms, and poverty. These challenges often result in lower levels of educational attainment for girls compared to boys, which can have long-term consequences for their empowerment, well-being, and economic opportunities.

The average Chadian woman will benefit from 1.5 years of formal schooling compared to 3.7 years for men. Furthermore, only 8% of women have attended secondary school compared to 24% of men. Adult literacy in Chad is also lower among women than among men, with 18.2% of women being literate and 35.4% of men being literate. The gap in adult literacy between men and women is 17.2, which is larger than the gap of the sub-Saharan Africa aggregate of 13.6.

## Women's economic participation and entrepreneurship

The labor force participation rate among females is 48.4% and among males is 70.8%, giving Chad a higher labor force participation gap than the rest of sub-Saharan Africa. Factors such as early marriage, early pregnancy, cultural norms that prioritize male education, and lack of access to quality education and vocational training may hinder women's ability to enter the workforce. Furthermore, social norms and traditional gender roles in Chad often dictate that women primarily engage in household and caregiving responsibilities, while men are expected to be the breadwinners. This can limit women's ability to participate in economic activities outside of their homes, and women may face discrimination, biases, and stereotypes in the workplace or as entrepreneurs.

In Chad, 75% of employed people work in agriculture, which includes subsistence farming, fishing, and animal husbandry. Women play a critical role in food production and contribute to the rural economy by cultivating crops, raising livestock, and engaging in other agricultural activities to sustain their households and communities. They also participate in the informal sector, which includes jobs in informal markets, petty trade, and small-scale enterprises. Despite their important contribution, 98.8% of female workers are in vulnerable employment, compared to 86.6% of men, both of which are higher than the sub-Saharan African average. These are workers who are least likely to have formal work arrangements, social protection, and safety nets to guard against economic shocks; thus they are more likely to fall into poverty, posing challenges to women's economic well-being and empowerment. As such, 41% of the employed female population lives below the poverty line compared to 39% of men.

There are also gender gaps in the formal sector. It is estimated that only 1% of female workers are salaried workers, compared to 13% of male workers, and women constitute less than 18% of professional and technical workers. There is also an entrepreneurship gap, with only 9% of firms having majority female ownership and only 12% of firms with female top managers. Furthermore, female entrepreneurs may face challenges such as limited access to finance, markets, and business development services due to gender-based discrimination.

The principle of non-discrimination on the ground of sex, age or nationality prevails within Chad's Labour Code (1996) with regards to employment, notably recruitment, hiring, terms and conditions, promotions, training, assignments and termination. The Labour Code also guarantees paid maternity leave of 14 weeks, during which mothers are entitled to the totality of their wages, with a further 6 months of unpaid parental leave. However, women are mostly involved in the informal sector and precarious jobs and thus remain excluded from the legal protection of the formal sector. Furthermore, even women within the formal sector face gender bias. The Labour Code prohibits women from entering certain professions which could

# 95

Women's economic empowerment is also threatened through challenges in accessing land, financial services, credit, and agricultural extension services, which can limit their productivity and income generation potential. Chad’s Civil Code stipulates that a woman can open a bank account at a formal institution for the funds given by her husband. However, the husband must be notified and must authorize withdrawals from the account.

Women’s access to credit is also limited due to discrimination and stereotypes, their lack of collateral and lack of information regarding credit. Additionally, it is reported that in some parts of Chad, women must seek the approval of their husband if they wish to take a bank loan to open a business. Even though Law on the Limitation of Land Rights guarantees the right to everyone to own, use, make decisions and use as collateral, customary law and practices often prioritize male ownership and inheritance of land. Most women don’t own land and usually work on the land of their father or husband, and they generate income from those lands, it is usually given back to their father or husband.

## Gender and energy in Chad

Only 11% of the population have access to electricity in Chad. Women in Chad, particularly those in rural areas, often face additional challenges in accessing energy services due to factors such as poverty, social norms, lack of infrastructure, and limited access to financial resources. These challenges can disproportionately impact women's ability to access and benefit from modern energy services, such as electricity for lighting, cooking, and productive uses. Sex-disaggregated data on access to energy as well as women’s participation in the sector is absent.

In 2022, the World Bank approved the $295M Chad Energy Access Scale Up Project (PAAET) which aims to increase access to electricity and clean cooking solutions via expansion of the main power grid and mini-grids, standalone solar systems, deployment of improved stoves, and natural resource management.

The project will include targeted interventions to improve access to electricity for female-headed households, including in refugee camps, and female-led enterprises, promote women entrepreneurship, and build awareness relevant to women and girls around energy services.

# Somalia

## Overview of gender equality and gender gaps in the country

Somalia is an East African country, with a population of around 17 million people – 49.9% of which are women – an annual population growth of 3.1%, with 47% of the total population under the age of 15.

There is a scarcity of socioeconomic data in Somalia, including on gender, because of years of political instability and civil war. This has led to lack of institutions and systems needed to collect, analyze, and disseminate reliable data, limited capacity of the Government to undertake comprehensive data collection, an inability for researchers and organizations to access certain regions for data collection, and significant displacement and migration of Somali people which which complicates data collection and tracking of social and economic indicators. As such, Somalia does not have a Human Development Index, Gender Inequality Index nor a Gender Development Index.

It’s likely that Somalia would belong to the group with the lowest human development group for all three indices, and that gender inequality and deviation from gender parity is high.

### Legal status of women

Women in Somalia face difficulties in accessing justice due to cultural restrictions, differing interpretations of judicial regimes, and the absence of women within the judicial system. As of 2021, there are only two female judges out of a total of 295 and 14 female prosecutors out of a total of 65, highlighting the need for gender parity in the judicial system.

# Somalia's Ministry of Women and Human Rights Development

Somalia has a Ministry of Women and Human Rights Development, mandated to advance the promotion and protection of gender equality and human rights, including the rights of women, children and other vulnerable groups. Increased awareness of the rights of women and girls has been driven by this ministry alongside international rights groups.

The Federal Government of Somalia launched the Somali National Action Plan (NAP) in September 2022. Its purpose is to lay out and guide implementation of the Somali Women's Charter and UN Security Council Resolution 1325 (UNSCR 1325). The inclusion and participation of Somali women in peacebuilding and decision-making at all levels is the primary goal of the NAP, where these ideals are recognized as a crucial foundation for effective governance, long-term peace, and development.

Despite progress in the formal recognition of women’s legal status, the United Nations has reported that cultural factors and customary law can be more accepting of inequities or victimization of women and girls. For example, while no laws limit the participation of women or members of historically marginalized groups in the political process (and they do participate), cultural factors have limited their participation.

There is some hope the National Action Plan will be able to have a broad impact on women’s lived experience in Somalia, including their legal standing. Conflict-related sexual and gender-based violence, access to justice, inclusion in constitutional review processes, state building and peacebuilding, national reconciliation and transitional justice mechanisms, prevention of violent extremism, as well as climate change, humanitarian emergencies, and COVID-19, are all identified as relevant themes/action areas for women’s wellbeing and equity, and are to be addressed by the national action plan.

## Political participation

In Somalia, women account for 50.15% of the population. In 2016, Somalia reached a milestone with nearly a quarter of parliamentary seats occupied by women. In January 2021, a quota was announced for a third of parliamentary seats to be reserved for female lawmakers. There has since been limited progress in female representation in the national legislative bodies - the 329 seats in Somalia's lower and upper houses of parliament - despite the quota.

After the parliamentary elections in 2021-2022, women’s representation fell to 20% in the House of the People, which is lower than the 2016 figure of 24%. There were some gains made with 26% seats for women in the Upper House in 11th Parliament, in comparison to 24% in 10th Parliament. Given the clan system at the foundation of Somalia’s politics, which is male-dominated, Somali women largely remain excluded from key political and decision-making processes.

## Social and cultural context

Traditional customs, religious doctrines and societal expectations are among the various factors that contribute to the social and cultural environment that Somali women experience. Women and girls across the country face significant challenges, as gender inequality is pervasive. Somalia ranks as the fourth-lowest country in the world for gender equality, with high rates of maternal and infant mortality, as well as

# 98

## Prevalence of Early Marriage

Stereotypes about women in the workplace, clan-based identity politics that center men, and deep-rooted gender-based typecasts are among the factors behind gender inequity and injustice. For example, barriers to education systematically exclude women from attaining skills, which has implications for female participation in the economy. Certain social norms negatively impact women: for example, gender-based violence through female genital mutilation, which can have serious physiological and psychological impacts throughout a woman’s life, and the expectation to marry early.

Despite the introduction of the Sexual Offences Bill (SOB) in 2018, which would provide greater protection for victims of sexual violence and help end the culture of impunity relating to GBV, women in Somalia remain under-represented within and excluded from key political and decision-making processes. Efforts from government bodies such as the Ministry of Women and Human Rights Development display some awareness of the inequities and challenges existing in the sociocultural context. However, amid ongoing fragility in terms of the security situation within Somalia, with numerous attacks carried out by militant groups in both urban and rural areas, and with GBV being a feature of this conflict, there are even greater challenges for the government to adequately provide for the security, protection, social, environmental, and economic needs of the female population.

## Health

A high maternal mortality rate is a major health issue in Somalia. 829 women die per 100,000 live births, which is higher than the sub-Saharan African average. The high maternal mortality rate is caused by several factors such as limited access to health care, poor healthcare infrastructure, socio-cultural factors like early childbearing, and ongoing conflict.

Somali women suffer from worse health outcomes than men and face significant barriers to reproductive health, with only 24% of pregnant women receiving adequate prenatal healthcare, and 37% of married women with unmet need for contraception. Furthermore, 99% of women in girls and Somalia have undergone female genital mutilation, which can lead to significant health issues throughout life, including complications in childbirth which can lead to maternal mortality. What’s more, abortion in Somalia has been criminalized, except to save the life of the mother, and a cesarean is performed only if the husband and/or the mother-in-law give permission. Women are more malnourished than men and are less food secure.

## Education

The education situation for girls in Somalia is characterized by various challenges and disparities, such as long distances to school, safety concerns, and lack of female teachers. Although there have been some improvements in recent years, girls' education in the country continues to face obstacles. Socio-cultural norms in Somalia often favor boys' education over girls', leading to gender disparities in access to education.

# Gender and energy in Somalia

Energy access in Somalia is limited, particularly in rural areas, and women are disproportionately affected by the lack of reliable and affordable energy sources. National energy access in Somalia is 18% and less than 5% in rural areas.651 The lack of electricity affects women in various ways, such as limiting their access to information, communication, and education, as well as constraining their economic opportunities. Sex-disaggregated data on access to energy as well as women's participation in the sector is absent.

Several programs and initiatives, led by international organizations, non-governmental organizations, and the Somali government, aim to improve women's access to energy in Somalia. These initiatives focus on promoting renewable energy technologies, building capacity, and fostering gender equality in the energy sector. For instance, the Sustainable Charcoal Reduction and Alternative Livelihoods (SCREAL) project aims to reduce dependency on charcoal by promoting sustainable energy alternatives, including fuel-efficient stoves, and creating alternative livelihood opportunities, particularly for women and youth.652 The Africa Minigrids Program (AMP) aims to bring new development opportunities to rural communities and to increase access to electricity for 67K people, half of whom will be women.653

651 IEA WEO, 2020

# Conclusions and Recommendation for the Gender Action Plan

This assessment provides an overview of the status of gender equality in the sixteen H2R countries. The H2R initiative has the opportunity to address some of the underlying causes of gender inequality in the countries by enhancing access for women to clean energy technologies and job opportunities in the sector.

In particular, the following objectives are relevant:

## H2R’s Gender Objectives:
1. Elevate women in the OGS sector as customers, employees and entrepreneurs
2. Do no harm from a gender perspective
3. Provide poorest and most vulnerable women and Women Headed Households with clean energy
4. Invest in women-led businesses and businesses with gender-forward business practices
5. Positively impact organizational culture for women employees through the influencing portfolio companies to adopt and/or expand gender business practices

The H2R Gender Assessment informs the development of the Gender Action Plan through the identification of key barriers and entry points for the H2R initiative. In particular, the country-level assessments identified barriers for engaging women as employees and leaders in these contexts, with prevalent negative gender norms, incomplete education attainment, and limited access and control of assets influencing women’s participation in the economy and in productive activities.

Elevating women as customers, employees, and business leaders in the OGS will require a comprehensive approach both within the investment process and in the allocation of capital.

## H2R’s Gender Action Plan will achieve its objectives through 4 components:

1. **Apply a gender lens across its investment processes** to mitigate gender biases and identify hidden opportunities. This involves integrating gender considerations at each step of the decision-making process to produce a portfolio of investments positioned to meet H2R’s gender objectives.

2. **Offer post-investment gender-focused TA to investees** to incentivize companies to adopt, expand, and/or implement gender-forward business practices. Post-investment TA support will focus on the development and implementation of company Gender Action Plans (GAP) and on providing training to level set any gaps in gender awareness at the investee level.

3. **Work to build opportunities for women in the broader ecosystem** through a dedicated training and employment facility to drive employment access and readiness in OGS markets.

4. **Catalyze OGS ecosystems in H2R markets by crowding in a variety of stakeholders and sharing lessons.** H2R will invest in insights, data aggregation, and OGS ecosystem development to build a robust community of actors working together towards common goals.

These components are further detailed in the GAP.

# H2R Gender Action Plan

## Impact Statement
If the appropriate investment capital and TA is provided to OGS companies, then they will be able to deliver clean energy access to women customers and women-headed households and to increase diversity in their workforce, increasing companies' growth opportunities and contributing to women economic empowerment because they will be adequately incentivized and capacitated to operate in 'Hardest-to-Reach' (H2R) contexts.

## Outcome statement:
- Access to clean and decentralized energy to 34,047,623 women (47% of 72,441,750 total beneficiaries)
- Sustainable and inclusive growth of OGS businesses
- Reduction in 5.1 m tCO2eq to preserve and improve women's health
- Increased resilience of women and girls via improved health, safety and wellbeing
- Reduced vulnerability of women

## Output statement:
- Funding and technical assistance lead to increased capacity of OGS companies to serve low-income women customers and to hire and retain women employees, as well as creating gender inclusive workplaces.

## H2R's Gender Objectives:
1. Elevate women in the OGS sector as customers, employees and entrepreneurs
2. Do no harm from a gender perspective
3. Provide poorest and most vulnerable women and Women Headed Households with clean energy
4. Invest in women-led businesses and businesses with gender-forward business practices
5. Positively impact organizational culture for women employees through the influencing portfolio companies to adopt and/or expand gender business practices
